    *   **Complex Tasks:**  Successfully demonstrated on challenging, whole-body interaction tasks.
    *   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, and provides sufficient details about the method and experiments.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Skill Composition:** The current implementation does not seem to support composing individual skills.
    *   **Dexterous Manipulation and Object Geometries:** The system currently does not support very complex hand manipulation skills or complex object geometries. The choice of the inspire hand with only a binary open/close operation is limiting.
    *   **Reliance on Simulation:** While it shows real-world deployment, the training is still primarily simulation-based. While the vision is promising and mentioned in the paper, future work needs to focus on minimizing the sim-to-real gap.
    *   **OWLv2 perception latency:**  The reliance on using OWL-V2 during inference poses performance limitations, as it freezes the root during the task execution process.
    *   **Refinement and Filtering** :  The refinement and filtering steps of generated trajectories is manual and requires heuristic decisions. An automated strategy for generating those steps is needed for full autonomy.

*   **Potential Influence:** DreamControl has the potential to significantly influence the field of humanoid robotics, especially in task automation. By facilitating more data-efficient learning and improving sim-to-real transfer, this method could enable broader adoption of humanoid robots in real-world applications.

**Justification for Score:**

While the components of DreamControl are not individually groundbreaking, the novel combination and its application to a significant problem in humanoid robotics warrant a high score. The reduced reliance on expensive teleoperation data, the improved sim-to-real transfer, and the successful demonstration on a real robot performing complex tasks are important advancements. The limitations regarding skill composition, dexterity, and sim2real still keep it from being a truly transformative paper. Overall, the paper presents a strong and valuable contribution to the field.
This is a notable improvement in how data is used in robotics by shifting the data bottleneck from task-specific demonstrations to leveraging abundant human motion data, demonstrating promising results on a humanoid robot. This is a good step in the right direction.

### CRAFT: Coaching Reinforcement Learning Autonomously using Foundation Models for Multi-Robot Coordination Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14380v1)
- **Authors**: Seoyeon Choi, Kanghyun Ryu, Jonghoon Ock, Negar Mehr
- **Abstract**: Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) provides a powerful framework for learning coordination in multi-agent systems. However, applying MARL to robotics still remains challenging due to high-dimensional continuous joint action spaces, complex reward design, and non-stationary transitions inherent to decentralized settings. On the other hand, humans learn complex coordination through staged curricula, where long-horizon behaviors are progressively built upon simpler skills. Motivated by this, we propose CRAFT: Coaching Reinforcement learning Autonomously using Foundation models for multi-robot coordination Tasks, a framework that leverages the reasoning capabilities of foundation models to act as a "coach" for multi-robot coordination. CRAFT automatically decomposes long-horizon coordination tasks into sequences of subtasks using the planning capability of Large Language Models (LLMs). In what follows, CRAFT trains each subtask using reward functions generated by LLM, and refines them through a Vision Language Model (VLM)-guided reward-refinement loop. We evaluate CRAFT on multi-quadruped navigation and bimanual manipulation tasks, demonstrating its capability to learn complex coordination behaviors. In addition, we validate the multi-quadruped navigation policy in real hardware experiments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CRAFT, a novel framework for training multi-robot coordination tasks by leveraging the reasoning capabilities of foundation models (LLMs and VLMs).  CRAFT automatically decomposes long-horizon coordination tasks into a sequence of subtasks, generating reward functions for each subtask using an LLM.  A VLM evaluates the trained policies and provides feedback for reward refinement.  This iterative process creates a curriculum, guiding the robots to learn complex coordination behaviors.  The framework is evaluated on multi-quadruped navigation and bimanual manipulation tasks in simulation and validated with real-world hardware experiments on quadruped navigation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its holistic approach to multi-robot coordination, combining curriculum generation, reward design, and policy evaluation within a single framework that is driven by foundation models. While individual components (curriculum learning, LLM-based reward design, VLM policy evaluation) have been explored separately, CRAFT’s integrated architecture for multi-agent systems is novel. It's important to note that existing works also leverage LLMs for curriculum design and reward shaping, so the **incremental advancement** comes from the fusion and application to a challenging multi-agent robotics setting.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in multi-robot systems: the difficulty of designing effective learning frameworks for coordinated behaviors. By automating the curriculum generation and reward shaping processes, CRAFT has the potential to reduce the human effort required to train robots for complex tasks. The real-world validation, although limited to quadruped navigation, enhances the practical relevance of the work. The potential impact is on making multi-agent robotics more accessible and scalable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Integrated Framework:** The architecture that combines curriculum generation, reward design, and evaluation is well-defined and logical.
    *   **Foundation Model Utilization:** The paper effectively leverages the reasoning capabilities of LLMs and VLMs to generate meaningful subtasks, reward functions, and advice for reward refinement.
    *   **Real-World Validation:** Demonstrating the transfer of learned policies to real-world robots adds significant credibility.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Well-designed ablation studies effectively demonstrate the importance of curriculum learning and the reward refinement loop.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Real-World Validation:**  The real-world experiments are limited to only the quadruped gate task. Validation on the bimanual manipulation task in the real world would significantly strengthen the paper.
    *   **Foundation Model Dependency:** The framework's performance relies heavily on the capabilities of the LLMs and VLMs used. Future advancements in these models might improve CRAFT's performance, but current limitations in these models could also restrict its applicability. There should be deeper discussion on which particular capabilities of LLMs/VLMs are most vital for CRAFT's success, and how potential biases or limitations in the models impact the system.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Traditional MARL:**  While the paper showcases improvement over simple baselines, it lacks a comparison to more sophisticated traditional MARL algorithms that are specifically tailored for partially observable environments, perhaps including intrinsic motivation or communication. This makes it difficult to assess how much of the improvement comes from foundation models vs advanced RL techniques.
    *   **Efficiency:** The computational overhead of querying VLMs and LLMs for reward refinement is not thoroughly addressed. This might be a bottleneck for complex tasks. The training time and compute resources need to be better clarified.
    *   **Reproducibility:** There isn't enough clarity for reproducibility, specifically the precise prompting strategy (including examples and few-shot configurations) employed for the LLMs/VLMs. This is crucial for other researchers to build on this work.

*   **Potential Influence:** CRAFT has the potential to influence research in multi-robot learning by providing a blueprint for automating the training process. It can inspire further investigations into leveraging foundation models for complex robotics tasks. Future research may explore how to improve the robustness of the framework to the limitations of current LLMs/VLMs, reduce its computational cost, and expand its application to other challenging domains.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of multi-robot coordination. The integrated framework, effective use of foundation models, and real-world validation demonstrate the potential of CRAFT. While there are weaknesses, primarily the limited real-world validation and potential reliance on foundation model capabilities, the strengths outweigh the limitations. Therefore, the paper warrants a score of **7** due to its novelty, significance, and potential to advance the field, but also acknowledging areas for improvement. There's a strong argument to be made for this score, as the paper introduces a compelling approach even if not all facets are fully substantiated by conclusive results. It offers valuable directions for future work.

### Detecting Pipeline Failures through Fine-Grained Analysis of Web Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14382v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel Röder, Akhil Juneja, Roland Roller, Sven Schmeier
- **Abstract**: Web agents powered by large language models (LLMs) can autonomously perform complex, multistep tasks in dynamic web environments. However, current evaluations mostly focus on the overall success while overlooking intermediate errors. This limits insight into failure modes and hinders systematic improvement. This work analyzes existing benchmarks and highlights the lack of fine-grained diagnostic tools. To address this gap, we propose a modular evaluation framework that decomposes agent pipelines into interpretable stages for detailed error analysis. Using the SeeAct framework and the Mind2Web dataset as a case study, we show how this approach reveals actionable weaknesses missed by standard metrics - paving the way for more robust and generalizable web agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of evaluating web agents powered by large language models (LLMs). Current evaluation methods focus primarily on end-to-end task success, obscuring intermediate errors and hindering systematic improvement. To address this, the authors propose a modular evaluation framework that decomposes agent pipelines into interpretable stages (action prediction/planning, grounding, and action selection).  They apply this framework to SeeAct, a multimodal web agent, using the Mind2Web dataset as a case study.  Their approach reveals actionable weaknesses missed by standard metrics, specifically highlighting issues with context fragmentation, grounding errors, and ambiguity in HTML interfaces.  The authors enhance the SeeAct architecture, augment the Mind2Web evaluation protocol with alternative valid actions, and demonstrate the utility of their modular framework in identifying bottlenecks and guiding system improvement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a valuable perspective shift from end-to-end evaluation to modular analysis of web agent pipelines. While modularity in AI systems is not a new concept *per se*, its application to *web agent evaluation*, particularly with the level of detail presented, constitutes a significant contribution. The augmentation of the Mind2Web dataset with alternative valid actions directly addresses a key limitation of the original benchmark and increases its real-world relevance. The adapted SeeAct architecture also contributes to the methodology.
*   **Significance:** Identifying error modes in web agent pipelines is crucial for improving their robustness and generalizability. The paper provides clear evidence that standard metrics can be misleading and that a more fine-grained analysis is necessary. The identification of bottlenecks such as the "Action Prediction" stage (planning) and the "Action Selection" stage helps to focus research efforts on critical areas. Furthermore, the demonstrated trade-offs between different input modifications (Textual Grounding vs. Visual Clues) offer valuable insights for web agent design. The insights into benchmarking limitations and the call for more flexible and dynamic evaluation environments are also significant.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing evaluation methods.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The modular evaluation framework is well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The case study using SeeAct and Mind2Web provides strong empirical support for the proposed approach.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The paper identifies specific weaknesses in web agents and suggests potential solutions.
    *   **Benchmark Augmentation:** Addressing the limitation in Mind2Web with alternative paths
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is focused on a single framework (SeeAct) and a single benchmark (Mind2Web). While this provides a detailed case study, it is important to consider the generalizability of the findings to other web agents and tasks. While multiple models are evaluated on the augmented SeeAct framework, a direct benchmark comparison to related approaches could strengthen the impact of the work.
    *   **Dependency on LLMs:** The evaluation framework relies on LLMs for tasks such as element extraction and classification, which introduces its own set of potential biases and errors. Although the prompt engineering seems robust, a potential analysis on prompt stability might strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **Computational Resources**: The use of expensive models in the evaluation can be costly, so there is room for experimentation with lower cost approaches.
*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design and evaluation of future web agents. By providing a more detailed understanding of agent behavior, it can help researchers and developers to create more robust, reliable, and generalizable systems. The call for more flexible and dynamic evaluation environments can also shape the development of new benchmarks and evaluation protocols.
*   **Justification of Score:** This work provides a significant improvement in web agent evaluation through the proposed modular evaluation framework that enables fine-grained analysis. This allows for more interpretable and diagnostic evaluations, ultimately aiding in web agent improvement. Augmentation of the Mind2Web dataset also significantly improves the accuracy of testing web agents in real world flexible environments.

### Annotating Training Data for Conditional Semantic Textual Similarity Measurement using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14399v1)
- **Authors**: Gaifan Zhang, Yi Zhou, Danushka Bollegala
- **Abstract**: Semantic similarity between two sentences depends on the aspects considered between those sentences. To study this phenomenon, Deshpande et al. (2023) proposed the Conditional Semantic Textual Similarity (C-STS) task and annotated a human-rated similarity dataset containing pairs of sentences compared under two different conditions. However, Tu et al. (2024) found various annotation issues in this dataset and showed that manually re-annotating a small portion of it leads to more accurate C-STS models. Despite these pioneering efforts, the lack of large and accurately annotated C-STS datasets remains a blocker for making progress on this task as evidenced by the subpar performance of the C-STS models. To address this training data need, we resort to Large Language Models (LLMs) to correct the condition statements and similarity ratings in the original dataset proposed by Deshpande et al. (2023). Our proposed method is able to re-annotate a large training dataset for the C-STS task with minimal manual effort. Importantly, by training a supervised C-STS model on our cleaned and re-annotated dataset, we achieve a 5.4% statistically significant improvement in Spearman correlation. The re-annotated dataset is available at https://LivNLP.github.io/CSTS-reannotation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the problem of noisy annotation in the Conditional Semantic Textual Similarity (C-STS) dataset proposed by Deshpande et al. (2023). The authors propose a method to improve the dataset by using Large Language Models (LLMs) to correct condition statements and re-annotate similarity ratings. The process involves identifying issues with the original conditions, using GPT-4o to refine them, and then employing GPT-4o and Claude-3.7-Sonnet to independently re-annotate similarity ratings, which are subsequently combined with original human ratings.  The cleaned dataset is then used to train a supervised C-STS model, achieving a statistically significant improvement in Spearman correlation compared to models trained on the original dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a real problem:** The paper tackles a crucial issue in the C-STS field – the quality of existing datasets. Noisy labels and ambiguous conditions hinder the development of accurate C-STS models.
    *   **LLM-based approach:** Using LLMs for data cleaning is a practical and cost-effective approach. The study leverages LLMs' understanding of language to correct errors and inconsistencies.
    *   **Detailed analysis of annotation errors:** The paper provides a thorough analysis of different types of errors in both the conditions and similarity ratings (imbalanced conditions, subjective conditions, inconsistent phrasing, invalid/ambiguous conditions, verbose expressions, grammatical errors, unrelated conditions)
    *   **Quantifiable improvements:** The results show a tangible improvement (5.4% statistically significant improvement in Spearman correlation) when training models on the re-annotated data. This provides evidence of the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide a link to the re-annotated dataset, promoting further research and reproducibility.
    *   **Systematic experiments:** The authors conduct a reasonable set of experiments to validate their approach, analyzing the impact of condition modifications and label re-annotation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope of LLM evaluation:**  While GPT-4o and Claude-3.7-Sonnet are strong LLMs, there is no systematic evaluation of how the choice of LLM impacts the final C-STS performance. Different LLMs might have different biases.
    *   **Dependence on LLM judgements:** Reliance on LLMs introduces potential biases and limitations. The quality of the re-annotated data is limited by the LLMs' inherent abilities and potential for hallucination. There is some validation through human checking but only on 300 instances.
    *   **The specific prompt to be used may be sensitive to changes and future experiments may fail to reproduce the result**
    *   **Limited generalizability:** The study is focused on the English language.  While the approach could potentially be applied to other languages, there may be specific challenges and adaptations needed.
    *   **Limited comparative analysis:** There are limited examples of how the different identified classes of errors affect the downstream tasks. It could be interesting to evaluate the approach while correcting each class separately

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The novelty lies in the *systematic application of LLMs to address specific annotation issues in C-STS datasets*. While LLMs have been used for data augmentation and generation in NLP, this paper demonstrates a specific and effective method for *improving the quality of existing C-STS datasets*.  The analysis of various types of annotation errors is useful for future dataset creation.

    The significance is moderate. It contributes to making better, more reliable datasets for C-STS, which is important. The improvement in performance is worthwhile and shows the necessity of the work, but the improvements are only tested on an existing C-STS model and there are limited comparisons with recent literature. It is a good example of using LLMs to address data quality issues.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has a strong methodical approach for addressing a concrete problem in a subfield of NLP. The paper is well-structured, presents tangible improvements and provides clear analysis. However, the contribution doesn't seem ground-breaking and is based on leveraging existing LLMs, but their specific use for C-STS data cleaning and improved performance warrants a positive evaluation. The evaluation could have been more thorough.

### A Taxonomy of Prompt Defects in LLM Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14404v1)
- **Authors**: Haoye Tian, Chong Wang, BoYang Yang, Lyuye Zhang, Yang Liu
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have become key components of modern software, with prompts acting as their de-facto programming interface. However, prompt design remains largely empirical and small mistakes can cascade into unreliable, insecure, or inefficient behavior. This paper presents the first systematic survey and taxonomy of prompt defects, recurring ways that prompts fail to elicit their intended behavior from LLMs. We organize defects along six dimensions: (1) Specification and Intent, (2) Input and Content, (3) Structure and Formatting, (4) Context and Memory, (5) Performance and Efficiency, and (6) Maintainability and Engineering. Each dimension is refined into fine-grained subtypes, illustrated with concrete examples and root cause analysis. Grounded in software engineering principles, we show how these defects surface in real development workflows and examine their downstream effects. For every subtype, we distill mitigation strategies that span emerging prompt engineering patterns, automated guardrails, testing harnesses, and evaluation frameworks. We then summarize these strategies in a master taxonomy that links defect, impact, and remedy. We conclude with open research challenges and a call for rigorous engineering-oriented methodologies to ensure that LLM-driven systems are dependable by design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a taxonomy of prompt defects in large language model (LLM) systems.  It identifies six major dimensions along which prompts can fail: Specification & Intent, Input & Content, Structure & Formatting, Context & Memory, Performance & Efficiency, and Maintainability & Engineering.  Each dimension is further broken down into finer-grained subtypes, each illustrated with examples, impact analysis, and mitigation strategies. The goal is to provide a systematic way to understand and address common failure modes in prompt-driven systems, moving prompt engineering from an ad-hoc practice to a more disciplined engineering approach. The paper also emphasizes the importance of considering the model and runtime as potential sources of failure alongside the prompt itself.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in its systematic classification of prompt defects. While some individual defect types may have been previously recognized in isolation, this paper consolidates and organizes them into a coherent taxonomy. This is a novel and helpful framework for understanding prompt engineering challenges. Prior work largely focused on specific prompting strategies or attack vectors, not a comprehensive failure mode analysis. However, the novelty is somewhat constrained by the fact that some of the defect types are conceptually similar to common software bugs.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing reliance on LLMs in software systems and the critical role of prompts as the de-facto programming interface. By providing a shared vocabulary and a structured understanding of prompt defects, the paper can aid developers in creating more robust, reliable, and secure LLM-based applications. The identification of mitigation strategies further enhances the paper's practical value.  The taxonomy can inform the development of automated prompt testing, debugging, and security assessment tools.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The taxonomy appears relatively comprehensive, covering a broad range of potential prompt-related issues.
    *   **Clear Organization:**  The hierarchical structure and clear definitions make the taxonomy easy to understand and apply.
    *   **Practical Examples:**  The illustrative examples for each defect subtype help ground the concepts in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Mitigation Strategies:** The inclusion of mitigation strategies for each defect adds significant practical value for developers.
    *   **Addresses a Timely Problem:** As LLMs become more prevalent, understanding and mitigating prompt defects is becoming increasingly crucial.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Overlapping Categories:** Some of the categories and subtypes may exhibit overlap. For example, "Underspecified Constraints" and "Ambiguous Instruction" can be closely related. Some cross-cutting concerns might be better represented through a different organizational structure.
    *   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** While the taxonomy seems well-reasoned, it could benefit from empirical validation to assess its coverage and usefulness in practice. A study involving developers using the taxonomy to analyze real-world prompt defects would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Model-Specific Considerations:**  While the paper acknowledges the importance of the model and runtime, the taxonomy and mitigation strategies are primarily focused on prompt-level defects. A more in-depth discussion of how model-specific characteristics (e.g., context window size, instruction following abilities) influence the occurrence and severity of different defect types would be beneficial. The relationship between "hallucination" and prompt defects is only implicitly addressed.
    *   **Limited Focus on Attack Mitigation:** Although vulnerabilities are mentioned, the "Security/Safety Review Gaps" section and mitigation is light. Better cross-referencing with existing work on adversarial attacks is needed.
    *   **"Maintainability & Engineering" feels a bit tacked on:** While prompt management is important, its inclusion feels less organically related to the other defect categories which are more focused on eliciting correct behavior from the LLM during runtime.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of prompt engineering methodologies, automated prompt testing tools, and security assessment techniques for LLM-based systems. It could also serve as a valuable resource for educating developers about common prompt-related pitfalls.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution by systematically categorizing prompt defects, a crucial area as LLMs become increasingly integrated into software development. While the taxonomy may have some overlapping categories and requires more empirical validation, its comprehensiveness, clear organization, practical examples, and mitigation strategies make it a significant resource for developers and researchers. The lack of depth in integrating model specificities and adversarial considerations, along with the slightly forced fit for the maintainability section, lowers the score. Overall, it provides a foundation for further research and the development of better tooling for prompt engineering.

### Adding LLMs to the psycholinguistic norming toolbox: A practical guide to getting the most out of human ratings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14405v1)
- **Authors**: Javier Conde, María Grandury, Tairan Fu, Carlos Arriaga, Gonzalo Martínez, Thomas Clark, Sean Trott, Clarence Gerald Green, Pedro Reviriego, Marc Brysbaert
- **Abstract**: Word-level psycholinguistic norms lend empirical support to theories of language processing. However, obtaining such human-based measures is not always feasible or straightforward. One promising approach is to augment human norming datasets by using Large Language Models (LLMs) to predict these characteristics directly, a practice that is rapidly gaining popularity in psycholinguistics and cognitive science. However, the novelty of this approach (and the relative inscrutability of LLMs) necessitates the adoption of rigorous methodologies that guide researchers through this process, present the range of possible approaches, and clarify limitations that are not immediately apparent, but may, in some cases, render the use of LLMs impractical. In this work, we present a comprehensive methodology for estimating word characteristics with LLMs, enriched with practical advice and lessons learned from our own experience. Our approach covers both the direct use of base LLMs and the fine-tuning of models, an alternative that can yield substantial performance gains in certain scenarios. A major emphasis in the guide is the validation of LLM-generated data with human "gold standard" norms. We also present a software framework that implements our methodology and supports both commercial and open-weight models. We illustrate the proposed approach with a case study on estimating word familiarity in English. Using base models, we achieved a Spearman correlation of 0.8 with human ratings, which increased to 0.9 when employing fine-tuned models. This methodology, framework, and set of best practices aim to serve as a reference for future research on leveraging LLMs for psycholinguistic and lexical studies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a practical guide for using Large Language Models (LLMs) to augment psycholinguistic norming, traditionally done with human ratings. It covers various aspects, from choosing the right LLM (commercial vs. open-source), prompt engineering, and fine-tuning to validation and addressing common pitfalls. The authors advocate for validating LLM-generated data with human "gold standards" and provide a software framework to implement their methodology. A case study estimating word familiarity in English is presented, demonstrating improved correlations with human ratings through fine-tuning. The paper emphasizes reproducibility and ethical considerations in leveraging LLMs for psycholinguistic research. The main argument is that LLMs, when used carefully and validated, can be valuable tools for efficiently obtaining word characteristics, especially when human data collection is difficult or expensive.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs for psycholinguistic norming isn't entirely new. Several recent papers explore this avenue. However, the paper's *practical focus* and *comprehensive approach* are novel aspects. It compiles implicit knowledge gained from experience and converts it into concrete, actionable advice. The accompanying software framework also adds value. It directly addresses a common concern within the community and has been noted as a much-needed effort.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field by:

    *   **Making LLMs more accessible to psycholinguists:** The practical guide and framework lower the barrier to entry, enabling researchers with varying technical skills to utilize LLMs.
    *   **Promoting methodological rigor:** By emphasizing validation, reproducibility, and ethical considerations, the paper contributes to responsible adoption of LLMs in research.
    *   **Facilitating research efficiency:** The framework streamlines the norming process, saving time and resources, particularly for languages or constructs where human data is scarce.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive scope:** The paper covers a wide range of crucial aspects, from model selection to fine-tuning and validation.
    *   **Practical focus:** The guide is filled with actionable advice, code examples, and real-world insights.
    *   **Emphasis on validation:** The authors strongly advocate for validating LLM outputs with human data, which is essential for ensuring reliability.
    *   **Open-source framework:** The availability of the framework enhances reproducibility and allows other researchers to build upon the work.
    *   **Ethical considerations:** The paper explicitly discusses ethical concerns relating to AI and recommends that LLM data is only used to augment human datasets, rather than replacing them.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Rapidly evolving landscape:** The field of LLMs is changing quickly. Some of the specific recommendations (e.g., about model performance) might become outdated relatively soon.
    *   **Limited discussion of theoretical implications:** While the paper focuses on practical aspects, a deeper discussion of the theoretical implications of using LLMs for norming would be beneficial. For example, if LLMs are merely approximating human judgements and if this approximation has a theoretical impact on downstream tasks or theories.
    *   **Dependence on external APIs:** The framework relies on external APIs (e.g., OpenAI), which can introduce dependencies and cost considerations. The advice on selecting 'fully open models' is somewhat undermined by the suggestion to run the process on external API's in other sections.
    *   **Data contamination is still a concern, even with validation:** While the validation process addresses data contamination, the fact that LLMs are trained on vast amounts of data and the inability to determine every component of the pre-training data leaves a shadow of a doubt as to whether the results are independent of pre-existing human data.

*   **Influence on the field:** The guide has the potential to become a standard reference for psycholinguists interested in using LLMs for norming. It will likely influence research practices and contribute to the development of more robust and reliable methods.

**Rationale for the Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a much-needed practical guide to a rapidly evolving area of research.  It addresses a clear need within the psycholinguistics community for guidance on using LLMs responsibly. The framework and validation advice are key strengths. However, the rapidly changing nature of LLMs and some of the inherent limitations prevent it from achieving a very high score.

### GestOS: Advanced Hand Gesture Interpretation via Large Language Models to control Any Type of Robot
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14412v1)
- **Authors**: Artem Lykov, Oleg Kobzarev, Dzmitry Tsetserukou
- **Abstract**: We present GestOS, a gesture-based operating system for high-level control of heterogeneous robot teams. Unlike prior systems that map gestures to fixed commands or single-agent actions, GestOS interprets hand gestures semantically and dynamically distributes tasks across multiple robots based on their capabilities, current state, and supported instruction sets. The system combines lightweight visual perception with large language model (LLM) reasoning: hand poses are converted into structured textual descriptions, which the LLM uses to infer intent and generate robot-specific commands. A robot selection module ensures that each gesture-triggered task is matched to the most suitable agent in real time. This architecture enables context-aware, adaptive control without requiring explicit user specification of targets or commands. By advancing gesture interaction from recognition to intelligent orchestration, GestOS supports scalable, flexible, and user-friendly collaboration with robotic systems in dynamic environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GestOS paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GestOS, a gesture-based operating system for controlling heterogeneous robot teams using Large Language Models (LLMs). Unlike traditional systems that map gestures to fixed commands, GestOS interprets gestures semantically and dynamically distributes tasks across robots based on their capabilities, current state, and supported instruction sets. The system leverages lightweight visual perception (MediaPipe) to extract symbolic descriptions of hand gestures, which are then interpreted by an LLM to infer user intent and generate robot-specific commands. A robot selection module ensures that tasks are assigned to the most suitable agent in real-time. The authors claim that this architecture enables context-aware, adaptive control without requiring explicit user specification. They demonstrate the system's functionality on a robotic manipulator (UR3) and a quadruped robot (Unitree GO1).

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Integration of LLMs for robotic control:** The use of LLMs for semantic reasoning in gesture-based robotic control is a promising direction.  Leveraging LLMs to handle ambiguity and context is a significant advantage.
    * **Multi-robot task distribution:** The dynamic allocation of tasks to robots based on their capabilities and state is a valuable feature, addressing a key limitation of many existing gesture control systems.
    * **Modular architecture:** The modular design allows for easier extension to new robot types, gesture sets, or interaction paradigms.
    * **Real-time performance:** Emphasis on real-time responsiveness by using lightweight visual perception alongside LLM reasoning is a notable achievement.
    * **Experimental validation:** The experimental results, while limited in scope, provide evidence of the system's functionality and accuracy on two different robotic platforms.
    * **Clear problem statement:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing gesture-based control systems and proposes a solution to address these limitations.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Novelty:** While the integration of the various components is interesting, the individual components themselves (MediaPipe, LLMs) are not novel. The specific *application* of LLMs for task distribution is incremental. CognitiveOS [15] mentioned as related work, raises the concern about the true novelty if there are similar works in this area.
    * **Experiment Scope:** The experimental validation is limited to a small set of commands and two robotic platforms. The robustness and generalizability of the system to more complex environments and tasks is not fully demonstrated.
    * **Command Set limitations:** The paper acknowledges decreased accuracy for dynamic gestures, suggesting that the system may struggle with more complex or continuous interactions.
    * **LLM reliance:** The system's performance is heavily reliant on the performance of the LLM. The paper does not thoroughly address the potential for LLM errors or biases to impact the system's behavior.
    * **Lack of comparative analysis:**  The paper does not include a direct comparison to existing gesture control systems (e.g., GestureGPT) to demonstrate the superiority of GestOS. Quantifying the improvement offered by the multi-robot task distribution aspect would strengthen the contribution.
    * **No mention of safety features:** Given the use of LLMs, there's no discussion of how GestOS handles safety concerns and prevents potentially harmful actions by the robots.

* **Significance:**

The significance of the paper lies in its potential to advance the field of human-robot interaction by providing a more intuitive, flexible, and scalable approach to gesture-based control. The integration of LLMs for semantic reasoning and multi-robot task distribution has the potential to enable more seamless and collaborative human-robot interactions in various domains. However, the limitations in the experimental validation and the reliance on LLM performance need to be addressed in future research to fully realize this potential.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a promising system for gesture-based robotic control using LLMs. While the individual components are not novel, the integration of these components to enable multi-robot task distribution is a valuable contribution. However, the limited experimental validation, the acknowledged performance limitations, the absence of the thorough comparitive analysis, and lack of safety considerations detract from the paper's overall impact.

### Causal-Counterfactual RAG: The Integration of Causal-Counterfactual Reasoning into RAG
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14435v1)
- **Authors**: Harshad Khadilkar, Abhay Gupta
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have transformed natural language processing (NLP), enabling diverse applications by integrating large-scale pre-trained knowledge. However, their static knowledge limits dynamic reasoning over external information, especially in knowledge-intensive domains. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) addresses this challenge by combining retrieval mechanisms with generative modeling to improve contextual understanding. Traditional RAG systems suffer from disrupted contextual integrity due to text chunking and over-reliance on semantic similarity for retrieval, often resulting in shallow and less accurate responses. We propose Causal-Counterfactual RAG, a novel framework that integrates explicit causal graphs representing cause-effect relationships into the retrieval process and incorporates counterfactual reasoning grounded on the causal structure. Unlike conventional methods, our framework evaluates not only direct causal evidence but also the counterfactuality of associated causes, combining results from both to generate more robust, accurate, and interpretable answers. By leveraging causal pathways and associated hypothetical scenarios, Causal-Counterfactual RAG preserves contextual coherence, reduces hallucination, and enhances reasoning fidelity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Causal-Counterfactual RAG: The Integration of Causal-Counterfactual Reasoning into RAG":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces Causal-Counterfactual RAG, a new retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) framework that aims to improve answer quality, robustness, and interpretability by integrating explicit causal graphs and counterfactual reasoning. Unlike traditional RAG systems that primarily rely on semantic similarity for retrieval, this framework constructs a Causal Knowledge Graph (CKG) representing cause-effect relationships. When answering a user query, the system parses the query into causal components, retrieves relevant context from the CKG, and then performs counterfactual interventions. It generates logical opposites of potential causes and simulates their effects to determine which causes are truly necessary for the outcome. This process allows the system to validate causal relationships, reduce hallucinations, and generate more coherent and trustworthy answers. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that Causal-Counterfactual RAG outperforms regular RAG in terms of precision, recall, causal chain integrity, and counterfactual robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of integrating causal graphs and counterfactual reasoning into RAG is relatively novel. While some prior work has explored causal discovery or causal effects estimation with LLMs, this paper more directly integrates these concepts into the retrieval and generation pipeline of a RAG system.  The focus on *counterfactual validation* of retrieved information is a significant contribution. However, the individual components (RAG, causal graphs, counterfactual reasoning) are well-established, so the primary novelty lies in their specific integration within this context.

* **Significance:** The potential significance of this work is high.  Current RAG systems often struggle with generating accurate and trustworthy answers, particularly in knowledge-intensive domains where nuanced causal relationships are important. By incorporating causal reasoning and counterfactual validation, this framework addresses a key limitation of existing RAG systems. Improvements in answer quality, robustness, and interpretability are crucial for building trust in LLM-based applications.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addresses a critical limitation:**  The paper clearly identifies and tackles a significant problem in RAG: the inability to distinguish between correlation and causation, leading to inaccuracies and hallucinations.
    * **Well-defined framework:** The proposed Causal-Counterfactual RAG framework is well-defined and described in detail, including the construction of the CKG, query parsing, context retrieval, and counterfactual intervention steps.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** The experimental evaluation includes a comparison against a baseline RAG system across multiple metrics (precision, recall, causal chain integrity, counterfactual robustness), providing evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed framework. The use of an LLM judge to assess correctness, faithfulness, and reasoning is a valuable addition.
    * **Clear presentation:**  The paper is well-written and easy to understand, with clear explanations of the key concepts and methodology.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on LLM accuracy:** The entire framework relies heavily on the accuracy of LLMs for cause-effect extraction, query parsing, and counterfactual generation. The paper acknowledges this limitation but doesn't thoroughly explore the potential impact of LLM errors on the overall performance of the system. Error propagation within the LLM processes is a real danger.
    * **Complexity and Scalability:** Constructing and maintaining a CKG can be computationally expensive, and the counterfactual intervention step adds further complexity. The paper doesn't adequately address the scalability of the framework to large-scale knowledge bases or high-volume query workloads. The description of experimentation mentions datasets being "custom" and the OpenAlex corpus, leaving questions of reproducibility.
    * **Limited Empirical Scope:**  While the evaluation includes multiple metrics, the datasets used for evaluation are not extensively described and the impact of causal grounding on more benchmark retrieval-augmented generation tasks remains unclear.
    * **Lack of Generalizability:**  The specialized nature of the model creates a concern of generalizability of this framework to tasks that involve a reduced emphasis on causal relations. The framework is likely overkill for many RAG applications.

* **Potential Influence:** This work could influence the development of more robust and trustworthy RAG systems by highlighting the importance of causal reasoning and counterfactual validation.  It could inspire further research into methods for incorporating causal knowledge into LLMs and for improving the interpretability and explainability of LLM-generated answers.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the integrated approach, the significance of the problem being addressed, and the well-defined framework with supporting experimental results, but also considering the limitations in scalability, reliance on LLM accuracy, and limited empirical scope, I assign a score of **7**.  The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating the potential benefits of integrating causal reasoning and counterfactual validation into RAG systems. However, further research is needed to address the limitations and to assess the scalability and generalizability of the framework.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Content is Goliath and Algorithm is David: The Style and Semantic Effects of Generative Search Engine
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14436v1)
- **Authors**: Lijia Ma, Juan Qin, Xingchen Xu, Yong Tan
- **Abstract**: Generative search engines (GEs) leverage large language models (LLMs) to deliver AI-generated summaries with website citations, establishing novel traffic acquisition channels while fundamentally altering the search engine optimization landscape. To investigate the distinctive characteristics of GEs, we collect data through interactions with Google's generative and conventional search platforms, compiling a dataset of approximately ten thousand websites across both channels. Our empirical analysis reveals that GEs exhibit preferences for citing content characterized by significantly higher predictability for underlying LLMs and greater semantic similarity among selected sources. Through controlled experiments utilizing retrieval augmented generation (RAG) APIs, we demonstrate that these citation preferences emerge from intrinsic LLM tendencies to favor content aligned with their generative expression patterns. Motivated by applications of LLMs to optimize website content, we conduct additional experimentation to explore how LLM-based content polishing by website proprietors alters AI summaries, finding that such polishing paradoxically enhances information diversity within AI summaries. Finally, to assess the user-end impact of LLM-induced information increases, we design a generative search engine and recruit Prolific participants to conduct a randomized controlled experiment involving an information-seeking and writing task. We find that higher-educated users exhibit minimal changes in their final outputs' information diversity but demonstrate significantly reduced task completion time when original sites undergo polishing. Conversely, lower-educated users primarily benefit through enhanced information density in their task outputs while maintaining similar completion times across experimental groups.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of Generative Search Engines (GEs), powered by Large Language Models (LLMs), on content selection and user experience. It explores how GEs cite websites, how LLM characteristics influence these citations, and how LLM-driven content polishing affects information diversity and user outcomes. The study finds that GEs favor content with lower perplexity (higher predictability for LLMs) and semantic similarity. Experiments show that LLM-based content polishing paradoxically *increases* information diversity in AI summaries. Finally, a user study reveals differential impacts based on education: higher-educated users improve task *efficiency* with polished content, while lower-educated users benefit from increased information *density*.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Relevant Topic:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and timely issue: the evolving landscape of search with the emergence of generative AI and its implications for SEO and user behavior.
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:**  The study employs a multi-faceted approach, combining large-scale data analysis of search engine outputs with controlled experiments and user studies. This strengthens the validity and generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Interesting Findings:** The counterintuitive finding that LLM-driven polishing enhances information diversity is particularly compelling. The differential effects based on user education also offer valuable insights into human-AI interaction.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors employ sound econometric techniques, control for confounding factors, and perform robustness checks. The experimental designs are well-structured and the analyses are clearly presented.
    *   **Theoretical Contributions:** The paper offers valuable insights into the interplay between LLM characteristics, content optimization strategies, and user experiences in the context of generative search.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Google-Centric:** The primary data analysis focuses on Google's AI Overview. While justified by Google's market share, this limits the generalizability of the findings to other GE platforms. Robustness checks with Bing are helpful, but not as comprehensive.
    *   **Limited User Task:** The user study employs a specific task (policy brief on smartphone bans). While chosen for its relevance, the generalizability of the findings to other information-seeking tasks could be explored further.
    *   **Evolution of LLMs:** The study's temporal validity is a concern, given the rapid evolution of LLMs and search engine algorithms. The observed effects may change as models become more sophisticated. However, the multiple time period analysis does alleviate this.
    *   **Lack of Discussion on Ethical Concerns:** Despite mentioning strategic manipulation in the conclusion, the paper does not delve into the potentially negative ethical implications of LLM-driven content optimization and its impact on information quality and bias.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive exploration of the mechanisms underlying GE citations, particularly the role of LLM characteristics and the surprising effect of content polishing.
    *   It sheds light on the complex dynamics of the evolving search ecosystem, offering insights for website owners, search engine providers, and end-users.
    *   The identification of differential effects based on user education is a valuable contribution to the human-AI interaction literature.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field. It is well-executed, methodologically sound, and addresses a timely and relevant topic. While the reliance on Google data and the specific user task limit generalizability to some extent, the robust analyses and intriguing findings outweigh these weaknesses. The paper is more than just an incremental improvement; it presents a novel perspective on the impact of GEs and offers actionable insights. However, with a discussion on the ethical implications and a discussion on the broader impact of information quality and bias, the score would be higher.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Simulating a Bias Mitigation Scenario in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14438v1)
- **Authors**: Kiana Kiashemshaki, Mohammad Jalili Torkamani, Negin Mahmoudi, Meysam Shirdel Bilehsavar
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have fundamentally transformed the field of natural language processing; however, their vulnerability to biases presents a notable obstacle that threatens both fairness and trust. This review offers an extensive analysis of the bias landscape in LLMs, tracing its roots and expressions across various NLP tasks. Biases are classified into implicit and explicit types, with particular attention given to their emergence from data sources, architectural designs, and contextual deployments. This study advances beyond theoretical analysis by implementing a simulation framework designed to evaluate bias mitigation strategies in practice. The framework integrates multiple approaches including data curation, debiasing during model training, and post-hoc output calibration and assesses their impact in controlled experimental settings. In summary, this work not only synthesizes existing knowledge on bias in LLMs but also contributes original empirical validation through simulation of mitigation strategies.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates bias mitigation strategies in Large Language Models (LLMs). It begins by reviewing the landscape of bias in LLMs, classifying biases as implicit or explicit and tracing their origins from data, architectural design, and deployment contexts. The core contribution is a simulation framework designed to evaluate bias mitigation techniques such as data curation, debiasing during model training, and post-hoc output calibration. The paper then details a case study using the "Bias in Bios" dataset, experimenting with oversampling, loss weighting, and post-processing Equalized Odds to mitigate gender bias in profession prediction.  The results are presented in terms of accuracy and fairness metrics (Disparate Impact and Equalized Odds Difference), and the authors discuss the trade-offs between performance and fairness, emphasizing the importance of selecting mitigation strategies based on the desired balance. The paper argues for a move toward causal modeling to identify and address systemic biases, with implications for AI systems that are more socially responsible.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Overview:** The paper provides a good overview of the bias problem in LLMs, covering different types of biases and their potential sources.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The use of a simulation framework to evaluate bias mitigation strategies is a practical contribution. The choice of the "Bias in Bios" dataset is relevant and well-justified, and the experiments are relatively well-defined.
    *   **Clear Presentation of Results:** The results are presented clearly with performance and fairness metrics, allowing for easy comparison of different mitigation strategies.
    *   **Emphasis on Trade-offs:** The paper acknowledges and discusses the inherent trade-offs between model performance and fairness, which is crucial in real-world applications.
    *   **Causal Modeling Perspective:** The shift toward a causal modeling paradigm is a valuable contribution, as it recognizes the importance of systemic and societal biases rather than just surface-level statistical imbalances. The paper connects the technical problem to a broader discussion of equality and equity, providing a more nuanced approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Mitigation Techniques:** The bias mitigation techniques themselves (oversampling, loss weighting, Equalized Odds) are not particularly novel. The paper primarily applies existing techniques to a specific dataset and evaluation framework. The innovation is more in the overall experimental design and in placing mitigation in a broader causal-model framing.
    *   **Specific Dataset:** The "Bias in Bios" dataset, while useful, is somewhat limited in scope. The findings might not generalize directly to other datasets or tasks with different types of biases.
    *   **Depth of Analysis:** While the paper presents a good overview, the analysis of the causal relationships and systemic biases could be more in-depth. More exploration of "how" and "why" particular interventions worked (or didn't work) would enhance the analysis. For example, the limited novelty in the bias mitigation techniques means that more detailed analysis is needed.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Analysis:** While the paper discusses limitations of current mitigation techniques, a deeper comparison and benchmark with more recent methods, especially those specifically designed for LLMs, is missing.
    *   **Causal Modeling is High-Level:** The concept of causal modeling is discussed, but it's not fully implemented in the experiments. While the direction is correct, there is a limited attempt to learn the causal structures between sensitive attributes (gender, profession) and other data.
    *   **Methodology for Bias mitigation techniques is not fully explained:** Lack of insight into the specific parameters and configurations used for loss weighting and post-processing methods limits reproducibility and understanding.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of work on fairness and bias in LLMs, which is a critical area of research. The experimental framework and the discussion of trade-offs are valuable for practitioners.
    *   The emphasis on causal modeling and systemic biases aligns with a more responsible approach to AI development. While the paper doesn't fully implement this vision, it sets a good direction for future research.
    *   Provides a practical example of how to evaluate and compare bias mitigation strategies.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **6**.  While the paper addresses a crucial problem and has some strengths, it doesn't demonstrate significant novelty in its mitigation techniques or depth of causal analysis. The primary contributions are the relatively well-executed experiments, the comparative evaluations of the different methods, and the discussion of trade-offs, all within the broader (but not fully realized) framing of causal modeling. The limited novelty in the methods, the focus on one dataset, and the lack of in-depth analysis of the experimental results prevent a higher score.
