### VCBench: Benchmarking LLMs in Venture Capital
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14448v1)
- **Authors**: Rick Chen, Joseph Ternasky, Afriyie Samuel Kwesi, Ben Griffin, Aaron Ontoyin Yin, Zakari Salifu, Kelvin Amoaba, Xianling Mu, Fuat Alican, Yigit Ihlamur
- **Abstract**: Benchmarks such as SWE-bench and ARC-AGI demonstrate how shared datasets accelerate progress toward artificial general intelligence (AGI). We introduce VCBench, the first benchmark for predicting founder success in venture capital (VC), a domain where signals are sparse, outcomes are uncertain, and even top investors perform modestly. At inception, the market index achieves a precision of 1.9%. Y Combinator outperforms the index by a factor of 1.7x, while tier-1 firms are 2.9x better. VCBench provides 9,000 anonymized founder profiles, standardized to preserve predictive features while resisting identity leakage, with adversarial tests showing more than 90% reduction in re-identification risk. We evaluate nine state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs). DeepSeek-V3 delivers over six times the baseline precision, GPT-4o achieves the highest F0.5, and most models surpass human benchmarks. Designed as a public and evolving resource available at vcbench.com, VCBench establishes a community-driven standard for reproducible and privacy-preserving evaluation of AGI in early-stage venture forecasting.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces VCBench, a novel benchmark for evaluating large language models (LLMs) in the context of venture capital (VC) founder success prediction. The authors address the challenges of building such a benchmark, including data irregularity, coverage imbalance, and the potential for data contamination (LLMs re-identifying founders). VCBench provides 9,000 anonymized founder profiles with a 9% success baseline, a standardized format, and an anonymization pipeline validated by adversarial re-identification tests. The paper evaluates nine state-of-the-art LLMs, demonstrating that several outperform human baselines, while ensuring that the performance gain is not simply due to data leakage, rather predictive signal. Finally, the authors release the dataset and a public leaderboard to encourage further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and interesting problem: benchmarking LLMs in a domain where expert forecasting is difficult (VC) and where signals are sparse and uncertain. Building a clean, anonymized, and standardized dataset in this context is a valuable contribution.

*Novelty:* The creation of VCBench is itself a novel contribution. There are other benchmark datasets (such as those for medical diagnosis and software engineering), but the paper rightly points out that the VC benchmark faces different challenges. The integration of an anonymization pipeline with adversarial re-identification tests is particularly novel, demonstrating awareness of LLM limitations and a focus on evaluating genuine reasoning. While some prior work exists on startup success prediction, this paper's emphasis on standardization, anonymization, and public availability differentiates it significantly from existing proprietary datasets.

*Significance:* The paper has the potential to impact the field by:
1.  Providing a standardized platform for evaluating LLMs for expert forecasting.
2.  Offering human expert baselines to measure model performance against.
3.  Addressing concerns about data leakage and ensuring fair model comparison.
4.  Encouraging community-driven research in VC prediction.

*Weaknesses:*
1.  *Prevalence Shift:* The dataset uses an inflated success rate (9% vs 1.9% in the real world). This is a well-justified decision, as it makes the evaluation of models easier, but the paper highlights that this prevalence shift makes it difficult to extrapolate results to the real-world setting, as relationships between precision and the success rate might not scale linearly.
2.  *Limited Data:* The dataset size (9,000) is not extremely large, and its representativeness is limited by its reliance on LinkedIn and Crunchbase data.
3.  *Censoring Bias:* The 8-year success window also introduces a censoring bias, negatively impacting younger firms.
4.  *Comparability of Human Baselines:* It's unclear how well the process of normalising the performance of VC's actually reflects how well they perform.

These limitations do not negate the value of the benchmark, but researchers must remain aware of them when interpreting results.

*Potential Influence:* VCBench has a high probability of being used in the field, as the authors have already created a website and leaderboard. It will serve as a good standard, encouraging further work in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

VCBench is a solid contribution that tackles a complex and relevant problem. The anonymization and standardization efforts are thorough. The paper acknowledges the limitations of its approach, and the benchmarking results are clearly presented. However, several issues limit the broad applicability of its results (most obviously, the issue of prevalence shift). While valuable, this is not a paradigm-shifting paper, but rather a strong step forward.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Correct-Detect: Balancing Performance and Ambiguity Through the Lens of Coreference Resolution in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14456v1)
- **Authors**: Amber Shore, Russell Scheinberg, Ameeta Agrawal, So Young Lee
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are intended to reflect human linguistic competencies. But humans have access to a broad and embodied context, which is key in detecting and resolving linguistic ambiguities, even in isolated text spans. A foundational case of semantic ambiguity is found in the task of coreference resolution: how is a pronoun related to an earlier person mention? This capability is implicit in nearly every downstream task, and the presence of ambiguity at this level can alter performance significantly. We show that LLMs can achieve good performance with minimal prompting in both coreference disambiguation and the detection of ambiguity in coreference, however, they cannot do both at the same time. We present the CORRECT-DETECT trade-off: though models have both capabilities and deploy them implicitly, successful performance balancing these two abilities remains elusive.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CORRECT-DETECT: Balancing Performance and Ambiguity Through the Lens of Coreference Resolution in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to both resolve coreference (identifying which noun a pronoun refers to) and detect ambiguity in coreference resolution.  The authors argue that LLMs, unlike humans, struggle to simultaneously achieve high accuracy in unambiguous cases *and* reliably identify ambiguity when it is present. They introduce the "CORRECT-DETECT trade-off," demonstrating that improving performance on one of these tasks often comes at the expense of the other. They leverage the AmbiCoref dataset, which includes sentences designed to be either unambiguous or genuinely ambiguous regarding coreference, and compare LLM performance against human annotations. The study examines different prompting strategies and analyzes model responses to explore this trade-off, considering factors like gender bias and the presence of explanations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's core contribution lies in highlighting and demonstrating the CORRECT-DETECT trade-off within coreference resolution in LLMs. While individual aspects of the problem (LLM coreference resolution capabilities, ambiguity handling) have been explored before, the *explicit articulation and empirical demonstration* of this trade-off are novel. Specifically, the structured experiments contrasting performance on unambiguous *vs.* ambiguous sentences, tied to human annotations, lend credibility to the argument. Prior work has touched on ambiguity detection, but rarely in direct competition with solving the problem (coreference resolution).

* **Significance:** This research is significant because it addresses a fundamental aspect of human-like language understanding: the ability to gracefully handle ambiguity. It underscores that LLMs, despite their impressive performance on many NLP tasks, may lack the nuanced ability to discern when information is truly insufficient to make a confident decision. This limitation can have implications for various downstream applications where LLMs are used, such as summarization, question answering, and dialogue systems. Furthermore, the study raises important questions about the training incentives for LLMs, suggesting that the focus on confident output, even when unwarranted, may hinder the development of more robust and reliable language models.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The CORRECT-DETECT trade-off is clearly defined and well-motivated.
    * **Rigorous Methodology:** The use of a controlled dataset (AmbiCoref), comparison with human annotations, and systematic exploration of different prompting strategies contribute to the rigor of the study.
    * **Detailed Analysis:** The analysis of model responses, including gender bias and the presence of explanations, provides valuable insights into the underlying mechanisms.
    * **Focus on base capabilities:** It provides insights on the trade-off using models' base capabilities without finetuning, thus providing fundamental trade-off insights.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study focuses specifically on coreference resolution. While this is a prototypical case of ambiguity, it remains to be seen how well the CORRECT-DETECT trade-off generalizes to other types of linguistic ambiguity (e.g., lexical ambiguity, syntactic ambiguity). While there were expansion of the task, they were limited to Entailment.
    * **Prompt Sensitivity:** The paper acknowledges the sensitivity of LLM performance to prompting. While they explore multiple prompts, the results might still be influenced by the specific prompts used, and other prompting approaches could potentially mitigate the trade-off to some extent.
    * **Model-Specific Observations:** Some of the observations, like the significance of "explain" responses, appear to be model-specific (e.g., more relevant to GPT-4o).
    * **Limited datasets:** The dataset is limited to English, which could induce bias.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to influence future research in several ways:
    * It motivates the development of new evaluation metrics that explicitly consider both accuracy and ambiguity detection capabilities.
    * It encourages researchers to explore training strategies that incentivize LLMs to acknowledge uncertainty.
    * It provides a framework for analyzing the trade-offs involved in designing LLMs for robustness and reliability.
    * It highlights the importance of grounding LLMs' learning and reasoning mechanisms in such tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a clear, important, and demonstrably novel contribution to our understanding of how LLMs handle ambiguity. While the scope is limited to coreference resolution and the results may be somewhat prompt- and model-specific, the identification and articulation of the CORRECT-DETECT trade-off are valuable insights that will likely stimulate further research and have a positive impact on the field. There are still opportunities for further work to extend the findings and make them applicable more broadly.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Not What the Doctor Ordered: Surveying LLM-based De-identification and Quantifying Clinical Information Loss
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14464v1)
- **Authors**: Kiana Aghakasiri, Noopur Zambare, JoAnn Thai, Carrie Ye, Mayur Mehta, J. Ross Mitchell, Mohamed Abdalla
- **Abstract**: De-identification in the healthcare setting is an application of NLP where automated algorithms are used to remove personally identifying information of patients (and, sometimes, providers). With the recent rise of generative large language models (LLMs), there has been a corresponding rise in the number of papers that apply LLMs to de-identification. Although these approaches often report near-perfect results, significant challenges concerning reproducibility and utility of the research papers persist. This paper identifies three key limitations in the current literature: inconsistent reporting metrics hindering direct comparisons, the inadequacy of traditional classification metrics in capturing errors which LLMs may be more prone to (i.e., altering clinically relevant information), and lack of manual validation of automated metrics which aim to quantify these errors. To address these issues, we first present a survey of LLM-based de-identification research, highlighting the heterogeneity in reporting standards. Second, we evaluated a diverse set of models to quantify the extent of inappropriate removal of clinical information. Next, we conduct a manual validation of an existing evaluation metric to measure the removal of clinical information, employing clinical experts to assess their efficacy. We highlight poor performance and describe the inherent limitations of such metrics in identifying clinically significant changes. Lastly, we propose a novel methodology for the detection of clinically relevant information removal.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Not What the Doctor Ordered: Surveying LLM-based De-identification and Quantifying Clinical Information Loss":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to de-identify clinical text. It identifies limitations in current research, including inconsistent reporting metrics, inadequate evaluation of clinical information loss, and a lack of manual validation of automated metrics. The authors present a survey of LLM-based de-identification research, quantify inappropriate removal of clinical information by various models, and validate an existing metric for measuring clinical information loss. They highlight its poor performance and limitations, and propose a novel methodology (CIRE) for detecting clinically relevant information removal. Overall, the paper highlights the need for more rigorous and clinically informed evaluation when using LLMs for de-identification in healthcare.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a critical area in the application of NLP to healthcare: ensuring that de-identification methods not only remove Personally Identifiable Information (PII) but also preserve clinically relevant information. The survey of existing LLM-based de-identification research effectively highlights the heterogeneity in evaluation practices, a significant barrier to comparing and reproducing results.

The authors' identification of the shortcomings of traditional classification metrics and the existing CIR metric (JSC) in capturing clinically significant changes is well-articulated. This is a crucial observation, as relying solely on standard accuracy measures can lead to a false sense of security about the quality of de-identification. The manual validation of these metrics using clinical experts adds considerable weight to their findings.

The proposed methodology, CIRE (Clinical Information Retention Evaluation), represents a novel attempt to directly assess the semantic impact of de-identification using an LLM. The initial results showing a stronger correlation of CIRE with manual labeling compared to JSC are promising, although the gains are not overwhelmingly large.

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses an Important Problem:** Highlights the critical issue of clinical information loss during de-identification.
*   **Thorough Survey:** Provides a good overview of current LLM-based de-identification research and its limitations.
*   **Manual Validation:**  The use of clinical experts to validate metrics is a significant strength and increases the reliability of the findings.
*   **Novel Methodology (CIRE):** Presents a potentially useful approach for evaluating clinical information retention.
*   **Practical Insights:** Offers practical insights into the challenges of using LLMs for de-identification and the importance of rigorous evaluation.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Data:** While the authors use two datasets, the private dataset (AHS) is relatively small.
*   **Computational Cost:** The CIRE method relies on LLMs, which can be computationally expensive, potentially limiting its widespread adoption. The manual annotation required is another source of substantial cost.
*   **CIRE Refinement:** While CIRE shows promise, further refinement and validation are necessary to improve its precision and recall in detecting all types of clinically relevant changes. The observed correlation between CIRE and manual CIR, while stronger than JSC, still leaves room for improvement.
*   **Lack of comparison:** More discussion is needed regarding how CIRE compares to other methods to measure bias and hallucinations in clinical text generation.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it moves beyond simple accuracy metrics and advocates for a more nuanced and clinically informed evaluation of de-identification methods. It highlights potential risks associated with relying solely on LLMs for this task and emphasizes the importance of human oversight and validation. The proposed CIRE methodology, while still needing further development, represents a step in the right direction.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper addresses a pressing problem with well-executed experiments and strong manual validation. The study is relatively novel and useful. The limitations associated with data size and CIRE's refinement impact its immediate practical applicability, and hence, the score is not higher. Despite these limitations, the paper's contribution to highlighting the need for more robust and clinically relevant evaluation of LLM-based de-identification is significant. It represents a solid contribution that should influence future research in this area. The proposed CIRE model can have a real positive impact if it is further refined in future work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AToken: A Unified Tokenizer for Vision
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14476v1)
- **Authors**: Jiasen Lu, Liangchen Song, Mingze Xu, Byeongjoo Ahn, Yanjun Wang, Chen Chen, Afshin Dehghan, Yinfei Yang
- **Abstract**: We present AToken, the first unified visual tokenizer that achieves both high-fidelity reconstruction and semantic understanding across images, videos, and 3D assets. Unlike existing tokenizers that specialize in either reconstruction or understanding for single modalities, AToken encodes these diverse visual inputs into a shared 4D latent space, unifying both tasks and modalities in a single framework. Specifically, we introduce a pure transformer architecture with 4D rotary position embeddings to process visual inputs of arbitrary resolutions and temporal durations. To ensure stable training, we introduce an adversarial-free training objective that combines perceptual and Gram matrix losses, achieving state-of-the-art reconstruction quality. By employing a progressive training curriculum, AToken gradually expands from single images, videos, and 3D, and supports both continuous and discrete latent tokens. AToken achieves 0.21 rFID with 82.2% ImageNet accuracy for images, 3.01 rFVD with 32.6% MSRVTT retrieval for videos, and 28.19 PSNR with 90.9% classification accuracy for 3D. In downstream applications, AToken enables both visual generation tasks (e.g., image generation with continuous and discrete tokens, text-to-video generation, image-to-3D synthesis) and understanding tasks (e.g., multimodal LLMs), achieving competitive performance across all benchmarks. These results shed light on the next-generation multimodal AI systems built upon unified visual tokenization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ATOKEN: A Unified Tokenizer for Vision":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ATOKEN, a novel unified visual tokenizer designed to achieve high-fidelity reconstruction and semantic understanding across diverse visual modalities, including images, videos, and 3D assets. Unlike existing tokenizers that specialize in either reconstruction or understanding for a single modality, ATOKEN employs a shared 4D latent space to encode these diverse inputs. It utilizes a pure transformer architecture with 4D rotary position embeddings to process visual inputs of varying resolutions and temporal durations.  The training process incorporates an adversarial-free objective combining perceptual and Gram matrix losses for stable training and optimal reconstruction quality. ATOKEN further employs a progressive training curriculum, gradually expanding its capabilities from single images to videos and 3D assets, supporting both continuous and discrete latent tokens. Experiments demonstrate ATOKEN's effectiveness across several benchmarks for generation and understanding tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *unified* approach to visual tokenization.  While individual components, such as transformer architectures, VAE-based tokenizers, and adversarial-free training, are well-established, the integration into a single framework capable of handling images, videos, and 3D data *and* balancing reconstruction and understanding is a significant advance. The use of a sparse 4D representation is also a noteworthy architectural contribution, enabling the model to efficiently process data of varying dimensionality.
*   **Significance:** ATOKEN's significance stems from its potential to facilitate the development of next-generation multimodal AI systems.  A unified visual tokenizer can enable seamless knowledge transfer between different visual modalities, leading to more efficient and versatile AI models. The demonstration of competitive or state-of-the-art performance in various downstream tasks further supports its significance. However, the authors themselves acknowledge limitations in certain areas, such as precise image-to-3D generation and potential shortcomings in directly comparing video model architecture based solely from results retrieved from images in a large scale data set, implying the model relies heavily on image based modeling and thus requiring a dedicated video architecture remains essential.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Unified Framework:** The ability to handle images, videos, and 3D data within a single framework is a key strength.
    *   **High-Fidelity Reconstruction:** The adversarial-free training objective and progressive curriculum contribute to impressive reconstruction quality.
    *   **Semantic Understanding:** ATOKEN effectively balances reconstruction with semantic understanding, enabling its use in a wide range of downstream tasks.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive evaluations across various modalities and tasks, providing strong empirical support for its claims.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies, particularly the scaling and representation structure analyses, shed light on the model's behavior and design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The complexity of the architecture and training process could be a barrier to entry for some researchers.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Although native resolution handling is a positive, the overall computational cost for training such a multimodal tokenizer is likely considerable, potentially limiting accessibility to researchers with limited resources. The paper does include a detailed account in regards to required costs, however further optimization such as an algorithmically simple way to remove padding and packing would benefit the field.
    *   **Uneven Performance:** While generally strong, performance on some tasks (e.g., more complex tasks such as 3D reconstruction) is not always state-of-the-art and could benefit from further improvements. Also as acknowledged in the paper, directly comparing video model architecture from solely retrieved results on images can be misleading.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Discrete Tokens:** The use of discrete tokens is presented as an optional addition, and its full potential is not thoroughly explored.

*   **Impact:** ATOKEN's impact on the field will depend on its adoption by other researchers and developers. If the unified visual tokenizer becomes a widely used foundation for multimodal AI systems, its impact could be significant. The paper will likely inspire further research into unified representations for visual data and more efficient and stable training methods for transformer-based tokenizers.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a highly novel and significant contribution to the field of visual tokenization. While the individual components are not entirely new, their integration into a unified framework capable of handling diverse modalities and tasks is a substantial achievement. The strengths of the paper, including its comprehensive evaluation and ablation studies, outweigh its weaknesses. The limitations acknowledged by the authors show a critical understanding of the nuances to model design and areas where improvement is possible. Although there is room for improvement in certain areas, the potential impact of ATOKEN on the development of multimodal AI systems is significant.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Ticket-Bench: A Kickoff for Multilingual and Regionalized Agent Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14477v1)
- **Authors**: Thales Sales Almeida, João Guilherme Alves Santos, Thiago Laitz, Giovana Kerche Bonás
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as task-oriented agents, where success depends on their ability to generate accurate function calls under realistic, multilingual conditions. However, existing agent evaluations largely overlook cultural and linguistic diversity, often relying on monolingual or naively translated benchmarks. We introduce Ticket-Bench, a benchmark for multilingual agent evaluation in task-oriented scenarios. Ticket-Bench simulates the domain of soccer ticket purchases across six major languages: Portuguese, English, Spanish, German, Italian, and French. Using localized teams, cities, and user profiles to provide a higher level of realism. We evaluate a wide range of commercial and open-source LLMs, measuring function-calling accuracy and consistency across languages. Results show that reasoning-oriented models (e.g., GPT-5, Qwen3-235B) dominate performance but still exhibit notable cross-lingual disparities. These findings underscore the need for culturally aware, multilingual benchmarks to guide the development of robust LLM agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper "Ticket-Bench: A Kickoff for Multilingual and Regionalized Agent Evaluation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Ticket-Bench, a novel benchmark for evaluating the function-calling capabilities of Large Language Model (LLM) agents in multilingual, task-oriented scenarios. Ticket-Bench simulates soccer ticket purchases across six major languages (Portuguese, English, Spanish, German, Italian, and French), incorporating localized teams, cities, and user profiles to enhance realism.  The authors evaluate a range of commercial and open-source LLMs using Ticket-Bench, measuring function-calling accuracy and consistency across languages. Their results highlight significant cross-lingual disparities, even in high-performing models, underscoring the need for culturally aware, multilingual benchmarks to improve LLM agent robustness.  The benchmark provides an LLM-free, programmatic evaluation, and introduces a "pass^3" consistency metric that rewards reliable solutions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on *multilingual and regionalized* evaluation of LLM agents' function-calling abilities within a practical domain.  Existing benchmarks are often monolingual or rely on naive translations, missing the nuances of cultural context and localized entities. Ticket-Bench's deliberate localization of city names, team names, and user profiles represents a significant step forward. The introduction of the pass^3 metric to measure consistency is also a good addition.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of the work stems from the increasing deployment of LLMs as task-oriented agents in multilingual environments. Identifying and addressing cross-lingual disparities is crucial for ensuring fair and reliable performance across different user groups. The paper's findings demonstrate that current LLMs still struggle to maintain consistent accuracy across languages, even when performing the same underlying task. This highlights a critical gap in LLM development and motivates further research in this area. The paper also provides a valuable resource (Ticket-Bench) that can be used to evaluate and improve future LLM agents.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined benchmark with a clear task and evaluation metrics.
    *   Rigorous methodology involving careful localization and controlled experimental setup.
    *   LLM-free evaluation.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of a diverse set of LLMs.
    *   Identifies important cross-lingual performance gaps.
    *   Provides a publicly available benchmark for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is limited to a single domain (soccer ticket purchases). While this allows for controlled experiments, it remains to be seen whether the findings generalize to other domains.
    *   The reliance on a simulated environment, while useful for control, may not fully capture the complexities of real-world user interactions.
    *   Although 15% of the cases have no solution, it does not specify if the test includes cases with partial solutions.
    *   The number of runs (3) for each model is relatively small, which could limit the statistical significance of the results, although is justified by the authors.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   It establishes a new benchmark for evaluating multilingual LLM agents.
    *   It raises awareness of the importance of cultural context and localization in LLM development.
    *   It motivates the development of training methods and architectures that improve cross-lingual consistency.
    *   It provides a valuable resource for comparing the performance of different LLMs.

*   **Overall:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by highlighting the challenges of building multilingual and regionally aware LLM agents. Ticket-Bench provides a valuable tool for evaluating and improving future systems, and the paper's findings motivate further research in this important area.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed study on a relevant topic. The creation of Ticket-Bench is a significant contribution that addresses a gap in current evaluation methodologies.  While the evaluation is limited to a single domain and a simulated environment, the rigor and clarity of the methodology, as well as the importance of the findings, justify a high score. A higher score could be warranted with a more diverse set of tasks or real-world evaluations, but as a "kickoff," it is a very strong start.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Estimating Semantic Alphabet Size for LLM Uncertainty Quantification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14478v1)
- **Authors**: Lucas H. McCabe, Rimon Melamed, Thomas Hartvigsen, H. Howie Huang
- **Abstract**: Many black-box techniques for quantifying the uncertainty of large language models (LLMs) rely on repeated LLM sampling, which can be computationally expensive. Therefore, practical applicability demands reliable estimation from few samples. Semantic entropy (SE) is a popular sample-based uncertainty estimator with a discrete formulation attractive for the black-box setting. Recent extensions of semantic entropy exhibit improved LLM hallucination detection, but do so with less interpretable methods that admit additional hyperparameters. For this reason, we revisit the canonical discrete semantic entropy estimator, finding that it underestimates the "true" semantic entropy, as expected from theory. We propose a modified semantic alphabet size estimator, and illustrate that using it to adjust discrete semantic entropy for sample coverage results in more accurate semantic entropy estimation in our setting of interest. Furthermore, our proposed alphabet size estimator flags incorrect LLM responses as well or better than recent top-performing approaches, with the added benefit of remaining highly interpretable.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of uncertainty quantification (UQ) in large language models (LLMs), particularly in the black-box setting where access to internal model parameters is limited. The authors focus on semantic entropy (SE), a sample-based UQ technique. They demonstrate that the canonical discrete semantic entropy (DSE) estimator underestimates the "true" SE, especially with practical (small) sample sizes. To address this, they propose a modified semantic alphabet size estimator, inspired by population ecology, and use it to adjust DSE for sample coverage.  The paper empirically shows that this modified approach leads to more accurate SE estimation and improves the ability to identify incorrect LLM responses in question-answering tasks. The key advantage highlighted is the improved performance while maintaining high interpretability compared to more complex recent methods like Kernel Language Entropy (KLE) and Semantic Nearest Neighbor Entropy (SNNE).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its practical focus on improving a well-established UQ technique (semantic entropy) for black-box LLMs. While the individual components (e.g., coverage adjustment, alphabet size estimation) are not entirely new concepts, their combination and application within the context of LLM uncertainty quantification is a valuable contribution. Drawing inspiration from population ecology to refine alphabet size estimation for LLMs presents a moderately novel perspective. The hybrid semantic alphabet size estimator is incremental.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its focus on sample efficiency and interpretability in LLM UQ. Since obtaining numerous samples from LLMs can be computationally costly, a method that can effectively estimate uncertainty with fewer samples has real-world value. The interpretability aspect is crucial, because, it allows users to understand *why* a particular response is deemed uncertain, facilitating trust and debugging of the system. While the improvements in AUROC are not exceedingly large, achieving these improvements while maintaining interpretability and using a computationally simpler approach is a practical and relevant contribution. The paper adds evidence towards the suitability of alphabet size estimation as a standalone measure of uncertainty, a relatively interpretable quantity. The comparison with state-of-the-art methods (SNNE, KLE) is important for positioning the contribution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of underestimation in DSE and the limitations of more complex, less interpretable UQ methods.
    *   **Practical Focus:** The proposed solution is designed to be computationally efficient and applicable in black-box settings, addressing a key challenge in LLM deployment.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides empirical evidence across several LLMs and datasets to support the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Emphasis on Interpretability:**  A significant strength is the paper's prioritization of interpretable methods, which is important for building user trust and understanding model behavior.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** The improvement in performance, while consistent, is incremental. The paper could strengthen its argument by demonstrating more substantial gains in specific scenarios or by providing a more detailed analysis of the situations where the proposed method performs best. The improvement on the *canonical* discrete semantic entropy is not substantial.
    *   **Limited Datasets:** While four datasets are used, expanding the evaluation to include more diverse and challenging datasets would further strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Reliance on SE*:** The reliance on white-box semantic entropy with 100 samples to approximate ground truth is not fully justified, and there may be ways to assess the proposed estimator's performance without this assumption.

**Score: 6.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a meaningful contribution to the field of LLM uncertainty quantification by addressing a practical limitation of a widely used technique (DSE) and offering a more accurate and interpretable solution. The approach, inspired by population ecology, is interesting and the empirical results provide evidence of the method's effectiveness. However, the improvements are incremental, and there is room for improvement in the experimental validation. The paper's focus on interpretability is a notable strength. Overall, it provides a valuable step forward in the development of practical and trustworthy LLM uncertainty quantification techniques, deserving of a moderate score.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### An LLM-based multi-agent framework for agile effort estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14483v1)
- **Authors**: Thanh-Long Bui, Hoa Khanh Dam, Rashina Hoda
- **Abstract**: Effort estimation is a crucial activity in agile software development, where teams collaboratively review, discuss, and estimate the effort required to complete user stories in a product backlog. Current practices in agile effort estimation heavily rely on subjective assessments, leading to inaccuracies and inconsistencies in the estimates. While recent machine learning-based methods show promising accuracy, they cannot explain or justify their estimates and lack the capability to interact with human team members. Our paper fills this significant gap by leveraging the powerful capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). We propose a novel LLM-based multi-agent framework for agile estimation that not only can produce estimates, but also can coordinate, communicate and discuss with human developers and other agents to reach a consensus. Evaluation results on a real-life dataset show that our approach outperforms state-of-the-art techniques across all evaluation metrics in the majority of the cases. Our human study with software development practitioners also demonstrates an overwhelmingly positive experience in collaborating with our agents in agile effort estimation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SEEAgent, a novel LLM-based multi-agent framework designed to enhance agile software effort estimation. SEEAgent not only provides estimates for user stories but also communicates, coordinates, and discusses with human developers and other agents to reach a consensus, mirroring the collaborative nature of agile practices. The framework addresses limitations of existing AI-based estimation approaches, such as their "black box" nature and inability to participate in discussions.  SEEAgent incorporates prompt engineering and fine-tuning to enrich agents with project-specific and expertise-specific knowledge, addressing the LLM hallucination problem. Evaluation on a real-life dataset demonstrates that SEEAgent outperforms state-of-the-art techniques in terms of estimation accuracy.  A human study further validates the framework by showing a positive user experience and demonstrating that collaboration with the agents helps teams reach consensus and uncover hidden complexities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in integrating LLMs into a multi-agent system for agile effort estimation, specifically enabling communication and collaboration within the estimation process. While other studies use LLMs for software engineering tasks or AI for estimation, the combination of LLM-powered agents working *collaboratively* with humans *during* the estimation phase is a significant departure from prior work. Existing AI estimation tools are generally passive predictors, whereas SEEAgent actively participates in the estimation process.  The design incorporating project-specific knowledge and role-play through fine-tuning and prompt engineering further increases the novelty. This is a better application of LLMs to improve estimations while integrating well in agile methodologies.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and persistent problem in agile software development: inaccurate and inconsistent effort estimates. The significance stems from the potential to improve estimation accuracy, reduce bias, facilitate team conversation, and leverage cross-disciplinary knowledge, thereby enhancing project planning and execution. The human study provides initial evidence suggesting that SEEAgent has the potential to be trusted and adopted by developers, addressing a major hurdle for AI-driven estimation tools. Also by including explanations it removes the "black box" model of most of the AI models for estimation.

* **Strengths:**
    *  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of current estimation practices and AI-based solutions.
    *  The SEEAgent architecture is well-defined, with clear descriptions of each component and its role.
    *  The evaluation includes both quantitative (accuracy comparison) and qualitative (human study) components, providing a comprehensive assessment of the framework.
    *  The use of parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) to adapt LLMs for project-specific knowledge demonstrates resource-consciousness.
    *  The human study findings offer valuable insights into the benefits, challenges, and design improvements needed for effective human-AI collaboration in agile estimation.
    * The model is able to outperform current SOTA models in most cases using a better methodology.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *  The sample size of the human study (12 participants) is relatively small, limiting the generalizability of the findings.
    *  The human study focuses on a single, simplified e-commerce software project. Future work should involve a wider range of projects with varying complexities and development contexts.
    *  While the paper compares against SOTA *estimation* techniques, it does not compare against other *collaborative* AI systems or multi-agent systems in software engineering that aren't directly focused on effort estimation. A comparison to a broader class of collaborative tools would further highlight the unique contributions of SEEAgent.
    *  The paper could elaborate on the specific prompt engineering techniques employed to mitigate LLM hallucinations and ensure the agents provide coherent and relevant justifications for their estimates.
    * The lack of open sourced code will hinder other researchers from using and building upon SEEAgent.

* **Potential Influence:**  If proven effective and scalable in real-world settings, SEEAgent has the potential to influence the way agile teams approach effort estimation, moving away from purely subjective assessments towards a more data-driven and collaborative process. It could encourage the development of similar LLM-based multi-agent systems for other software engineering tasks, fostering closer human-AI collaboration.

* **Justification of Score:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by addressing a well-known problem (inaccurate agile estimation) with a novel solution (LLM-based multi-agent collaboration). The comprehensive evaluation, including both accuracy comparisons and a human study, strengthens the paper's claims.  While the limitations related to sample size and project context are valid, they do not detract from the overall novelty and significance of the approach.  However, considering the limited sample size of the human study and the narrow scope of the projects used, as well as the fact that the code isn't open sourced,  a score of 7 is given. This recognizes the strong potential and initial validation, but acknowledges the need for further research and real-world deployment to fully realize the impact of SEEAgent.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Introducing OmniGEC: A Silver Multilingual Dataset for Grammatical Error Correction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14504v1)
- **Authors**: Roman Kovalchuk, Mariana Romanyshyn, Petro Ivaniuk
- **Abstract**: In this paper, we introduce OmniGEC, a collection of multilingual silver-standard datasets for the task of Grammatical Error Correction (GEC), covering eleven languages: Czech, English, Estonian, German, Greek, Icelandic, Italian, Latvian, Slovene, Swedish, and Ukrainian. These datasets facilitate the development of multilingual GEC solutions and help bridge the data gap in adapting English GEC solutions to multilingual GEC. The texts in the datasets originate from three sources: Wikipedia edits for the eleven target languages, subreddits from Reddit in the eleven target languages, and the Ukrainian-only UberText 2.0 social media corpus. While Wikipedia edits were derived from human-made corrections, the Reddit and UberText 2.0 data were automatically corrected with the GPT-4o-mini model. The quality of the corrections in the datasets was evaluated both automatically and manually. Finally, we fine-tune two open-source large language models - Aya-Expanse (8B) and Gemma-3 (12B) - on the multilingual OmniGEC corpora and achieve state-of-the-art (SOTA) results for paragraph-level multilingual GEC. The dataset collection and the best-performing models are available on Hugging Face.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces OmniGEC, a silver-standard multilingual dataset for grammatical error correction (GEC) across eleven languages. The dataset is composed of Wikipedia edits, Reddit subreddits, and the Ukrainian UberText 2.0 corpus, with corrections generated using GPT-4o-mini. The authors evaluate the quality of the dataset using automated metrics and human evaluation (on the Ukrainian portion only). They then fine-tune Aya-Expanse (8B) and Gemma-3 (12B) on OmniGEC and show state-of-the-art results for paragraph-level multilingual GEC in the MultiGEC-2025 competition setting. Ablation studies examine the impact of different data sources on model performance.

**Novelty and Significance Evaluation:**

While the paper addresses a relevant problem (lack of multilingual GEC data) and presents empirical results, the overall novelty is somewhat limited. The core contributions fall into the following categories:

*   **Dataset Construction (OmniGEC):** This is perhaps the most significant contribution. Compiling and making available a multilingual GEC dataset is a valuable service to the community. However, the methods used (GPT-4o-mini for synthetic correction) are not particularly novel in themselves, and reliance on automatic correction raises concerns about data quality. The human evaluation is limited to only one language (Ukrainian), so the quality of synthetically generated data cannot be fully assessed for all languages. The approach for corrections isn't new either, it's a variation of a known approach.
*   **Model Fine-tuning (Aya-Expanse, Gemma-3):** Fine-tuning existing LLMs on a new dataset is a standard practice. The application of Aya-Expanse and Gemma-3 to this specific task and the resulting SOTA results within the MultiGEC-2025 competition add some value, but do not constitute a major breakthrough. Other teams in MultiGEC also leveraged the same models, limiting the impact of any findings.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different data sources. However, they are limited in scope and do not explore a wide range of data combinations. Also, one aspect is missing: more research on data quality vs quantity with UberText-GEC, which includes nearly 10 times more language data than Reddit-MultiGEC for the Ukrainian case study.
*   **UberText-GEC subset usage**: UberText-GEC, which is a part of OmniGEC is only included in the ablation study to a minor extent. UberText-GEC is expected to blaze trail for SOTA GEC model for the Ukrainian language, so the lack of more extensive experimentation with this corpus is a limitation.
The paper clearly presents the experiments and findings but could provide a more critical analysis of the limitations of using automatically generated corrections and the generalizability of the Ukrainian human evaluation to other languages. A more thorough error analysis beyond the simple grade distribution would be beneficial. The discussion concerning data bias and safety issues on the content of Reddit is pertinent. The analysis of per-language LoRA adapters and if the data can be shared between languages would be interesting. While there are some novel elements, the dependence on existing techniques and a limited evaluation of the results' broader significance make the novelty incremental rather than transformative.

**Justification:**

The paper's value lies primarily in providing a new resource (OmniGEC) to the GEC research community, particularly for languages other than English. The fine-tuning experiments and ablation studies offer some insights into the effectiveness of different data sources, but the methodological novelty is limited. The dependence of automatically translated and corrected data introduces concerns about data quality, as the authors themselves mention overcorrection as a challenge. The relatively short conclusion also suggests that not all of the potential directions have been explored by the authors. A significant limitation is the exclusive reliance on Ukrainian human evaluation, precluding a full quality assessment on other languages. Although OmniGEC is a helpful dataset, the limitations concerning its data quality and the limited exploration of the models' potential lead to a lower overall novelty score.

### DeKeyNLU: Enhancing Natural Language to SQL Generation through Task Decomposition and Keyword Extraction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14507v1)
- **Authors**: Jian Chen, Zhenyan Chen, Xuming Hu, Peilin Zhou, Yining Hua, Han Fang, Cissy Hing Yee Choy, Xinmei Ke, Jingfeng Luo, Zixuan Yuan
- **Abstract**: Natural Language to SQL (NL2SQL) provides a new model-centric paradigm that simplifies database access for non-technical users by converting natural language queries into SQL commands. Recent advancements, particularly those integrating Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, have made significant strides in enhancing NL2SQL performance. However, challenges such as inaccurate task decomposition and keyword extraction by LLMs remain major bottlenecks, often leading to errors in SQL generation. While existing datasets aim to mitigate these issues by fine-tuning models, they struggle with over-fragmentation of tasks and lack of domain-specific keyword annotations, limiting their effectiveness. To address these limitations, we present DeKeyNLU, a novel dataset which contains 1,500 meticulously annotated QA pairs aimed at refining task decomposition and enhancing keyword extraction precision for the RAG pipeline. Fine-tuned with DeKeyNLU, we propose DeKeySQL, a RAG-based NL2SQL pipeline that employs three distinct modules for user question understanding, entity retrieval, and generation to improve SQL generation accuracy. We benchmarked multiple model configurations within DeKeySQL RAG pipeline. Experimental results demonstrate that fine-tuning with DeKeyNLU significantly improves SQL generation accuracy on both BIRD (62.31% to 69.10%) and Spider (84.2% to 88.7%) dev datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the DeKeyNLU paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DeKeyNLU, a new dataset of 1,500 annotated question-SQL pairs, specifically designed to enhance Natural Language Understanding (NLU) in Natural Language to SQL (NL2SQL) systems. The dataset focuses on improving task decomposition and keyword extraction, which the authors identify as key bottlenecks in current NL2SQL approaches. They also introduce DeKeySQL, a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG)-based NL2SQL pipeline that leverages DeKeyNLU for fine-tuning. Experiments show that fine-tuning with DeKeyNLU significantly improves SQL generation accuracy on both the BIRD and Spider datasets. The paper also analyzes the contributions of different modules within the DeKeySQL pipeline and the impact of model size on different NLU tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty of this work lies primarily in the creation of the DeKeyNLU dataset, which is explicitly annotated for task decomposition and keyword extraction in the context of NL2SQL. While other datasets exist for NL2SQL, the focus on these specific NLU aspects is a valuable contribution. The DeKeySQL pipeline itself doesn't present a radically new architecture, but its emphasis on leveraging the DeKeyNLU dataset for fine-tuning is a significant aspect. The error analysis, too, although limited, contributes to understanding the shortcomings of existing approaches and points to opportunities for future research.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is two-fold:

    1.  **Dataset contribution:** The DeKeyNLU dataset fills a gap in the NL2SQL landscape by providing annotations that directly address known challenges in task decomposition and keyword extraction. This enables more targeted fine-tuning and evaluation of NL2SQL models.
    2.  **Performance improvement:** The experimental results clearly demonstrate the effectiveness of fine-tuning with DeKeyNLU in improving SQL generation accuracy. This is a practical demonstration of the value of the dataset.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies and addresses a specific problem (limitations of NLU in NL2SQL).
    *   The DeKeyNLU dataset is a valuable resource for the community.
    *   Experiments are well-designed and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   The error analysis provides insights into the types of errors that occur in NL2SQL systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The dataset size (1,500 samples) is relatively small, which might limit the generalizability of the results. The authors acknowledge this as a limitation. It isn't clear that this dataset offers enough training samples for complex models in the long run.
    *   While the DeKeySQL pipeline demonstrates improvements, the architecture itself is not fundamentally novel. It mostly leverages existing RAG and fine-tuning techniques.
    *   The error analysis, while helpful, is based on a relatively small sample size (20% of failed cases).
    *   The comparison of different LLMs could be more thorough. While they compare model sizes, further analysis into the underlying pretraining of each model and how the different architectures affect performance would be worthwhile.

*   **Potential Influence:** The DeKeyNLU dataset has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating NLU components of NL2SQL systems. The insights from the error analysis can also guide future research directions. The improvements achieved by fine-tuning with DeKeyNLU could lead to more accurate and robust NL2SQL models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed contribution to the field of NL2SQL. The DeKeyNLU dataset addresses a clearly identified need and demonstrates practical improvements in SQL generation accuracy. However, the relatively small dataset size and the incremental nature of the DeKeySQL architecture limit its overall impact. While a solid contribution, it doesn't represent a paradigm shift or a major breakthrough. The rigorous methodology warrants a high-end score.

### Event-LAB: Towards Standardized Evaluation of Neuromorphic Localization Methods
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14516v1)
- **Authors**: Adam D. Hines, Alejandro Fontan, Michael Milford, Tobias Fischer
- **Abstract**: Event-based localization research and datasets are a rapidly growing area of interest, with a tenfold increase in the cumulative total number of published papers on this topic over the past 10 years. Whilst the rapid expansion in the field is exciting, it brings with it an associated challenge: a growth in the variety of required code and package dependencies as well as data formats, making comparisons difficult and cumbersome for researchers to implement reliably. To address this challenge, we present Event-LAB: a new and unified framework for running several event-based localization methodologies across multiple datasets. Event-LAB is implemented using the Pixi package and dependency manager, that enables a single command-line installation and invocation for combinations of localization methods and datasets. To demonstrate the capabilities of the framework, we implement two common event-based localization pipelines: Visual Place Recognition (VPR) and Simultaneous Localization and Mapping (SLAM). We demonstrate the ability of the framework to systematically visualize and analyze the results of multiple methods and datasets, revealing key insights such as the association of parameters that control event collection counts and window sizes for frame generation to large variations in performance. The results and analysis demonstrate the importance of fairly comparing methodologies with consistent event image generation parameters. Our Event-LAB framework provides this ability for the research community, by contributing a streamlined workflow for easily setting up multiple conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Event-LAB, a unified framework designed to standardize the evaluation of neuromorphic localization methods, specifically those using event-based cameras. Event-LAB aims to address the growing complexity in the field, where diverse codebases, package dependencies, and data formats hinder fair comparisons between different localization approaches. The framework utilizes the Pixi package manager for easy installation and management, allowing researchers to run several event-based localization pipelines (VPR and SLAM) across multiple datasets with a single command-line invocation. The authors demonstrate the framework's capabilities by implementing common event-based pipelines, analyzing the impact of various parameters (like event count and window size) on performance, and highlighting the importance of consistent event image generation for fair comparisons.  They also present a "winner-takes-all" scheme to improve matching across different time windows. The code is designed for easy community contributions to make this tool even more robust.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its unified framework approach. While individual components like event-based VPR and SLAM methods, datasets, and some benchmarking tools already exist, Event-LAB brings them together under a common, easy-to-use umbrella. The adoption of Pixi for dependency management and environment creation significantly streamlines the setup process and enhances reproducibility, which is a substantial improvement over the often complex and time-consuming configuration required for many neuromorphic vision projects. The "winner-takes-all" strategy for time window matching is another potentially valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of Event-LAB stems from its potential to accelerate research in neuromorphic localization. By providing a standardized evaluation platform, it enables researchers to:

    *   **Fairly compare different methods:**  Eliminating inconsistencies in data formatting, preprocessing, and evaluation protocols.
    *   **Reproduce results more easily:** The Pixi-based environment guarantees consistency across different machines and research groups.
    *   **Rapidly prototype and test new algorithms:** The framework simplifies the process of integrating new methods and datasets.
    *   **Systematically analyze performance:** Event-LAB facilitates the investigation of how different parameters (event counts, window sizes, frame generation techniques) affect the performance of various localization approaches. This allows for a much deeper understanding of trade-offs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Ease of Use:** The single command-line invocation is a major advantage.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Pixi guarantees bit-for-bit reproducibility.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Includes VPR and SLAM pipelines, multiple datasets, and metrics.
    *   **Community Driven:**  Designed for easy contribution and expansion.
    *   **Practical Demonstration:** The paper provides experimental results and analyses highlighting the framework's capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope (Initially):** While extensible, the framework currently implements a relatively small number of baseline methods and datasets (as of the writing of the paper).  Its real impact will depend on community adoption and contribution of more methods and datasets.
    *   **Dependent on Community Adoption:** The value of Event-LAB is directly tied to the community's willingness to contribute. Without this, it will remain a useful, but limited, tool.
    *   **Image Reconstruction Bottleneck:** The image reconstruction process can be quite computationally intensive and can become a significant bottleneck. While they parallelize image generation, the impact is still substantial.

**Justification of Score:**

Event-LAB is a valuable contribution to the neuromorphic localization research community. It directly addresses a critical need for standardization and reproducibility in a rapidly growing field. The strengths of the framework (ease of use, reproducibility, comprehensive evaluation, community driven design) outweigh the weaknesses. While the initial scope is limited, the framework is designed to be extensible. The success of the framework will rest on community adoption.

### BEACON: Behavioral Malware Classification with Large Language Model Embeddings and Deep Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14519v1)
- **Authors**: Wadduwage Shanika Perera, Haodi Jiang
- **Abstract**: Malware is becoming increasingly complex and widespread, making it essential to develop more effective and timely detection methods. Traditional static analysis often fails to defend against modern threats that employ code obfuscation, polymorphism, and other evasion techniques. In contrast, behavioral malware detection, which monitors runtime activities, provides a more reliable and context-aware solution. In this work, we propose BEACON, a novel deep learning framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) to generate dense, contextual embeddings from raw sandbox-generated behavior reports. These embeddings capture semantic and structural patterns of each sample and are processed by a one-dimensional convolutional neural network (1D CNN) for multi-class malware classification. Evaluated on the Avast-CTU Public CAPE Dataset, our framework consistently outperforms existing methods, highlighting the effectiveness of LLM-based behavioral embeddings and the overall design of BEACON for robust malware classification.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BEACON, a deep learning framework for malware classification. BEACON leverages large language models (LLMs), specifically Google's textembedding-gecko@003, to generate dense, contextual embeddings from raw sandbox-generated behavioral reports.  These embeddings, capturing semantic and structural patterns, are then fed into a one-dimensional convolutional neural network (1D CNN) for multi-class malware classification. Evaluated on the Avast-CTU Public CAPE Dataset, BEACON demonstrates superior performance compared to existing methods, highlighting the effectiveness of LLM-based behavioral embeddings and the overall framework design. The paper emphasizes the automated feature extraction enabled by LLMs, reducing the need for manual feature engineering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of a modern LLM (Gemini's textembedding-gecko@003) for extracting embeddings directly from raw, unstructured behavioral reports and using these embeddings in conjunction with a 1D CNN. While LLMs have been used in cybersecurity before, this specific combination for *behavioral* malware classification, without extensive pre-processing or manual feature engineering, appears to be a valuable contribution. The ablation study comparing BEACON to prior MLP models shows a clear jump in performance indicating a benefit specific to how this LLM-CNN framework operates.

*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from the potential to improve malware detection accuracy and automation. By using LLMs to automatically generate informative embeddings, the need for expert-driven feature engineering is reduced, making the detection process more scalable and adaptable to evolving malware threats.  The use of raw behavioral data provides a more robust method compared to methods that rely on static characteristics.  The comparative results against existing approaches on a benchmark dataset strengthen the practical relevance of the work. However, there is no evaluation of the *speed* or scalability of using the full LLM embedding as part of the detection pipeline which is important for practical implementations. The framework might be impractical for real-time analysis given the computational overhead of LLM embeddings and needs to be evaluated.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with appropriate experimental setup and baselines.
    *   Thorough evaluation using standard metrics and a publicly available dataset.
    *   Demonstrated superior performance compared to existing methods.
    *   Highlights the potential of LLMs for automated feature extraction in malware analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited discussion of computational cost and real-time feasibility.  The paper does not address the practical implications of using LLMs for real-time malware detection where response speed is crucial.
    *   The paper could have explored a variety of LLM models, and perhaps compared embedding strategies for optimizing performance and feature extraction. There is no ablation for specific embedding strategies.
    *   Lack of explainability analysis. The paper would have benefited from an analysis of *what* the LLM embeddings are capturing regarding malware behavior to provide additional insight.
    *   Lack of direct comparison with other LLM-based approaches. While the paper compares against baselines using traditional methods and prior work on the same dataset, a direct comparison with another LLM based system would be valuable.
    *   The analysis focuses on *family* classification of malware which, while valuable, is a somewhat coarse-grained task. It would be useful to see if BEACON can distinguish between *variants* or even detect zero-day threats based on behavioral embeddings.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in malware analysis, particularly in exploring the use of LLMs for automated feature extraction and contextual understanding of malware behavior. The idea of raw behavioral reports being fed into LLMs provides a new direction for the community.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper presents a solid contribution to the field with a clear methodology and promising results. The use of LLMs in this specific context is a step forward. However, the limitations regarding computational cost, the single LLM architecture assessed, and the lack of an analysis of what the embeddings contain and its limited evaluation to malware *families*, bring down the overall score.
