### Delta Knowledge Distillation for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14526v1)
- **Authors**: Yihan Cao, Yanbin Kang, Zhengming Xing, Ruijie Jiang
- **Abstract**: Knowledge distillation (KD) is a widely adopted approach for compressing large neural networks by transferring knowledge from a large teacher model to a smaller student model. In the context of large language models, token level KD, typically minimizing the KL divergence between student output distribution and teacher output distribution, has shown strong empirical performance. However, prior work assumes student output distribution and teacher output distribution share the same optimal representation space, a premise that may not hold in many cases. To solve this problem, we propose Delta Knowledge Distillation (Delta-KD), a novel extension of token level KD that encourages the student to approximate an optimal representation space by explicitly preserving the distributional shift Delta introduced during the teacher's supervised finetuning (SFT). Empirical results on ROUGE metrics demonstrate that Delta KD substantially improves student performance while preserving more of the teacher's knowledge.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Delta Knowledge Distillation paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes Delta-KD, a novel knowledge distillation (KD) approach specifically designed for large language models (LLMs). Delta-KD addresses limitations of traditional token-level KD, which often assumes a shared representation space between teacher and student models, and overlooks the knowledge shift occurring during the teacher's supervised finetuning (SFT) process.  Instead of directly aligning the student's output distribution with the finetuned teacher's output, Delta-KD encourages the student to approximate an "optimal" representation space by explicitly preserving and learning the distributional shift (delta) introduced during the teacher's SFT. The paper presents token-level and sequence-level distillation experiments on instruction tuning and reasoning datasets, demonstrating that Delta-KD consistently outperforms standard KD baselines like Forward KL, Reverse KL, and SeqKD with notable improvements on ROUGE scores. The paper also introduces a Parallelogram Loss as a way to combine information from multiple teacher variants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of capturing and distilling the *change* in knowledge (the delta) acquired during finetuning, rather than the absolute knowledge state of the finetuned teacher, is reasonably novel and makes intuitive sense, especially when compressing LLMs.  Existing approaches focus on mimicking the final output, while this one explicitly tries to learn the *process* of finetuning. The parallelogram loss is an interesting, though less essential, extension of the core idea.

* **Significance:** The paper tackles a very relevant problem: compressing large language models for deployment. Knowledge distillation is a crucial technique in this area. If Delta-KD truly offers a consistent and significant performance improvement over existing methods, it represents a tangible step forward for more accessible LLMs.  The experimental results presented appear to support this, consistently outperforming baselines on tested tasks. The focus on preserving more of the teacher's knowledge, as measured by ROUGE scores, further strengthens the significance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing KD methods, particularly for LLMs.
    * **Intuitive Approach:** The concept of distilling the "delta" in knowledge is well-motivated and explained.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experimental setup seems thorough, with comparisons to strong baselines on relevant datasets. The gains in ROUGE scores are meaningful and consistent.
    * **Practical Considerations:**  The implementation details, such as the ZeroMQ based training architecture and data transfer in FP16 format address practical scalability issues during knowledge distillation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Datasets and Models:** While the paper mentions plans to extend experimentation, currently results are largely confined to Qwen2.5 models and two datasets.  Demonstrating effectiveness across diverse models (LLaMA-3, others) and datasets is crucial for establishing generalizability.
    * **Dependency on ROUGE Scores:** The evaluation primarily relies on ROUGE scores, which have known limitations and may not fully capture the nuances of language understanding and generation.  Additional metrics, particularly human evaluations or stronger automated LLM evaluation metrics, would bolster the findings.
    * **Parameter Sensitivity Discussion:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the sensitivity of the 'a' parameter and its impact on performance.  What is the sensitivity of performance to different 'a' values? How does the optimal 'a' vary across tasks?
    * **Absence of Ablation Studies:**  Ablating various components of the Delta-KD approach would help isolate the contribution of individual elements to the final results. For instance, ablating the distributional shift and only learning from one teacher model without computing ∆.

* **Potential Influence:**  Delta-KD could potentially influence future research in knowledge distillation, particularly for LLMs. It provides a new perspective on how to transfer knowledge more effectively by focusing on learning distributional shifts rather than directly mimicking the teacher's output. Other researchers could build upon this idea by exploring different ways to capture and distill the "delta" in knowledge, or by combining it with other advanced KD techniques.

* **Rigor**: The writing could be improved by adding concrete numbers that are omitted. For example, the phrase "notable improvements on ROUGE scores" should be accompanied by numbers for a reader to evaluate whether or not these improvements are indeed significant.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:** Delta-KD presents a novel and promising approach to knowledge distillation for LLMs, addressing some key limitations of existing techniques. The experimental results are encouraging, demonstrating consistent improvements over strong baselines. However, the limitations in the scope of datasets/models and primary reliance on ROUGE scores, coupled with the lack of comprehensive ablation and parameter sensitivity analysis, prevent the paper from reaching a higher score. Future work that addresses these limitations and demonstrates broader applicability would significantly increase its impact. While the idea is sound, the thoroughness and rigor of experimental analysis need to be bolstered before the contribution can be considered a game changer.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Catch Me If You Can? Not Yet: LLMs Still Struggle to Imitate the Implicit Writing Styles of Everyday Authors
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14543v1)
- **Authors**: Zhengxiang Wang, Nafis Irtiza Tripto, Solha Park, Zhenzhen Li, Jiawei Zhou
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) become increasingly integrated into personal writing tools, a critical question arises: can LLMs faithfully imitate an individual's writing style from just a few examples? Personal style is often subtle and implicit, making it difficult to specify through prompts yet essential for user-aligned generation. This work presents a comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art LLMs' ability to mimic personal writing styles via in-context learning from a small number of user-authored samples. We introduce an ensemble of complementary metrics-including authorship attribution, authorship verification, style matching, and AI detection-to robustly assess style imitation. Our evaluation spans over 40000 generations per model across domains such as news, email, forums, and blogs, covering writing samples from more than 400 real-world authors. Results show that while LLMs can approximate user styles in structured formats like news and email, they struggle with nuanced, informal writing in blogs and forums. Further analysis on various prompting strategies such as number of demonstrations reveal key limitations in effective personalization. Our findings highlight a fundamental gap in personalized LLM adaptation and the need for improved techniques to support implicit, style-consistent generation. To aid future research and for reproducibility, we open-source our data and code.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of large language models (LLMs) to mimic the implicit writing styles of everyday authors using few-shot in-context learning.  It introduces a comprehensive evaluation framework composed of authorship attribution, authorship verification, style matching, and AI detection to assess stylistic fidelity.  The study analyzes LLM-generated text across various domains like news articles, emails, forums, and blogs, using writing samples from over 400 real-world authors. The results indicate that while LLMs can partially emulate styles in structured formats like news and email, they struggle with the nuances of informal writing styles in blogs and forums.  The paper also explores the impact of varying the number of demonstrations and using different prompting strategies, concluding that there's a fundamental gap in personalized LLM adaptation, highlighting the need for improved techniques for style-consistent generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:* The paper makes a valuable contribution by moving beyond the common focus of mimicking the style of well-known figures or fictional characters and instead examining LLMs' ability to emulate the style of everyday users. This is a crucial direction, as the integration of LLMs into personal writing tools necessitates this capability. The comprehensive evaluation framework is also novel, combining traditional authorship analysis techniques with AI detection methods.

*Significance:* The findings are significant because they highlight a key limitation in the current generation of LLMs. While LLMs excel at fluency and content generation, accurately capturing subtle, individual writing styles remains a challenge. This is directly relevant to the development of truly personalized writing assistance tools. The study rigorously assesses the state-of-the-art LLMs and identifies the weaknesses in informal writing in forums and blogs. This paper's open-sourced dataset and code make the results reproducible and give a concrete way for follow-up research.

*Strengths:*
*   The use of a multi-faceted evaluation framework is a significant strength. Relying on just one metric would provide an incomplete picture of stylistic fidelity.
*   The large-scale evaluation across diverse domains and authors adds considerable weight to the findings.
*   The ablation studies on prompting strategies offer valuable insights into the limitations of current personalization techniques.
*   The open-sourcing of the data and code promotes reproducibility and future research.
*   The study's connection to real-world usage scenarios makes the research highly relevant.

*Weaknesses:*
*   The study focuses primarily on English-language datasets, limiting the generalizability of the findings to other languages and cultures.
*   The analysis is constrained to within-genre personalization, neglecting the complexities of cross-genre style transfer.
*   The study relies on automated metrics for authorship analysis, which may not fully capture human perceptions of style. The lack of a human evaluation is a notable omission.

*Potential Impact:*
The paper will likely influence future research in personalized LLM-based writing assistance. It clearly defines the problem, provides a robust evaluation methodology, and identifies specific areas for improvement. The study could spur the development of new prompting techniques, fine-tuning methods, or model architectures better suited for capturing individual writing styles. The findings will also be valuable for developers of AI detection tools and for addressing concerns about authorship dilution.

Score: 7

Rationale: The paper presents a novel and significant investigation into a currently under-explored aspect of LLM personalization. Its strengths are in its rigorous methodology, large-scale evaluation, and practical relevance. However, the limitations related to language, genre, and the lack of human evaluation prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the paper doesn't revolutionize the field, it provides a valuable contribution and sets a strong foundation for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Controlling Language Difficulty in Dialogues with Linguistic Features
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14545v1)
- **Authors**: Shuyao Xu, Wenguang Wang, Handong Gao, Wei Kang, Long Qin, Weizhi Wang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have emerged as powerful tools for supporting second language acquisition, particularly in simulating interactive dialogues for speaking practice. However, adapting the language difficulty of LLM-generated responses to match learners' proficiency levels remains a challenge. This work addresses this issue by proposing a framework for controlling language proficiency in educational dialogue systems. Our approach leverages three categories of linguistic features, readability features (e.g., Flesch-Kincaid Grade Level), syntactic features (e.g., syntactic tree depth), and lexical features (e.g., simple word ratio), to quantify and regulate text complexity. We demonstrate that training LLMs on linguistically annotated dialogue data enables precise modulation of language proficiency, outperforming prompt-based methods in both flexibility and stability. To evaluate this, we introduce Dilaprix, a novel metric integrating the aforementioned features, which shows strong correlation with expert judgments of language difficulty. Empirical results reveal that our approach achieves superior controllability of language proficiency while maintaining high dialogue quality.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of controlling the language difficulty of large language model (LLM)-generated responses in educational dialogue systems, particularly for L2 learners. It proposes a framework that leverages linguistic features (readability, syntactic complexity, and lexical features) to quantify and regulate text complexity. The authors train LLMs on dialogue data annotated with these features, demonstrating precise control over language proficiency, outperforming prompt-based methods. They introduce a novel metric, Dilaprix (Dialogue Language Proficiency Index), to evaluate dialogue complexity. Empirical results show superior controllability of language proficiency while maintaining dialogue quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the **integration of linguistic features for direct control over LLM-generated language difficulty within a dialogue context.** While individual components like readability scores or syntactic analysis are not novel, their combined use, especially within the educational dialogue system, and the introduction of Dilaprix for specifically evaluating dialogue complexity, contribute to a novel approach. The Dilaprix metric is significant because existing metrics often target non-conversational text or require manually labeled proficiency data which can be subjective or hard to come by. The idea of training on dialogue data annotated with linguistic features is also relatively new. This is different from simply prompting the model with a CEFR level, for instance.

*   **Significance:** The work has significant implications for improving educational dialogue systems. Accurate control over language difficulty is crucial for effective L2 learning. The paper's findings suggest a more reliable and precise method compared to existing approaches. The introduction of Dilaprix also offers a valuable tool for researchers to evaluate and compare dialogue system proficiency. The framework's ability to dynamically adjust complexity based on the learner's level holds promise for personalized learning experiences. The thorough empirical validation, including comparisons to baselines and human evaluation, strengthens the significance of the findings. The ablation studies further highlight the importance of each linguistic feature. It should be noted that while the CEFR baseline works well on closed LLMs, it is less successful for open models, further underscoring the value of this research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:**  The paper considers multiple categories of linguistic features, offering a more holistic view of language complexity.
    *   **Novel Metric:** Dilaprix provides a quantitative and interpretable measure of dialogue complexity.
    *   **Strong Empirical Validation:**  The experiments are well-designed, with appropriate baselines, ablation studies, and human evaluation.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making the approach and results easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Dialogue Type:** The study focuses primarily on textbook dialogues, which are structured and may not generalize well to more open-ended conversational scenarios.
    *   **Reliance on Constituency Parsing:**  Syntactic feature extraction relies on constituency parsing, which can be computationally expensive and may introduce errors.
    *   **Uniformity of τ(xi):** While the paper mentions the flexibility of adjusting τ(xi) individually for each feature, the experimental results primarily focus on a uniform value. Further exploration of this aspect could provide more nuanced insights.
    *   **Limited Diversity Baseline:** The prompt baseline that guides CEFR may not be robust.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is likely to influence the development of more effective and personalized educational dialogue systems. The framework and evaluation metric can be adopted and extended by other researchers in the field.
   * **Further Research:** The authors could explore adapting the approach to different dialogue tasks and learner populations, developing more sophisticated methods for weighting the different linguistic features, and integrating the framework with adaptive learning algorithms.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant approach to controlling language difficulty in educational dialogue systems. The use of linguistic features for direct control and the introduction of the Dilaprix metric represent valuable contributions to the field. The thorough empirical validation strengthens the findings. However, the limited scope of dialogue types and reliance on a single parsing method slightly constrain the impact. Future work addressing these limitations could further enhance the significance of this research.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Rationality Check! Benchmarking the Rationality of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14546v1)
- **Authors**: Zhilun Zhou, Jing Yi Wang, Nicholas Sukiennik, Chen Gao, Fengli Xu, Yong Li, James Evans
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs), a recent advance in deep learning and machine intelligence, have manifested astonishing capacities, now considered among the most promising for artificial general intelligence. With human-like capabilities, LLMs have been used to simulate humans and serve as AI assistants across many applications. As a result, great concern has arisen about whether and under what circumstances LLMs think and behave like real human agents. Rationality is among the most important concepts in assessing human behavior, both in thinking (i.e., theoretical rationality) and in taking action (i.e., practical rationality). In this work, we propose the first benchmark for evaluating the omnibus rationality of LLMs, covering a wide range of domains and LLMs. The benchmark includes an easy-to-use toolkit, extensive experimental results, and analysis that illuminates where LLMs converge and diverge from idealized human rationality. We believe the benchmark can serve as a foundational tool for both developers and users of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Rationality Check! Benchmarking the Rationality of Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a benchmark for evaluating the rationality of Large Language Models (LLMs). It proposes a comprehensive taxonomy organizing rationality into individual, interpersonal, and societal levels, spanning six domains: psychology, cognitive science, decision-making, economics, game theory, and collective rationality.  The benchmark includes a toolkit, questionnaires, tests, and games designed to assess both theoretical and practical rationality.  The authors present extensive experimental results comparing open-source and commercial LLMs, highlighting areas where LLMs converge and diverge from human rationality. Key findings include LLMs demonstrating overall high rationality (especially larger models), high collective rationality favoring cooperation, and increased rationality in real-world scenarios. The benchmark aims to serve as a fundamental evaluation tool for developers and users of LLMs, guiding model optimization, training, and risk assessment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its **holistic and multi-faceted approach** to evaluating rationality in LLMs. While individual aspects of rationality (e.g., logical reasoning) have been explored, this work is the first to systematically benchmark rationality across a broad spectrum of domains and levels (individual, interpersonal, societal). The proposed taxonomy provides a solid framework for future research.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper is high. As LLMs are increasingly deployed in decision-making roles, assessing their rationality becomes paramount for ensuring responsible AI development and deployment. The benchmark contributes a **practical tool for developers and users** to understand the limitations and strengths of LLMs, which should support safer and more effective AI applications. By emphasizing collective rationality, the authors touch upon emergent social and ethical implications of LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** Covering a wide range of domains and using established human rationality measures strengthens the benchmark's validity.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The toolkit offers ease of use and accessibility, making the benchmark more likely to be adopted by the community.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The experimental results provide insightful observations about LLM behavior, including domain-specific strengths/weaknesses and comparative analysis with human rationality.
    *   **Clear Structure:** Well-defined taxonomy and categories allow readers to easily follow and repeat experiments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Data Contamination:** The paper acknowledges data contamination as a limitation. While the authors attempt to address this, the solution presented has limitations. The paper does not quantify how much this has had an effect.
    *   **Limited Scope of Mitigation in Appendix C.6:** The changes made to the test were not comprehensive enough to fully mitigate the threat of data contamination. Changing character names and numerical values is unlikely to impact LLMs trained on massive datasets. Furthermore, the results of modifying these are too brief to be statistically meaningful.
    *   **Simplistic Metrics:** Some of the rationality metrics rely on simplified scoring schemes, particularly in game theory, which might not fully capture the complexity of strategic interactions.
    *   **Focus on Western Rationality:**  The underlying rationality concepts are largely based on Western philosophical traditions. Cross-cultural validity needs more explicit consideration, with reference to wider literature, particularly when considering societal and collective rationality.
    *   **Limited Model Comparison:** While the paper compares several prominent models, it is difficult to generalize the results due to constant releases of better models.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This work has high potential to influence the field. It provides a baseline for future LLM development, informs AI safety research, and encourages more nuanced understanding of LLM behavior.  The benchmark can be expanded with new rationality metrics, languages, or domains to address the dynamic capabilities of AI.

**Justification of Score:**

This paper makes a significant and novel contribution by providing a first step towards the development of a tool to measure rationality within LLMs. By providing this tool, it is likely that future LLM developers will use this framework to better fine-tune and align models with human preferences. Despite some weaknesses that limit the breadth of its value, this should form a solid foundation for continued improvement.

### (P)rior(D)yna(F)low: A Priori Dynamic Workflow Construction via Multi-Agent Collaboration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14547v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Lin, Lujin Zhao, Yijie Shi
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have shown that carefully designed workflows coordinating large language models(LLMs) significantly enhance task-solving capabilities compared to using a single model. While an increasing number of works focus on autonomous workflow construction, most existing approaches rely solely on historical experience, leading to limitations in efficiency and adaptability. We argue that while historical experience is valuable, workflow construction should also flexibly respond to the unique characteristics of each task. To this end, we propose an a priori dynamic framework for automated workflow construction. Our framework first leverages Q-table learning to optimize the decision space, guiding agent decisions and enabling effective use of historical experience. At the same time, agents evaluate the current task progress and make a priori decisions regarding the next executing agent, allowing the system to proactively select the more suitable workflow structure for each given task. Additionally, we incorporate mechanisms such as cold-start initialization, early stopping, and pruning to further improve system efficiency. Experimental evaluations on four benchmark datasets demonstrate the feasibility and effectiveness of our approach. Compared to state-of-the-art baselines, our method achieves an average improvement of 4.05%, while reducing workflow construction and inference costs to only 30.68%-48.31% of those required by existing methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "PriorDynaFlow" (PDF), a framework designed for the a priori and dynamic construction of workflows in multi-agent systems. Unlike existing approaches that rely primarily on historical experience (a posteriori), PDF aims to proactively adapt to the unique characteristics of each task. The framework uses Q-learning to optimize agent decision-making, enabling agents to select the next executing agent based on real-time task progress and learned experience.  It also incorporates mechanisms for cold-start initialization, early stopping, and pruning to improve efficiency. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of PDF on four benchmark datasets, showing improved performance and reduced computational cost compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The main contribution is the combination of Q-learning and a priori decision-making for adaptive workflow construction, aiming for a more efficient and adaptable solution than purely experience-driven methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of a priori decision-making with Q-learning for dynamic workflow construction in multi-agent systems. Existing research has explored either autonomous workflow construction based on historical experience (a posteriori) or multi-agent reinforcement learning, but PDF integrates both approaches. The mechanisms for cold-start, early stopping, and pruning are standard techniques in RL but are combined here for workflow optimization, which does show some level of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from addressing the limitations of existing autonomous workflow construction methods. By enabling a priori adaptation, PDF can potentially overcome the inefficiencies and inflexibility associated with purely experience-driven approaches. The reported performance improvements and cost reductions on benchmark datasets suggest that PDF could be a practical solution for real-world applications that involve complex, multi-step tasks and LLMs. The results demonstrate a 4.05% average performance improvement over state-of-the-art methods with significant cost savings, which represents a notable advancement.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem being addressed and the limitations of existing solutions.
    *   The proposed framework is well-defined and incorporates several mechanisms to enhance efficiency and adaptability.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, including comparisons against multiple baselines and evaluations on diverse datasets.
    *   The results demonstrate tangible improvements in both performance and computational cost.
    *   The ablation study effectively shows the importance of the a priori aspect.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The techniques employed (Q-learning, pruning) are relatively standard. The novelty resides in the combination and application, which, while demonstrating improvement, may not be conceptually groundbreaking.
    *   The empirical results, while demonstrating gains, are still relatively incremental. A more significant margin of improvement would considerably bolster the paper's impact.
    *   While the paper demonstrates adaptability through the a priori decision-making, the extent of this adaptability in truly novel and dynamic scenarios could be more deeply explored. Testing on highly variable, real-world data with dynamically shifting dependencies would increase the practical impact.

*   **Potential Influence:** PDF has the potential to influence the field by inspiring further research on adaptive workflow construction techniques. The combination of a priori reasoning with reinforcement learning could be a promising direction for addressing the limitations of existing LLM-based systems. Also, PDF provides practical advantages over existing alternatives when it comes to reduced computational cost, thereby helping realize these benefits from large-scale LLM deployments.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a sound and well-executed approach, but the core ideas rely on established techniques.  The real novelty lies in combining a priori decision-making based on task progress with traditional RL methods for workflow optimisation in multi-agent LLM systems. While the results demonstrate improvement, it's more of a practical refinement rather than a paradigm shift. The improvements are not substantial enough to warrant a score higher than the current.

### Generative Large Language Models for Knowledge Representation: A Systematic Review of Concept Map Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14554v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoming Zhai
- **Abstract**: The rise of generative large language models (LLMs) has opened new opportunities for automating knowledge representation through concept maps, a long-standing pedagogical tool valued for fostering meaningful learning and higher-order thinking. Traditional construction of concept maps is labor-intensive, requiring significant expertise and time, limiting their scalability in education. This review systematically synthesizes the emerging body of research on LLM-enabled concept map generation, focusing on two guiding questions: (a) What methods and technical features of LLMs are employed to construct concept maps? (b) What empirical evidence exists to validate their educational utility? Through a comprehensive search across major databases and AI-in-education conference proceedings, 28 studies meeting rigorous inclusion criteria were analyzed using thematic synthesis. Findings reveal six major methodological categories: human-in-the-loop systems, weakly supervised learning models, fine-tuned domain-specific LLMs, pre-trained LLMs with prompt engineering, hybrid systems integrating knowledge bases, and modular frameworks combining symbolic and statistical tools. Validation strategies ranged from quantitative metrics (precision, recall, F1-score, semantic similarity) to qualitative evaluations (expert review, learner feedback). Results indicate LLM-generated maps hold promise for scalable, adaptive, and pedagogically relevant knowledge visualization, though challenges remain regarding validity, interpretability, multilingual adaptability, and classroom integration. Future research should prioritize interdisciplinary co-design, empirical classroom trials, and alignment with instructional practices to realize their full educational potential.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper is a systematic review of research on the use of generative large language models (LLMs) for concept map generation. It aims to synthesize the methods and technical features of LLMs employed in constructing concept maps and to analyze the empirical evidence validating their educational utility.  The review identifies six major methodological categories: human-in-the-loop systems, weakly supervised learning models, fine-tuned domain-specific LLMs, pre-trained LLMs with prompt engineering, hybrid systems integrating knowledge bases, and modular frameworks combining symbolic and statistical tools. It also analyzes the validation strategies used in these studies, ranging from quantitative metrics to qualitative evaluations. The review concludes that LLM-generated concept maps show promise for scalable and adaptive knowledge visualization but highlights remaining challenges related to validity, interpretability, multilingual adaptability, and classroom integration.  The paper emphasizes the need for interdisciplinary co-design, empirical classroom trials, and alignment with instructional practices to fully realize the educational potential of LLM-generated concept maps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** The paper provides a thorough and well-organized overview of the emerging field of LLM-based concept map generation. The systematic review methodology adds rigor and credibility.
    *   **Clear Categorization:** The identified methodological categories offer a useful framework for understanding the different approaches being used.
    *   **Addresses Key Research Questions:** The review directly addresses the stated research questions, providing insights into the methods used and the evidence supporting their educational utility.
    *   **Highlights Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the limitations and challenges associated with LLM-generated concept maps, providing a balanced perspective.
    *   **Identifies Future Directions:** The paper suggests specific areas for future research, contributing to the development of the field.
    *   **Timeliness**: The paper addresses a currently evolving trend in education technology where generative AI models are growing in prominence.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Empirical Data:** As the field is relatively new, the review may be constrained by the limited availability of robust empirical studies evaluating the pedagogical effectiveness of LLM-generated concept maps in real-world classroom settings.
    *   **Speculative Projections**: Some sections are speculative. Predictions about the future, while informed, are ultimately hypotheses and need to be considered as such.
    *   **Lack of Deep Dive:** While the review covers a broad range of approaches, it doesn't delve deeply into the technical intricacies of each method. A more in-depth analysis of the algorithms and architectures might be beneficial for researchers working in this area.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** While the review uses systematic methods, the selection of studies and the interpretation of findings could be influenced by the authors' perspectives.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper is novel in that it synthesizes and categorizes the emerging research on a specific application of LLMs (concept map generation) within the educational context. The categorization of methodologies alone makes it a useful contribution.
    *   The review is significant because it helps to consolidate current findings, identify gaps in the literature, and provide directions for future research in a rapidly evolving field. It serves as a valuable resource for researchers, educators, and developers interested in leveraging LLMs for knowledge representation and learning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a solid contribution to the field of educational technology by providing a timely and comprehensive overview of the emerging use of LLMs for concept map generation. Its clear categorization of methodologies, identification of limitations, and suggestion of future research directions are all valuable aspects. However, the relatively small amount of empirical data in this nascent field, and the paper's limited depth in technical analysis, preclude it from being considered a truly exceptional contribution. The paper also relies on speculation on its conclusions, rather than strong empirical evidence.

### LLM Jailbreak Detection for (Almost) Free!
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14558v1)
- **Authors**: Guorui Chen, Yifan Xia, Xiaojun Jia, Zhijiang Li, Philip Torr, Jindong Gu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) enhance security through alignment when widely used, but remain susceptible to jailbreak attacks capable of producing inappropriate content. Jailbreak detection methods show promise in mitigating jailbreak attacks through the assistance of other models or multiple model inferences. However, existing methods entail significant computational costs. In this paper, we first present a finding that the difference in output distributions between jailbreak and benign prompts can be employed for detecting jailbreak prompts. Based on this finding, we propose a Free Jailbreak Detection (FJD) which prepends an affirmative instruction to the input and scales the logits by temperature to further distinguish between jailbreak and benign prompts through the confidence of the first token. Furthermore, we enhance the detection performance of FJD through the integration of virtual instruction learning. Extensive experiments on aligned LLMs show that our FJD can effectively detect jailbreak prompts with almost no additional computational costs during LLM inference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LLM Jailbreak Detection for (Almost) Free!":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a method for detecting jailbreak attacks on Large Language Models (LLMs) with minimal computational overhead.  The core idea is based on the observation that jailbreak prompts often lead to less confident output distributions in the LLM compared to benign prompts.  The proposed method, Free Jailbreak Detection (FJD), leverages this by prepending an affirmative instruction to the input and then scaling the logits using temperature scaling to amplify the difference in confidence of the first generated token.  Furthermore, they propose a variant, FJD-LI, using virtual instruction learning to improve detection performance. The authors present experimental results on various aligned LLMs showing the effectiveness of FJD with virtually no additional computational cost during LLM inference.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using the confidence of the first token to detect jailbreak prompts is *moderately* novel. While prior work has explored confidence-based methods, the specific combination of prepending an affirmative instruction, temperature scaling, and especially the low computational cost focus distinguishes it. The application of virtual instruction learning (FJD-LI) builds upon existing instruction learning methods, adapting them specifically for this jailbreak detection task, adding a slight level of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The primary significance lies in the *practicality* of the approach. Jailbreak detection is a crucial aspect of deploying LLMs safely, and most existing methods involve significant computational overhead, either requiring external models or multiple inferences. A method that can detect jailbreaks "for almost free" during standard inference is highly valuable. The ability to detect transferable attacks also adds to its practicality. The results seem strong and the methodology is clearly presented, this is a practical, applicable jailbreak detection approach that can be easily integrated without significantly impacting the computational cost.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Low Computational Cost:**  This is the biggest strength. The method integrates smoothly into the existing inference pipeline without adding extra model calls or complex computations.
    *   **Simplicity:** The approach is relatively straightforward to implement and understand.
    *   **Effectiveness:** Experimental results demonstrate strong performance across various LLMs and jailbreak attacks, including transferable ones.
    *   **Virtual instruction learning:** The FJD-LI variant uses virtual instructions for better performance
    *   **Reproducibility:** All materials were released

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Affirmative Instruction:** The core mechanism relies on a specific affirmative instruction to work. While the authors show that different instructions work, the overall performance might be sensitive to the precise instruction chosen. The virtual instruction learning technique helps to mitigate this problem.
    *   **Potential for Adversarial Circumvention:** The simplicity of the approach also makes it potentially susceptible to sophisticated jailbreak attacks that are designed to specifically target or circumvent this detection method.  The authors acknowledge this in relation to detection-aware attacks.  A more robust adversarial analysis is needed to understand the limitations. The reliance on the first token confidence can be a limitation. While authors show that it can be augmented to multiple initial tokens, it is still a fundamental reliance on the model's first token inference.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by shifting the focus toward more efficient and practical jailbreak detection strategies.  It provides a strong baseline for future research and could be easily adopted by practitioners deploying LLMs. The simplicity and effectiveness of the technique will allow other researchers to build on top of it. The results open the door for confidence-based detection methods to further leverage existing LLM functionality.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field. The "almost free" detection capability is a significant advantage, especially for real-world deployment scenarios. Despite potential limitations regarding adversarial circumvention and sensitivity to the instruction prompt (partially addressed by FJD-LI), the practical significance and strong empirical results warrant a high rating. The approach offers a new angle on safety mitigation with virtually no cost. Given the increasing importance of LLM safety, this is a timely and valuable contribution. The technique is simple and easy to reproduce, thus can be a basis to develop LLM safety research. However, while a few previous methods already adopt confidence, it opens the door for better detection through LLM's inference probability distribution

### Adaptive and Iterative Point Cloud Denoising with Score-Based Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14560v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaonan Wang, Manyi Li, ShiQing Xin, Changhe Tu
- **Abstract**: Point cloud denoising task aims to recover the clean point cloud from the scanned data coupled with different levels or patterns of noise. The recent state-of-the-art methods often train deep neural networks to update the point locations towards the clean point cloud, and empirically repeat the denoising process several times in order to obtain the denoised results. It is not clear how to efficiently arrange the iterative denoising processes to deal with different levels or patterns of noise. In this paper, we propose an adaptive and iterative point cloud denoising method based on the score-based diffusion model. For a given noisy point cloud, we first estimate the noise variation and determine an adaptive denoising schedule with appropriate step sizes, then invoke the trained network iteratively to update point clouds following the adaptive schedule. To facilitate this adaptive and iterative denoising process, we design the network architecture and a two-stage sampling strategy for the network training to enable feature fusion and gradient fusion for iterative denoising. Compared to the state-of-the-art point cloud denoising methods, our approach obtains clean and smooth denoised point clouds, while preserving the shape boundary and details better. Our results not only outperform the other methods both qualitatively and quantitatively, but also are preferable on the synthetic dataset with different patterns of noises, as well as the real-scanned dataset.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes an adaptive and iterative point cloud denoising method using a score-based diffusion model. The method estimates the noise variation in a noisy point cloud to determine an adaptive denoising schedule. A trained network is then iteratively invoked to update the point clouds according to this schedule. The network architecture and a two-stage training strategy facilitate feature fusion and gradient fusion for iterative denoising.  The approach aims to improve denoising performance, preserve shape boundaries and details, and generalize to different noise patterns and real-scanned datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant problem in point cloud processing and provides a novel solution using score-based diffusion models.  The core idea of an adaptive denoising schedule is potentially impactful, allowing the denoising process to be tailored to individual noisy point clouds. The feature and gradient fusion components appear to be well-designed for preserving details.

However, the novelty of the individual components might be limited. Score-based diffusion models are already established, so applying them to point cloud denoising, while a reasonable step, isn't fundamentally new. The feature/gradient fusion modules, while enhancing performance, may also be built on existing techniques. The two-stage sampling strategy is incremental, though practically useful.

The empirical results appear strong, demonstrating improvements over state-of-the-art methods both quantitatively and qualitatively. The generalization experiments across different noise patterns are compelling. However, the reliance on synthetic datasets for training is a weakness, and the effectiveness on more diverse and complex real-world data needs further validation. A more thorough exploration of the parameter space and the sensitivity to various hyperparameters could also strengthen the paper. The comparison to SOTA is reasonable, but a more recent and comprehensive comparison to the *very* latest advancements in geometric deep learning and point cloud processing could be beneficial.

The paper has a clear structure and good writing quality, making it easy to understand the proposed method and its advantages. The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of the different components. The limitations of the proposed method are not really discussed.

Overall, the paper is a solid contribution that advances the state-of-the-art in point cloud denoising. It combines existing ideas in a novel way to achieve impressive results. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the dependency on synthetic data for training, and lack of a thorough limitations discussion limit the overall impact.

Score: 7
Justification: The paper presents a well-executed application of score-based diffusion models to point cloud denoising, achieving strong results via adaptive scheduling and feature fusion. The empirical evaluation is convincing. However, the core novelty is somewhat incremental, being an adaptation of existing techniques rather than a fundamental breakthrough. Also, the generalization could be discussed in a little more detail. The strong results, however, justify a score in the 7-8 range. It does advance the state-of-the-art. I lean towards the lower end of that range due to the somewhat iterative nature of novelty.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LiMuon: Light and Fast Muon Optimizer for Large Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14562v1)
- **Authors**: Feihu Huang, Yuning Luo, Songcan Chen
- **Abstract**: Large models recently are widely applied in artificial intelligence, so efficient training of large models has received widespread attention. More recently, a useful Muon optimizer is specifically designed for matrix-structured parameters of large models. Although some works have begun to studying Muon optimizer, the existing Muon and its variants still suffer from high sample complexity or high memory for large models. To fill this gap, we propose a light and fast Muon (LiMuon) optimizer for training large models, which builds on the momentum-based variance reduced technique and randomized Singular Value Decomposition (SVD). Our LiMuon optimizer has a lower memory than the current Muon and its variants. Moreover, we prove that our LiMuon has a lower sample complexity of $O(\epsilon^{-3})$ for finding an $\epsilon$-stationary solution of non-convex stochastic optimization under the smooth condition. Recently, the existing convergence analysis of Muon optimizer mainly relies on the strict Lipschitz smooth assumption, while some artificial intelligence tasks such as training large language models (LLMs) do not satisfy this condition. We also proved that our LiMuon optimizer has a sample complexity of $O(\epsilon^{-3})$ under the generalized smooth condition. Numerical experimental results on training DistilGPT2 and ViT models verify efficiency of our LiMuon optimizer.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LiMuon: Light and Fast Muon Optimizer for Large Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LiMuon, a new optimizer designed for training large machine learning models with matrix-structured parameters. LiMuon builds upon the Muon optimizer but aims to reduce memory consumption and improve speed by incorporating momentum-based variance reduction and randomized Singular Value Decomposition (SVD).  The authors provide theoretical convergence guarantees, demonstrating a lower sample complexity of O(ε⁻³) for finding an ε-stationary solution under both smooth and generalized smooth conditions in non-convex stochastic optimization. They validate the effectiveness of LiMuon through experiments on DistilGPT2 and ViT models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining variance reduction techniques (like STORM) with randomized SVD to accelerate and reduce the memory footprint of matrix-parameter optimization is reasonably novel. Existing Muon variants often suffer from high memory usage or high sample complexity, and LiMuon addresses both. The extension of convergence analysis to generalized smooth conditions is also a valuable addition, as it relaxes a strong assumption often violated in practice.

*   **Significance:** Efficient training of large models is a central challenge in modern machine learning.  Optimizers like Adam and its variants are widely used, but structured optimizers such as Muon offer the potential for improved performance by exploiting the structure of model parameters. A memory-efficient and fast Muon variant could be very impactful. The reduction in sample complexity, if achieved, would allow faster training.  The generalized smooth condition is very practical.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Solid Theoretical Analysis:** The convergence proofs offer credible justification for the method's efficiency. The analysis under both smooth and generalized smooth conditions adds to the robustness of the claims.
    *   **Memory Efficiency:** The use of RSVD to compress the matrix-structured parameters is a smart approach to reduce the state memory that optimizers need to maintain.
    *   **Practical Validation:** The experiments on DistilGPT2 and ViT provide some evidence for the method's effectiveness.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Experimental Evaluation:** While the experiments are present, more comprehensive benchmarking would be required. A few runs with single models does not have high confidence. The scale and complexity of modern large language models demand thorough experimentation across diverse architectures and datasets to fully assess the optimizer's capabilities.
    *   **Implementation Details and Overhead:** The paper provides the algorithm but implementation overhead of RSVD needs to be very carefully optimized to ensure that it does not add excessive cost.
    *   **Missing Comparision with other Momentum Based Optimizers :** It can be more useful to compare with many momentum based adaptive optimizers (like AdamW, AdaBelief, etc), to prove the improvement by the proposed work.
    *   **Over Claims:** The empirical results does not significantly prove the "light and fast" of the proposed LiMuon.

*   **Impact and Potential Influence:**  If the memory reduction and speed improvements can be demonstrated at scale (i.e., on much larger models), LiMuon could influence the development of future optimizers, making it possible to train larger and more complex models with limited resources. If the optimization can address the problems as above mentioned, it can be a very useful, practical method for optimization community.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques to address a significant problem in the field. The theoretical analysis is convincing, and the initial experimental results are promising. However, the limited experimental scope and potential practical challenges temper the enthusiasm. Therefore a more solid proof for "light and fast" claim is needed before its great practical usage. The novelty is somewhat incremental, building upon the existing Muon optimizer.
