### On the Use of Agentic Coding: An Empirical Study of Pull Requests on GitHub
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14745v1)
- **Authors**: Miku Watanabe, Hao Li, Yutaro Kashiwa, Brittany Reid, Hajimu Iida, Ahmed E. Hassan
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being integrated into software development processes. The ability to generate code and submit pull requests with minimal human intervention, through the use of autonomous AI agents, is poised to become a standard practice. However, little is known about the practical usefulness of these pull requests and the extent to which their contributions are accepted in real-world projects. In this paper, we empirically study 567 GitHub pull requests (PRs) generated using Claude Code, an agentic coding tool, across 157 diverse open-source projects. Our analysis reveals that developers tend to rely on agents for tasks such as refactoring, documentation, and testing. The results indicate that 83.8% of these agent-assisted PRs are eventually accepted and merged by project maintainers, with 54.9% of the merged PRs are integrated without further modification. The remaining 45.1% require additional changes benefit from human revisions, especially for bug fixes, documentation, and adherence to project-specific standards. These findings suggest that while agent-assisted PRs are largely acceptable, they still benefit from human oversight and refinement.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper you provided, "On the Use of Agentic Coding: An Empirical Study of Pull Requests on GitHub." Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the practical impact of agentic coding (autonomous AI agents generating code and submitting pull requests) on open-source projects. The authors empirically analyze 567 GitHub pull requests (PRs) generated by Claude Code across 157 diverse open-source projects. The study examines differences between agentic PRs and human-authored PRs in terms of change size, purpose, acceptance rates, revision requirements, and types of revisions. The results reveal that while agentic PRs are often accepted, they frequently require human oversight and refinement, especially for bug fixes, documentation, and adherence to project standards. The paper concludes with implications for researchers, developers, and builders of agentic coding tools, emphasizing the need for improved benchmark development, enhanced integration with project-specific conventions, and robust review scaffolding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper provides a valuable contribution by being among the first to empirically investigate agentic coding's real-world impact using pull request data. Much previous work focused on chatbots, this work assesses the full cycle of code generation and PR submission by an AI agent. However, the paper primarily analyzes PRs generated by a specific tool (Claude Code), which somewhat limits its generalizability, although the authors acknowledge this limitation. While prior research has examined the impact of LLM tools like ChatGPT in software development workflows, this work sets itself apart by analyzing Agentic Coding, where AI autonomous agents automate the full code generation and PR submission pipeline. This is a distinctive element that contributes to the originality of this research.

*   **Significance:** The findings offer significant insights into the strengths and weaknesses of agentic coding in practice. Demonstrating that the PRs are largely acceptable (83.8%), is a major finding with significant implications for the adoption of AI in software development. It addresses a knowledge gap regarding their usefulness and acceptance in real-world projects. The study also sheds light on the importance of human-AI collaboration in code development, highlighting the need for human oversight to ensure correctness, maintainability, and project standards. This practical relevance makes the paper valuable for the software engineering community. Its insights can inform how developers use these tools, and how future agentic systems can be better designed.
    The identified problem areas -- Documentation issues, bug fixes, code style improvements -- are vital for tool builders in order to improve the performance of their agents.
    Finally, the paper's methodology, including the careful data collection and analysis of PRs, provides a valuable framework for future studies in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical focus:** The study's reliance on real-world PR data strengthens its findings and increases their practical relevance.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The paper thoroughly examines various aspects of agentic PRs, including acceptance rates, revision requirements, and types of revisions.
    *   **Clear implications:** The authors clearly articulate the implications of their findings for researchers, developers, and tool builders.
    *   **Well-structured and well-written:** The paper is logically organized, easy to follow, and clearly written.
    *   **Focus on agentic coding:** The study moves beyond traditional chatbot integrations to analyze the impact of fully autonomous code-generating agents.
    *   **Replication package:** The study shares its replication package, allowing future researchers to build upon and verify the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Tool-specific analysis:** The focus on Claude Code limits the generalizability of the findings to other agentic coding tools.
    *   **Limited time frame:** The data collection period (February-April 2025) is relatively short, which might not capture long-term trends in agentic coding.
    *   **Threats to Validity:** The authors acknowledge potential threats to internal and external validity, like the limitations related to relying solely on PRs explicitly marked as "Generated with Claude Code."

*   **Influence on the Field:** This paper could influence the field by:

    *   **Setting the stage for future research:** Providing a baseline empirical analysis of agentic coding.
    *   **Informing tool development:** Guiding the development of more effective and user-friendly agentic coding tools.
    *   **Promoting best practices:** Encouraging the development of best practices for human-AI collaboration in code development.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper is not groundbreaking in its theoretical contributions, its empirical investigation of a rapidly evolving area (agentic coding) and its practical implications warrant a high score. It significantly contributes to the understanding of agentic coding's practical benefits and challenges. Given the current state of the field, and the relatively new emergence of Agentic Coding the work is impactful and useful to researchers and industry alike.

**Score: 7.5**

The paper is a valuable contribution to a growing area of research. Its empirical approach, comprehensive analysis, and clear implications make it a significant addition to the literature on AI in software engineering. While its tool-specific focus and limited time frame somewhat limit its generalizability, the study provides a strong foundation for future research in this area.

### Chain-of-Thought Re-ranking for Image Retrieval Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14746v1)
- **Authors**: Shangrong Wu, Yanghong Zhou, Yang Chen, Feng Zhang, P. Y. Mok
- **Abstract**: Image retrieval remains a fundamental yet challenging problem in computer vision. While recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong reasoning capabilities, existing methods typically employ them only for evaluation, without involving them directly in the ranking process. As a result, their rich multimodal reasoning abilities remain underutilized, leading to suboptimal performance. In this paper, we propose a novel Chain-of-Thought Re-Ranking (CoTRR) method to address this issue. Specifically, we design a listwise ranking prompt that enables MLLM to directly participate in re-ranking candidate images. This ranking process is grounded in an image evaluation prompt, which assesses how well each candidate aligns with users query. By allowing MLLM to perform listwise reasoning, our method supports global comparison, consistent reasoning, and interpretable decision-making - all of which are essential for accurate image retrieval. To enable structured and fine-grained analysis, we further introduce a query deconstruction prompt, which breaks down the original query into multiple semantic components. Extensive experiments on five datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of our CoTRR method, which achieves state-of-the-art performance across three image retrieval tasks, including text-to-image retrieval (TIR), composed image retrieval (CIR) and chat-based image retrieval (Chat-IR). Our code is available at https://github.com/freshfish15/CoTRR .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Chain-of-Thought Re-Ranking (CoTRR), a novel method to improve image retrieval performance by leveraging the reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). CoTRR addresses the limitation of existing MLLM-based retrieval systems that primarily use MLLMs for evaluating candidate images after the initial retrieval, without directly involving them in the ranking process.  CoTRR employs three key components: (1) Query Deconstruction to break down the user's query into structured semantic components, (2) Image Evaluation to assess how well each candidate image aligns with these components, and (3) Listwise Ranking to compare the evaluations and re-rank the candidate images based on the MLLM's reasoning. The method is designed to be training-free and applicable to text-to-image retrieval (TIR), composed image retrieval (CIR), and chat-based image retrieval (Chat-IR). Experiments on five datasets demonstrate state-of-the-art performance across these tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the design of a CoT re-ranking framework specifically tailored to image retrieval. While CoT reasoning is not a new concept in NLP, applying it to the image retrieval pipeline *during* re-ranking (rather than simply evaluation, as previous works did) and integrating it with query deconstruction and a nuanced image evaluation paradigm constitutes a valuable contribution. The authors have correctly identified a gap in current approaches which fail to utilize fully the reasoning capabilities of MLLMs for improving re-ranking.

*   **Significance:** The significant performance gains across multiple image retrieval tasks (TIR, CIR, Chat-IR) and datasets demonstrate the practical utility of CoTRR. The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of each component (query deconstruction, image evaluation, listwise ranking) in contributing to the overall performance.  The fact that it's a training-free approach further enhances its appeal and ease of adoption. The qualitative results are illustrative and add to the explanation of the effectiveness of the method.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem it addresses: the underutilization of MLLM reasoning abilities in image retrieval.
    *   **Well-Designed Method:** The CoTRR framework is well-structured, with a logical flow from query deconstruction to image evaluation to listwise ranking.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple datasets and tasks, and comparing against strong baselines. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of each component.
    *   **Training-Free:** Training-free approach enables wider adoption with less resource needed
    *   **Clear Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand, with clear explanations of the method and results.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on MLLM Performance:** The performance of CoTRR is heavily dependent on the quality of the underlying MLLM. While the authors demonstrate robustness across different MLLMs, the framework will inevitably be limited by the capabilities of the chosen MLLM. It would be beneficial to investigate the scalability of CoTRR with further advanced MLLMs as they are released to ensure consistent progress in performance.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the method is training-free, it involves multiple API calls to MLLMs, which could be computationally expensive and time-consuming, particularly for large-scale retrieval tasks.  The authors should include a discussion of the computational overhead and potential optimizations.
    *   **Limited Analysis on Failure Cases:** The paper primarily focuses on demonstrating the successes of CoTRR. A more detailed analysis of failure cases, identifying the types of queries or images where CoTRR struggles, would be valuable for further improving the method.
    * **Dependence on Initial Retrieval:** The method performance is dependent on a reasonable set of initial candidates retrieved. Although is it mentioned as a strength that CoTRR doesn't depend on a specific retrieval method, there are limitations since the re-ranking happens on the top-K results, where if the ground truth is not present, the method will not be able to correctly re-rank the images.

*   **Potential Influence:** The CoTRR framework has the potential to influence the design of future image retrieval systems by demonstrating the value of incorporating MLLM reasoning directly into the ranking process. The query deconstruction and image evaluation components could also be adopted or adapted in other multimodal tasks.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-designed approach to image retrieval that effectively leverages MLLM reasoning. The performance improvements are significant, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. The training-free nature of the method enhances its practical utility. However, the reliance on MLLM performance and the potential computational cost are limitations that need to be considered. There are also gaps in the failure analysis and scalability aspects, so these should be addressed with future research to better improve the method. Overall, the paper represents a substantial contribution to the field, but improvements and further analysis are needed to reach its full potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating Large Language Models for Cross-Lingual Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14749v1)
- **Authors**: Longfei Zuo, Pingjun Hong, Oliver Kraus, Barbara Plank, Robert Litschko
- **Abstract**: Multi-stage information retrieval (IR) has become a widely-adopted paradigm in search. While Large Language Models (LLMs) have been extensively evaluated as second-stage reranking models for monolingual IR, a systematic large-scale comparison is still lacking for cross-lingual IR (CLIR). Moreover, while prior work shows that LLM-based rerankers improve CLIR performance, their evaluation setup relies on lexical retrieval with machine translation (MT) for the first stage. This is not only prohibitively expensive but also prone to error propagation across stages. Our evaluation on passage-level and document-level CLIR reveals that further gains can be achieved with multilingual bi-encoders as first-stage retrievers and that the benefits of translation diminishes with stronger reranking models. We further show that pairwise rerankers based on instruction-tuned LLMs perform competitively with listwise rerankers. To the best of our knowledge, we are the first to study the interaction between retrievers and rerankers in two-stage CLIR with LLMs. Our findings reveal that, without MT, current state-of-the-art rerankers fall severely short when directly applied in CLIR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Evaluating Large Language Models for Cross-Lingual Retrieval" provides a systematic evaluation of LLMs for cross-lingual information retrieval (CLIR).  It focuses on two-stage retrieval, comparing different first-stage retrieval methods (lexical BM25, multilingual bi-encoders) and second-stage reranking models (listwise, pairwise).  A key aspect of the work is evaluating these components *without* relying on machine translation (MT) as a core part of the retrieval process, which is a departure from many existing CLIR evaluations. The study analyzes the interaction between retrievers and rerankers, the impact of document length, and the effectiveness of different reranking strategies (listwise vs. pairwise) across different resource language settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its large-scale, systematic comparison of LLMs for CLIR *without* full reliance on MT. While previous work has explored LLMs in CLIR, most approaches still use MT to bridge the language gap at the retrieval stage. The authors take a further step in exploring the interaction of first-stage retrievers (including more recent multilingual bi-encoders) with second-stage LLM rerankers. The comparison of pairwise and listwise reranking techniques in this context also adds to the novelty. The finding that benefits from document translation diminishes with stronger rankers is insightful.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant for several reasons:

    *   It demonstrates the potential of multilingual bi-encoders as first-stage retrievers in CLIR, offering an alternative to computationally expensive MT-based lexical retrieval.
    *   It highlights the limitations of current LLM rerankers when directly applied in CLIR *without* MT, revealing a substantial gap in their cross-lingual understanding.
    *   It provides practical guidance for building effective CLIR systems by showing how the choice of first-stage retriever and second-stage reranker interacts and affects overall performance.
    *   The release of code and resources enhances reproducibility and enables future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough experimental evaluation using established datasets (CLEF, CIRAL) and a diverse set of LLMs.
    *   **Well-Defined Research Questions:** The research questions (RQ1-RQ4) are clearly stated and guide the experimental design and analysis.
    *   **Insightful Analysis:** The authors provide insightful explanations for their findings, considering factors such as language resource availability, document length, and translation quality.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper's findings have practical implications for building real-world CLIR systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Model Scope:** While a good range of LLMs is considered, the field is rapidly evolving.
    *   **Zero-Shot Focus:** The experiments focus on zero-shot reranking, which might underestimate the potential of LLMs in CLIR. Fine-tuning could further improve performance, and the lack of it limits the generalizability of the conclusions somewhat.
    *   **Benchmark Choice:** The reliance on CLEF and CIRAL is good for comparability, but these benchmarks may not fully represent the diversity and challenges of modern web-scale CLIR. The document style and domain are potentially restrictive.
*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is very likely to influence future research in CLIR. The systematic comparison and the emphasis on MT-free approaches will encourage researchers to explore alternative architectures and training strategies for cross-lingual retrieval.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a significant contribution to the field of CLIR by providing a comprehensive, rigorous evaluation of LLMs *without* relying on machine translation, a methodology still common in CLIR. The findings are insightful and provide valuable guidance for building more effective and efficient CLIR systems. While the evaluation could be extended to include more recent models and fine-tuning experiments, the paper addresses a critical gap in the literature and has the potential to shape future research directions.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Data Augmentation via Latent Diffusion Models for Detecting Smell-Related Objects in Historical Artworks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14755v1)
- **Authors**: Ahmed Sheta, Mathias Zinnen, Aline Sindel, Andreas Maier, Vincent Christlein
- **Abstract**: Finding smell references in historic artworks is a challenging problem. Beyond artwork-specific challenges such as stylistic variations, their recognition demands exceptionally detailed annotation classes, resulting in annotation sparsity and extreme class imbalance. In this work, we explore the potential of synthetic data generation to alleviate these issues and enable accurate detection of smell-related objects. We evaluate several diffusion-based augmentation strategies and demonstrate that incorporating synthetic data into model training can improve detection performance. Our findings suggest that leveraging the large-scale pretraining of diffusion models offers a promising approach for improving detection accuracy, particularly in niche applications where annotations are scarce and costly to obtain. Furthermore, the proposed approach proves to be effective even with relatively small amounts of data, and scaling it up provides high potential for further enhancements.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of detecting smell-related objects in historical artworks, which is challenging due to stylistic variations, fine-grained semantic classes, and annotation sparsity. The authors explore the potential of synthetic data generation using latent diffusion models (LDMs) to alleviate these issues. They evaluate various diffusion-based augmentation strategies, focusing on a method called "Edge-Conditioned Object Generation with Contextual Inpainting" (EDGE). This method involves replacing objects in the original ODOR dataset with synthetically generated counterparts, conditioned on edge maps and textual prompts, and balancing underrepresented classes. The results show that incorporating synthetic data, particularly with the EDGE approach, can improve object detection performance compared to training solely on the original dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper's novelty lies in the specific application of latent diffusion models to the problem of smell-related object detection in artworks. While diffusion models have been used for data augmentation in other domains, their use for addressing annotation scarcity and class imbalance in this highly specialized area represents a unique contribution. Furthermore, the integration of ControlNet with edge-based conditioning and contextual inpainting tailored for the stylistic characteristics of artworks showcases a considered and innovative approach to synthetic data generation within a challenging domain. Although there are works on using diffusion models for data augmentation in other fields, the combination of components and problem context is novel.

*Significance and Impact:*

The significance of the work stems from its potential to advance the field of computational art history, specifically in the context of olfactory heritage research. By demonstrating the effectiveness of synthetic data generation, the authors offer a promising solution to overcome the limitations imposed by scarce and costly annotations. The research may enable more accurate and robust detection of smell references in artworks, leading to a deeper understanding of historical olfactory dimensions. The findings can also be useful in other niche applications with limited data where the problem is not only limited to artwork analysis.

*Strengths:*

*   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges associated with smell-related object detection in artworks.
*   **Novel application of LDM:** Using LDMs to address annotation sparsity in this specific domain.
*   **Systematic evaluation:** The authors evaluate several augmentation strategies and provide a detailed comparison.
*   **EDGE Method Implementation Details:** The EDGE method makes effective use of pre-trained components, making it easily extensible.
*   **Good Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to understand.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Performance Gains:** The performance gains achieved with synthetic data augmentation are relatively modest, while significant, the improvements do not appear to be a major breakthrough.
*   **Heuristic Selection:** The selection of the EDGE strategy relies on a heuristic approach, which introduces subjectivity and limits the generalizability of the findings. A more principled selection methodology would strengthen the conclusions.
*   **EDGE parameter details:** The paper does not mention details on the edge-conditioned parameters and thresholds used to select the best candidate.
*   **Dataset Size:** The paper has limited dataset size which could have benefited from generating more data.

*Potential Influence:*

The paper's potential influence lies in demonstrating the feasibility of using LDMs for data augmentation in specialized domains where annotations are scarce. The proposed approach can serve as a template for researchers working on similar problems in computational art history and other fields. The results also highlight the importance of carefully considering the masking strategy and training scheme when using LDMs for data augmentation. Future work could explore more sophisticated conditioning techniques, adaptive training strategies, and larger-scale synthetic data generation.

*Rigorous Rationale for Score:*

Given the novelty of the application and its potential for impact in computational art history, but tempered by the limited performance gains and heuristic selection process, a score of **6.5** is appropriate. While the paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating the feasibility of using LDMs for data augmentation in a specific domain, the results do not constitute a major breakthrough. Future work is needed to address the limitations and realize the full potential of the proposed approach. The well-defined problem, systematic evaluation, and insights on masking strategies and training schemes support this score.
Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Reasoning over Boundaries: Enhancing Specification Alignment via Test-time Delibration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14760v1)
- **Authors**: Haoran Zhang, Yafu Li, Xuyang Hu, Dongrui Liu, Zhilin Wang, Bo Li, Yu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in diverse real-world scenarios, each governed by bespoke behavioral and safety specifications (spec) custom-tailored by users or organizations. These spec, categorized into safety-spec and behavioral-spec, vary across scenarios and evolve with changing preferences and requirements. We formalize this challenge as specification alignment, focusing on LLMs' ability to follow dynamic, scenario-specific spec from both behavioral and safety perspectives. To address this challenge, we propose Align3, a lightweight method that employs Test-Time Deliberation (TTD) with hierarchical reflection and revision to reason over the specification boundaries. We further present SpecBench, a unified benchmark for measuring specification alignment, covering 5 scenarios, 103 spec, and 1,500 prompts. Experiments on 15 reasoning and 18 instruct models with several TTD methods, including Self-Refine, TPO, and MoreThink, yield three key findings: (i) test-time deliberation enhances specification alignment; (ii) Align3 advances the safety-helpfulness trade-off frontier with minimal overhead; (iii) SpecBench effectively reveals alignment gaps. These results highlight the potential of test-time deliberation as an effective strategy for reasoning over the real-world specification boundaries.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Reasoning Over Boundaries: Enhancing Specification Alignment via Test-Time Deliberation":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of specification alignment for Large Language Models (LLMs) in diverse, real-world scenarios where each situation has unique behavioral and safety specifications. The authors formalize this as a "specification alignment" problem.  They propose ALIGN3, a Test-Time Deliberation (TTD) method that uses hierarchical reflection and revision to better adhere to these changing specifications. To assess this, they introduce SPECBENCH, a new benchmark dataset across 5 scenarios, 103 specifications, and 1500 prompts.  They then evaluate several models and TTD methods to demonstrate the effectiveness of their ALIGN3 approach and highlight the potential of TTD for this problem.  Key findings include the benefit of TTD for spec alignment, ALIGN3 achieving a better safety/helpfulness trade-off, and SPECBENCH effectively revealing alignment gaps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in several aspects:

    *   **Problem Formalization:** Formalizing the problem of dynamic specification alignment for LLMs is a valuable contribution. While instruction following and safety alignment have been studied, the paper highlights the importance of adapting to scenario-specific specs which are often custom-tailored, thereby bridging a gap between existing research and real-world LLM deployment. This is particularly relevant given the increasing use of LLMs in diverse applications governed by bespoke requirements.
    *   **ALIGN3 Approach:**  ALIGN3's three-step process (behavior optimization, safety-guided refinement, and holistic specification audit) leverages Test-Time Deliberation (TTD) effectively to reason over safety and behavioral specifications. It is a pragmatic and lightweight solution, distinguishing it from more costly fine-tuning methods. The hierarchical reflection and revision strategy is also a novel way to approach aligning LLMs with complex specifications, offering a structured approach to balancing behavior and safety.
    *   **SPECBENCH Benchmark:** Creating SPECBENCH, a unified benchmark specifically designed for measuring specification alignment, is another significant novelty. The benchmark spans realistic scenarios and incorporates diverse specifications derived from real-world resources, providing a comprehensive assessment of LLMs' ability to adapt to different contexts.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from its potential impact on:

    *   **LLM Alignment:** By addressing the specification alignment problem, the paper contributes to developing more controllable and adaptable LLMs, making them better suited for real-world applications.
    *   **Safety and Ethics:** The focus on balancing behavior and safety specifications can help create LLMs that are both helpful and harmless, contributing to responsible AI development.
    *   **Benchmarking and Evaluation:** SPECBENCH offers a valuable resource for researchers to evaluate and compare different alignment techniques, potentially accelerating progress in this area. It enables organizations to establish their own specification boundaries in real-world applications.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-Defined Problem:** The paper clearly defines the problem of specification alignment and presents a compelling case for its importance.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed and use a variety of models and TTD methods to evaluate the effectiveness of ALIGN3 and the challenges of specification alignment.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The analysis is in depth, including ablation studies, a case study, and detailed comparisons across different subsets of the dataset.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper provides practical insights for developing and deploying LLMs in real-world scenarios where specification alignment is critical.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **LLM Evaluator Reliance:** The evaluation relies heavily on GPT-4.1 as an evaluator. While a human alignment study is conducted, this reliance introduces a potential bias depending on the specificities of this LLM model, which could influence the measured performance, and it might be subject to the changing behavior of that model over time.
    *   **Limited Complexity and Granularity of the Benchmarking Tasks:** Despite the breadth of the scenarios included within it, SPECBENCH still operates at a rather simplistic level of measurement. Specifications have been simplified. This necessitates more nuanced scenarios to more precisely pinpoint limitations within LLMs.
    *   **Potential Generalizability Limitations of the Experimental Results:**  While the study covers a range of models and benchmarks, the performance of ALIGN3 and other TTD methods might vary across different LLM architectures and deployment environments, highlighting the importance of continued research in this area. The current paper includes results on a subset of leading architectures.
*   **Potential Influence on the Field:**

    *   The paper is likely to spur further research on specification alignment, leading to new methods and benchmarks for addressing this challenge.
    *   The ALIGN3 approach could be adopted as a practical solution for improving the adaptability of LLMs in real-world applications.
    *   SPECBENCH may become a widely used benchmark for evaluating specification alignment, helping to drive progress in this area.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper is very solid. The work clearly contributes to the field by identifying and formalizing the specification alignment problem. It introduces a practical solution (ALIGN3) and a novel benchmark (SPECBENCH), supported by thorough experimentation and analysis. A rigorous rationale for the score is provided by the strengths and weaknesses outlined above. The primary factor preventing a higher score is the dependency on OpenAI's model, introducing evaluator bias, in order to assess the task.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### UMind: A Unified Multitask Network for Zero-Shot M/EEG Visual Decoding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14772v1)
- **Authors**: Chengjian Xu, Yonghao Song, Zelin Liao, Haochuan Zhang, Qiong Wang, Qingqing Zheng
- **Abstract**: Decoding visual information from time-resolved brain recordings, such as EEG and MEG, plays a pivotal role in real-time brain-computer interfaces. However, existing approaches primarily focus on direct brain-image feature alignment and are limited to single-task frameworks or task-specific models. In this paper, we propose a Unified MultItask Network for zero-shot M/EEG visual Decoding (referred to UMind), including visual stimulus retrieval, classification, and reconstruction, where multiple tasks mutually enhance each other. Our method learns robust neural-visual and semantic representations through multimodal alignment with both image and text modalities. The integration of both coarse and fine-grained texts enhances the extraction of these neural representations, enabling more detailed semantic and visual decoding. These representations then serve as dual conditional inputs to a pre-trained diffusion model, guiding visual reconstruction from both visual and semantic perspectives. Extensive evaluations on MEG and EEG datasets demonstrate the effectiveness, robustness, and biological plausibility of our approach in capturing spatiotemporal neural dynamics. Our approach sets a multitask pipeline for brain visual decoding, highlighting the synergy of semantic information in visual feature extraction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UMind, a unified multitask network for decoding visual information from M/EEG signals. UMind aims to perform zero-shot visual stimulus retrieval, classification, and reconstruction simultaneously. The key innovations include a multimodal alignment strategy incorporating M/EEG signals, images, and both coarse-grained and fine-grained text to extract robust neural visual and semantic representations. These representations are then used as dual conditional inputs to a pre-trained diffusion model for visual reconstruction. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of UMind through experiments on MEG and EEG datasets, showing its superior performance compared to single-task approaches and its ability to capture spatiotemporal neural dynamics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *unified multitask framework* for zero-shot M/EEG visual decoding, specifically addressing retrieval, classification, and reconstruction jointly. While individual components like contrastive learning, diffusion models, and multimodal alignment have been used before, their integration within a *single, end-to-end framework for M/EEG* visual decoding is a significant contribution. The dual-granularity text integration (coarse and fine-grained) is also a novel aspect that improves semantic understanding and enhances visual feature extraction.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical limitation in the field of BCI, namely the focus on single-task models and the limited use of semantic information. By providing a unified framework, UMind offers a more efficient and potentially more robust approach to M/EEG-based visual decoding. The zero-shot capability is also crucial, as it allows generalization to new visual stimuli without retraining, a major advantage in practical BCI applications. Furthermore, the paper provides a strong demonstration on real-world datasets (THINGS-EEG and THINGS-MEG), increasing the practical relevance of the work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Unified Multitask Framework:** The framework is a core strength, offering a synergistic approach to M/EEG visual decoding.
    *   **Dual-Granularity Text Integration:** Using coarse and fine-grained text descriptions enhances semantic representation learning.
    *   **Zero-Shot Capability:** The framework can generalize to unseen visual stimuli, making it more practical for real-world BCI applications.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides extensive quantitative and qualitative results on two different datasets, including ablation studies and comparisons with state-of-the-art methods.
    *   **Temporal and Spatial Analysis:** The EEG analysis elucidates the brain regions involved in visual processing.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The framework is complex, integrating multiple components. While this complexity yields improved performance, it also increases the engineering effort and makes it harder to diagnose individual component contributions.
    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained Models:** The framework relies heavily on pre-trained models (CLIP, diffusion models), meaning the performance is fundamentally limited by the capabilities of these pre-trained models.
    *   **Incremental improvements:** While the results outperform baselines, the gains on certain metrics like low-level metrics in Table 3 seem only marginally improved.
    *   **Reconstruction Quality:** Although the paper achieves promising results, the quality of reconstructed images is acknowledged to be behind fMRI based methods.
*   **Impact:** The framework will likely influence future research in M/EEG visual decoding. It sets a new standard for comprehensive models and highlights the importance of integrating semantic information. Other researchers might adopt the framework, adapt it to different tasks, or explore alternative architectures and pre-trained models within the same overall pipeline.

*Score:** 7.5*

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-engineered unified multitask framework that significantly advances the state-of-the-art in M/EEG visual decoding. The dual-granularity text integration is a valuable contribution, and the thorough experimental evaluation provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method. While the framework is complex and relies on pre-trained models, the significant performance gains and the zero-shot capability justify a strong score. The score of 7.5 reflects the substantial contribution, tempered by the complexity, the reliance on pre-trained models, and by only somewhat incrementally improving performance on some of the low-level metrics when compared with the baselines. It is clearly a step forward but with room for improvement.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Dataset Distillation for Super-Resolution without Class Labels and Pre-trained Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14777v1)
- **Authors**: Sunwoo Cho, Yejin Jung, Nam Ik Cho, Jae Woong Soh
- **Abstract**: Training deep neural networks has become increasingly demanding, requiring large datasets and significant computational resources, especially as model complexity advances. Data distillation methods, which aim to improve data efficiency, have emerged as promising solutions to this challenge. In the field of single image super-resolution (SISR), the reliance on large training datasets highlights the importance of these techniques. Recently, a generative adversarial network (GAN) inversion-based data distillation framework for SR was proposed, showing potential for better data utilization. However, the current method depends heavily on pre-trained SR networks and class-specific information, limiting its generalizability and applicability. To address these issues, we introduce a new data distillation approach for image SR that does not need class labels or pre-trained SR models. In particular, we first extract high-gradient patches and categorize images based on CLIP features, then fine-tune a diffusion model on the selected patches to learn their distribution and synthesize distilled training images. Experimental results show that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance while using significantly less training data and requiring less computational time. Specifically, when we train a baseline Transformer model for SR with only 0.68\% of the original dataset, the performance drop is just 0.3 dB. In this case, diffusion model fine-tuning takes 4 hours, and SR model training completes within 1 hour, much shorter than the 11-hour training time with the full dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel data distillation method for Single Image Super-Resolution (SISR) that addresses limitations of existing approaches, namely the dependence on pre-trained SR models and class-specific information. The proposed method leverages a Latent Diffusion Model (LDM) and replaces explicit class labels with a semantic feature space derived from CLIP embeddings. The approach consists of three key stages: (1) patch selection and clustering based on high-gradient patches and CLIP features for pseudo-label generation, (2) fine-tuning the diffusion model using a composite loss function incorporating a Minimax loss and a super-resolution specific loss, and (3) generating synthetic training images from the fine-tuned diffusion model.  Experiments demonstrate competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art methods, while significantly reducing training time and storage requirements and offering better generalizability across SR model architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper presents a significant advancement in data distillation for SR by removing the need for pre-trained models and class labels. This is a key strength, as it addresses a major limitation of previous methods like GSDD. The use of CLIP features for pseudo-labeling is also a novel approach that allows for semantic categorization of images without explicit annotations. The combination of Minimax loss and SR-specific loss for diffusion model fine-tuning is another notable contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper's impact lies in its potential to make SR model training more efficient and accessible. The ability to train SR models with significantly reduced datasets and computational resources opens up new possibilities for resource-constrained environments and faster experimentation. The improved generalizability across different SR model architectures makes the distilled datasets more versatile and reusable. The quantitative results demonstrate the superiority of the proposed method over existing techniques, especially in high compression scenarios.  The comprehensive ablation studies further solidify the contribution by isolating the benefits of each component of the pipeline.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addressing limitations of existing methods:** The paper successfully addresses the reliance on pre-trained models and class labels in GSDD.
    * **Novel use of CLIP features:**  The use of CLIP embeddings for pseudo-label generation is a clever and effective approach.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper provides thorough experimental validation across various SR architectures and dataset sizes.
    * **Significant reduction in training resources:** The achieved reduction in training time and storage requirements is substantial.
    * **Clear and well-organized presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The authors acknowledge that some hyperparameters may require dataset-specific tuning. This could limit the applicability of the method in some cases and requires further research into adaptive mechanisms.
    * **Performance Saturation:** The paper mentions that performance improvements saturate beyond a certain number of distilled patches. More research is needed to optimize the dataset size for different scenarios.
    * **Limited qualitative analysis:** While quantitative results are compelling, a more detailed qualitative analysis comparing the generated images to the original dataset would further strengthen the paper.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of SR by providing a more efficient and generalizable data distillation approach. It can lead to further research on adaptive hyperparameter tuning, optimal dataset size selection, and the exploration of alternative semantic feature spaces for pseudo-label generation.  The approach could also be extended to other image restoration tasks and even other deep learning problems requiring large datasets.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a significant contribution to the field of SR by introducing a novel and effective data distillation method.  The removal of pre-trained model and class label dependencies represents a crucial step forward.  The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating competitive performance with significantly reduced training resources. While there are minor weaknesses regarding hyperparameter sensitivity and performance saturation, these do not diminish the overall significance of the paper. The potential for influence in the SR field and beyond warrants a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Radiology Report Conditional 3D CT Generation with Multi Encoder Latent diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14780v1)
- **Authors**: Sina Amirrajab, Zohaib Salahuddin, Sheng Kuang, Henry C. Woodruff, Philippe Lambin
- **Abstract**: Text to image latent diffusion models have recently advanced medical image synthesis, but applications to 3D CT generation remain limited. Existing approaches rely on simplified prompts, neglecting the rich semantic detail in full radiology reports, which reduces text image alignment and clinical fidelity. We propose Report2CT, a radiology report conditional latent diffusion framework for synthesizing 3D chest CT volumes directly from free text radiology reports, incorporating both findings and impression sections using multiple text encoder. Report2CT integrates three pretrained medical text encoders (BiomedVLP CXR BERT, MedEmbed, and ClinicalBERT) to capture nuanced clinical context. Radiology reports and voxel spacing information condition a 3D latent diffusion model trained on 20000 CT volumes from the CT RATE dataset. Model performance was evaluated using Frechet Inception Distance (FID) for real synthetic distributional similarity and CLIP based metrics for semantic alignment, with additional qualitative and quantitative comparisons against GenerateCT model. Report2CT generated anatomically consistent CT volumes with excellent visual quality and text image alignment. Multi encoder conditioning improved CLIP scores, indicating stronger preservation of fine grained clinical details in the free text radiology reports. Classifier free guidance further enhanced alignment with only a minor trade off in FID. We ranked first in the VLM3D Challenge at MICCAI 2025 on Text Conditional CT Generation and achieved state of the art performance across all evaluation metrics. By leveraging complete radiology reports and multi encoder text conditioning, Report2CT advances 3D CT synthesis, producing clinically faithful and high quality synthetic data.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Report2CT, a novel framework for generating 3D chest CT volumes directly from full, unstructured radiology reports. This report-conditional latent diffusion model utilizes three pre-trained medical text encoders to capture nuanced clinical context from both the findings and impression sections of radiology reports. It trains a 3D latent diffusion model conditioned on these text embeddings and voxel spacing on the CT-RATE dataset.  The model's performance is evaluated using Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) and CLIP-based metrics, demonstrating anatomical consistency, visual quality, and text-image alignment superior to existing methods, notably GenerateCT. The model ranked first in the VLM3D Challenge at MICCAI 2025. The authors argue that their approach advances the state-of-the-art in 3D CT synthesis, producing clinically faithful and high-quality synthetic data that can benefit data augmentation, fairness, and robustness in medical imaging AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant challenge in medical image synthesis: generating realistic and clinically relevant 3D CT volumes from textual descriptions, and takes significant strides in bridging the gap between the rich information contained within medical reports and the output of image generation models.

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of several elements:
    *   **Full Radiology Report Conditioning:** While previous approaches used simplified prompts, Report2CT leverages entire findings and impression sections, capturing a more detailed clinical context. This is a significant step forward.
    *   **Multi-Encoder Architecture:** Using three pre-trained medical text encoders (BiomedVLP-CXR-BERT, MedEmbed, and ClinicalBERT) to process the text is a clever way to capture different aspects of the clinical language. This is particularly strong because medical language can be highly specific, and different encoders might be sensitive to different nuances.
    *   **Text-Conditional Latent Diffusion for 3D CT generation:** While Latent Diffusion Models (LDMs) have been applied to 2D medical images, extending them to 3D CT volumes with detailed clinical context is non-trivial due to the high dimensionality and memory constraints.

* **Significance:**  The ability to generate realistic and clinically faithful synthetic CT data has several important implications:
    *   **Data Augmentation:** Addresses the shortage of labeled medical data, particularly for rare diseases.
    *   **Fairness and Robustness:** Can be used to create datasets that are more representative of diverse patient populations, reducing bias in AI models. Enables robustness testing of models to different scenarios.
    *   **Explainability:** Synthetic counterfactual cases can be generated to understand how AI models make decisions.
    *   **Privacy:** Allows data sharing without compromising patient confidentiality.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Results:** The quantitative and qualitative results presented in the paper are compelling, showcasing the superior performance of Report2CT compared to existing methods. The reported ranking in the VLM3D challenge further validates the model's effectiveness.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a clear and detailed description of the proposed framework, including the architecture, training procedure, and evaluation metrics. The methodological details are thoroughly explicated, facilitating reproducibility.
    *   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is well-written and organized, making it easy to understand the problem, the proposed solution, and the results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** The high computational cost of training is a significant limitation. This restricts the number of experiments and might limit the applicability of the method in resource-constrained settings.
    *   **Reliance on High-Quality Reports:** The model's performance depends on the availability of detailed and comprehensive radiology reports. In situations where reports are incomplete or poorly written, the model's performance might degrade.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** While FID and CLIP scores are commonly used, they are not perfect measures of clinical realism. Further evaluation by expert radiologists would strengthen the findings.  Specifically, a clinical utility study could assess whether synthetic data can benefit real-world clinical tasks.
    *   **Lack of Direct Comparison with Text2CT:** The authors mention that direct comparison with Text2CT was not possible due to code and data unavailability. This omission is a weakness, as Text2CT seems to be the most closely related prior work.

* **Potential Influence:**  Report2CT has the potential to significantly influence the field of medical image synthesis by demonstrating the feasibility of generating high-quality 3D CT volumes from detailed radiology reports. It can inspire further research on leveraging unstructured text data for medical image generation and lead to the development of more robust and clinically relevant AI models.

**Justification for Score:**

Report2CT demonstrates substantial novelty by significantly improving upon existing methods for text-conditional 3D CT generation.  The clever use of multiple encoders and conditioning on full radiology reports allows the framework to synthesize high-quality images with clinically relevant detail. The results are persuasive, with both the quantitative and qualitative analysis demonstrating significant improvement. While the computational cost is a limiting factor, the results presented in the paper suggest that Report2CT has the potential to have a strong influence on medical imaging AI. However, the absence of a direct comparison to Text2CT and limited validation through clinical assessment with expert radiologists are drawbacks that limit the scope of influence.

### SINAI at eRisk@CLEF 2023: Approaching Early Detection of Gambling with Natural Language Processing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14797v1)
- **Authors**: Alba Maria Marmol-Romero, Flor Miriam Plaza-del-Arco, Arturo Montejo-Raez
- **Abstract**: This paper describes the participation of the SINAI team in the eRisk@CLEF lab. Specifically, one of the proposed tasks has been addressed: Task 2 on the early detection of signs of pathological gambling. The approach presented in Task 2 is based on pre-trained models from Transformers architecture with comprehensive preprocessing data and data balancing techniques. Moreover, we integrate Long-short Term Memory (LSTM) architecture with automodels from Transformers. In this Task, our team has been ranked in seventh position, with an F1 score of 0.126, out of 49 participant submissions and achieves the highest values in recall metrics and metrics related to early detection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes the SINAI team's approach to Task 2 (early detection of pathological gambling) in the eRisk@CLEF 2023 lab. The approach focuses on using Transformer-based pre-trained language models, data preprocessing and balancing techniques, and the integration of LSTM architecture with Transformer automodels. The team achieved seventh position out of 49 submissions with an F1 score of 0.126, and highest values in recall and metrics for early detection. The paper details data preprocessing steps, different model architectures explored (including RoBERTa-Large, XLM-ROBERTa-Large, and a hybrid RoBERTa-Large + LSTM), and the experimental setup, including hyperparameter tuning.  It presents the results obtained in both decision-based and ranking-based evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty is somewhat limited. While it explores different configurations of pre-trained language models for gambling detection, the use of Transformers for this task is not fundamentally new. The integration of an LSTM with a Transformer model represents a more novel approach. However, the specific implementation and the way it leverages sequential information isn't deeply explored. The description of the integration lacks details, making it hard to assess how well the LSTM actually complements the Transformer. The data preprocessing methods are also fairly standard in NLP tasks.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its practical application of existing NLP techniques to a real-world problem (early detection of gambling addiction). The eRisk@CLEF lab provides a valuable benchmark, and the paper contributes to the overall body of knowledge in this area. The seventh place ranking and highest recall values show promise. However, the low F1 score (0.126) suggests that the models struggle with precision. The focus on high recall, in this context, is reasonable as missing potential gambling addiction cases is a larger concern than incorrectly flagging non-addicts.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear description of the task and datasets.
    *   Systematic exploration of different Transformer models and the LSTM hybrid.
    *   Focus on data preprocessing and balancing techniques.
    *   Discussion of both decision-based and ranking-based evaluation metrics.
    *   Attempts at hyperparameter optimization and justification for choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited novelty in core techniques.
    *   Lack of detail in the LSTM integration, particularly about how temporal dependencies are captured and utilized.
    *   The experimental setup details are somewhat superficial; more information about training time, parameter count, etc. would have been valuable.
    *   The discussion of results is descriptive but lacks in-depth analysis.  Why did certain models perform better than others?  What specific challenges were encountered? The insights are superficial.
    *   Low F1 score raises concerns about overall performance. More in depth error analysis is needed.
    *   The paper describes exploration of an LSTM based method but the description and results are high level.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper may serve as a useful reference point for researchers entering the field of early risk detection. It highlights the importance of data preprocessing and the potential of Transformers for this type of task. However, due to the lack of significant innovation, its influence on the field is likely to be incremental rather than transformative. The main added value is the empirical comparison of different transformer model choices and the attempt to combine LSTM and transformer architectures. The high-recall results and a description of the types of techniques that yielded those results for this task is useful.

**Score: 5**

**Justification:**

The paper is a solid, practical application of existing NLP methods to an important problem. However, it lacks the depth of innovation and rigorous analysis required for a higher score. The LSTM integration, while interesting, is not sufficiently detailed or thoroughly analyzed. The high recall is a positive aspect, but the low F1 score raises questions about the overall performance and applicability of the approach. Ultimately, the paper provides a useful benchmark and confirms the potential of Transformers for early risk detection, but falls short of making a significant breakthrough or contribution to the field.

### OnlineMate: An LLM-Based Multi-Agent Companion System for Cognitive Support in Online Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14803v1)
- **Authors**: Xian Gao, Zongyun Zhang, Ting Liu, Yuzhuo Fu
- **Abstract**: In online learning environments, students often lack personalized peer interactions, which play a crucial role in supporting cognitive development and learning engagement. Although previous studies have utilized large language models (LLMs) to simulate interactive dynamic learning environments for students, these interactions remain limited to conversational exchanges, lacking insights and adaptations to the learners' individualized learning and cognitive states. As a result, students' interest in discussions with AI learning companions is low, and they struggle to gain inspiration from such interactions. To address this challenge, we propose OnlineMate, a multi-agent learning companion system driven by LLMs that integrates the Theory of Mind (ToM). OnlineMate is capable of simulating peer-like agent roles, adapting to learners' cognitive states during collaborative discussions, and inferring their psychological states, such as misunderstandings, confusion, or motivation. By incorporating Theory of Mind capabilities, the system can dynamically adjust its interaction strategies to support the development of higher-order thinking and cognition. Experimental results in simulated learning scenarios demonstrate that OnlineMate effectively fosters deep learning and discussions while enhancing cognitive engagement in online educational settings.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "OnlineMate: An LLM-Based Multi-Agent Companion System for Cognitive Support in Online Learning".

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OnlineMate, a multi-agent learning companion system powered by LLMs and enhanced with Theory of Mind (ToM) capabilities.  The system aims to address the limitations of current online learning environments by providing personalized peer-like interactions that adapt to students' cognitive and psychological states. OnlineMate infers these states during collaborative discussions and dynamically adjusts its interaction strategies to cater to learners' interests and needs, promoting deeper reflection and cognitive development. The authors decompose the response generation process into three stages, aligning with metacognitive theory: ToM hypothesis generation, hypothesis refinement and filtering, and response generation and validation. The system's effectiveness is evaluated using automated and human evaluations in simulated classroom scenarios, demonstrating improvements in cognitive engagement and learning outcomes.  The paper also includes an ablation study to investigate the impact of various factors, such as the number of discussion rounds and agents, on student learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of leveraging LLMs to create interactive learning environments isn't entirely novel.  Several prior works have explored LLMs for simulating students and teachers (referenced in the paper). However, the integration of Theory of Mind (ToM) into a multi-agent learning system and its focus on *adaptive personalization based on inferred cognitive and psychological states* represents a meaningful advancement.  The decomposition of the ToM reasoning process into distinct stages (hypothesis generation, refinement, and validation) to maintain persona consistency is also a novel aspect of the design.

* **Significance:** The potential impact of OnlineMate on online education is significant. By addressing the lack of personalized interactions and cognitive adaptation, it could make online learning more engaging and effective, potentially bridging the gap between traditional classroom settings and virtual learning environments.  The system aims to foster higher-order thinking skills and promote emotional investment in learning, which are crucial for student success. The rigorous experimental evaluation, including both automated and human assessments, provides solid evidence of the system's effectiveness.  The ablation study offers valuable insights into the factors that contribute to positive learning outcomes, informing future system design.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of limited interaction and lack of personalized support in online learning.
    * **Innovative Approach:** The integration of ToM into a multi-agent learning system is a creative and promising solution.
    * **Well-Defined System Architecture:** The paper provides a detailed description of OnlineMate's architecture, including the Classroom Context Manager, Classroom Behavior Controller, and Evaluation Agent.
    * **Rigorous Evaluation:** The use of both automated and human evaluations, along with an ablation study, strengthens the validity of the results.
    * **Detailed Experiments:** The descriptions of experiments are comprehensive with solid evaluation and reasoning in the results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Simulated Environment:** The evaluation is conducted in a simulated environment, which may not fully capture the complexities of real-world classroom interactions.  The simulated students could behave predictably, impacting the evaluations. The jump from simulation to practical application is non-trivial and is not yet demonstrated.
    * **Reliance on LLM Assumptions:** The system relies heavily on the ToM capabilities of LLMs, which are still evolving and may exhibit biases or limitations. While the authors decompose the ToM process, the inherent challenges of LLM-based reasoning persist.
    * **Limited Scope of Courses Evaluated:**  The evaluation is limited to two specific courses, potentially limiting the generalizability of the results.

* **Potential Influence:** OnlineMate could influence the design of future online learning systems by demonstrating the value of personalized, adaptive interactions and the potential of ToM-enhanced agents.  It could also inspire further research into the use of LLMs for simulating social and cognitive processes in educational contexts.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and promising approach to online learning, the reliance on a simulated environment and the inherent limitations of LLMs temper its overall significance. The use of ToM and adaptive personalization is a definite step forward, but the practical implications and generalizability remain to be thoroughly demonstrated.

### Towards Building Speech Large Language Models for Multitask Understanding in Low-Resource Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14804v1)
- **Authors**: Mingchen Shao, Bingshen Mu, Chengyou Wang, Hai Li, Ying Yan, Zhonghua Fu, Lei Xie
- **Abstract**: Speech large language models (SLLMs) built on speech encoders, adapters, and LLMs demonstrate remarkable multitask understanding performance in high-resource languages such as English and Chinese. However, their effectiveness substantially degrades in low-resource languages such as Thai. This limitation arises from three factors: (1) existing commonly used speech encoders, like the Whisper family, underperform in low-resource languages and lack support for broader spoken language understanding tasks; (2) the ASR-based alignment paradigm requires training the entire SLLM, leading to high computational cost; (3) paired speech-text data in low-resource languages is scarce. To overcome these challenges in the low-resource language Thai, we introduce XLSR-Thai, the first self-supervised learning (SSL) speech encoder for Thai. It is obtained by continuously training the standard SSL XLSR model on 36,000 hours of Thai speech data. Furthermore, we propose U-Align, a speech-text alignment method that is more resource-efficient and multitask-effective than typical ASR-based alignment. Finally, we present Thai-SUP, a pipeline for generating Thai spoken language understanding data from high-resource languages, yielding the first Thai spoken language understanding dataset of over 1,000 hours. Multiple experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our methods in building a Thai multitask-understanding SLLM. We open-source XLSR-Thai and Thai-SUP to facilitate future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of building speech large language models (SLLMs) for multitask understanding in low-resource languages, specifically focusing on Thai. The authors identify three key limitations: inadequate performance of existing speech encoders, the high computational cost of ASR-based alignment, and the scarcity of paired speech-text data. To overcome these, they introduce XLSR-Thai, a self-supervised learning (SSL) speech encoder trained on a large Thai speech dataset. They propose U-Align, a more resource-efficient and multitask-effective speech-text alignment method. Furthermore, they present Thai-SUP, a pipeline for generating Thai spoken language understanding data from high-resource languages.  Experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of their methods in building a Thai multitask understanding SLLM. XLSR-Thai and Thai-SUP are open-sourced.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates good novelty by addressing a critical gap in the SLLM field: the lack of effective SLLMs for low-resource languages, especially for multitask understanding.  XLSR-Thai is the first SSL speech encoder specifically trained on a large Thai corpus, and Thai-SUP is the first spoken language understanding dataset for Thai. The U-Align method provides a more efficient alignment strategy compared to traditional ASR-based approaches. The combination of these three components represents a novel and coherent approach to the problem.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to democratize access to SLLMs for languages beyond English and Chinese. The solutions presented address the key bottlenecks in low-resource language SLLM development, including data scarcity, encoder performance, and alignment efficiency. By open-sourcing XLSR-Thai and Thai-SUP, the authors are facilitating future research and development in this area. The results indicate substantial improvements in both ASR and multitask understanding tasks for Thai, demonstrating the practical value of the proposed methods. However, it's important to acknowledge the relatively small scale of the experiments, especially regarding the finetuning with Thai-SUP.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clearly identifies and addresses critical limitations in low-resource language SLLM development.
    *   Proposes novel solutions for each limitation, including XLSR-Thai, U-Align, and Thai-SUP.
    *   Provides empirical evidence demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed methods.
    *   Open-sources resources (XLSR-Thai and Thai-SUP) to promote future research.
    *   Comprehensive comparison with baselines.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The experiments are primarily focused on Thai. While this allows for a targeted investigation, it limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The reliance on LLMs for data augmentation and translation in Thai-SUP introduces potential biases and artifacts. The quality filtering step is important, but it might not eliminate all issues.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the U-Align method, including a theoretical justification for its effectiveness.
    *   While U-Align aims for efficient alignment, details on the exact computational savings would be useful, compared to training the entire SLLM ASR based approach, especially showing its efficiency.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of SLLMs for other low-resource languages. The proposed methods can be adapted to other languages by creating similar SSL encoders and generating spoken language understanding datasets.  The U-Align method could also be applied to improve the alignment of speech and text embeddings in other SLLM architectures.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper represents a significant step towards building effective SLLMs for low-resource languages. The proposed solutions are novel and address key limitations in the field. The empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of the methods, and the open-sourcing of resources facilitates future research. However, the limited generalizability due to the focus on Thai and potential biases introduced by LLM-based data augmentation slightly temper the score. The significant improvement shown warrants a high score, but the above weaknesses prevent a score higher than 7.5. The paper is more than a incremental change, and is a notable advancement in the field of SLLMs in low-resource settings.

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### SINAI at eRisk@CLEF 2022: Approaching Early Detection of Gambling and Eating Disorders with Natural Language Processing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14806v1)
- **Authors**: Alba Maria Marmol-Romero, Salud Maria Jimenez-Zafra, Flor Miriam Plaza-del-Arco, M. Dolores Molina-Gonzalez, Maria-Teresa Martin-Valdivia, Arturo Montejo-Raez
- **Abstract**: This paper describes the participation of the SINAI team in the eRisk@CLEF lab. Specifically, two of the proposed tasks have been addressed: i) Task 1 on the early detection of signs of pathological gambling, and ii) Task 3 on measuring the severity of the signs of eating disorders. The approach presented in Task 1 is based on the use of sentence embeddings from Transformers with features related to volumetry, lexical diversity, complexity metrics, and emotion-related scores, while the approach for Task 3 is based on text similarity estimation using contextualized word embeddings from Transformers. In Task 1, our team has been ranked in second position, with an F1 score of 0.808, out of 41 participant submissions. In Task 3, our team also placed second out of a total of 3 participating teams.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper describes the SINAI team's participation in the eRisk@CLEF 2022 lab, focusing on two tasks: Task 1 (early detection of pathological gambling) and Task 3 (measuring the severity of eating disorder signs). For Task 1, the team utilized sentence embeddings from Transformers combined with features related to volumetry, lexical diversity, complexity metrics, and emotion scores. For Task 3, they employed text similarity estimation using contextualized word embeddings from Transformers. The team achieved second place in both tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses relevant and important problems: early detection of mental health risks like gambling and eating disorders, leveraging social media data.
    *   The approach for Task 1 is well-reasoned, combining Transformer models with carefully engineered features. The use of volumetry, lexical diversity, complexity metrics and emotion is a sensible combination.
    *   Achieving second place in both tasks demonstrates the effectiveness of the developed methods.
    *   The paper provides clear descriptions of the system architectures, methodologies, and experimental setups. The detailed explanation of the features used in Task 1 and the heuristic used in Task 3 is helpful.
    *   The error analysis, however high level, gives insight in the limitation of the developed methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty is somewhat limited. While the combination of techniques might be unique to this particular challenge, the individual components (Transformers, volumetry analysis, etc.) are well-established.
    *   The approach for Task 3 relies on a relatively simple text similarity and a heuristic. While understandable given the lack of training data, it is less sophisticated than the Task 1 approach.
    *   The evaluation of Task 3 highlights a major limitation: very high error rates. While the authors acknowledge the difficulty of the task, it underscores the limitations of their approach and suggests it is not very performant.
    *   The discussion of results could be more in-depth, especially for Task 3, with more fine-grained error analysis (not just "food restriction" and "shape").
    *   There is no comparison against any previous state-of-the-art that solved a similar task or a discussion of the previous work done by SINAI in similar tasks.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper's significance lies primarily in its application of NLP techniques to mental health risk detection.  Demonstrating success (second place) in the eRisk@CLEF challenge can motivate further research in this area. However, the limited novelty and the issues with Task 3 temper the overall impact. The work contributes to the ongoing effort to develop automated tools for early detection, but it is not a groundbreaking advance.

*   **Novelty:** Limited. While the specific feature combinations and hyperparameter settings are unique to this work, the underlying techniques are not novel. Text similarity is a well-established technique that is frequently used in the context of NLP.
*   **Impact:** Moderate. The paper validates the use of NLP for early risk detection and contributes to a specific benchmark (eRisk@CLEF). However, the error rates, especially in Task 3, limit the real-world applicability of the proposed methods.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a competent application of NLP techniques to an important problem. The methods are well described, and the results for Task 1 are promising. However, the limited novelty, the reliance on heuristics in Task 3, and the high error rates temper the overall impact. The paper provides a solid contribution to the eRisk@CLEF benchmark, but it does not represent a major breakthrough in the field.
