The overall framework represents a significant departure from approaches that rely on retraining or fine-tuning.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the ability to unlock the potential of pre-trained VDMs for spatial intelligence tasks without incurring the costs and risks associated with retraining. This significantly lowers the barrier to entry for using these models in applications requiring precise 3D/4D control.  The paper effectively bridges the gap between the strong priors learned by VDMs and their practical use in demanding tasks.  The potential impact is amplified by the plug-and-play nature of the framework, which can be applied to different VDM backbones. The experiments showcasing high-quality 360 views and dynamic re-rendering highlight the practical value of the framework.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Training-free approach:**  A major advantage, avoiding computational costs and preventing degradation of pre-trained knowledge.
    *   **Modular design:**  Allows for analysis and potential future improvements to individual components.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  Extensive experiments on diverse datasets and tasks provide strong empirical support for the claims.
    *   **Strong results:** Demonstrates superior performance compared to both training-based and training-free baselines in key metrics.
    *   **Model agnostic:** The framework's applicability across various VDM backbones indicates its general utility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Depth Estimation:** The warping-and-repainting approach is inherently limited by the accuracy of depth estimation. While the paper mentions the method's robustness, extremely poor depth estimations could still lead to failures.
    *   **Global Guidance Limitations:**  The paper acknowledges the limitations in controlling small objects or fine details due to the global nature of the guidance. Future research could explore more localized control mechanisms.
    *   **Computational overhead:** While the framework avoids retraining, the IRR mechanism introduces a ~50% increase in runtime, which could be a limiting factor in some applications.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of video generation and 3D/4D reconstruction by providing a powerful and practical tool for controlling VDMs. It opens up new avenues for exploring spatial intelligence and building emergent world models using readily available pre-trained resources. The training-free aspect is particularly appealing to researchers and practitioners with limited computational resources. The framework could inspire new research on disentangling latent representations and developing more fine-grained control mechanisms for generative models.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:** WorldForge represents a significant advancement in controllable video generation, striking a compelling balance between controllability, visual quality, and generalization. Its innovative use of inference-time guidance and the unique combination of IRR, FLF, and DSG merit a high score. While the reliance on depth estimation and global guidance pose limitations, the strengths of the framework significantly outweigh its weaknesses. The impact on the field could be substantial, making it a notable contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 8/10

### A1: Asynchronous Test-Time Scaling via Conformal Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15148v1)
- **Authors**: Jing Xiong, Qiujiang Chen, Fanghua Ye, Zhongwei Wan, Chuanyang Zheng, Chenyang Zhao, Hui Shen, Alexander Hanbo Li, Chaofan Tao, Haochen Tan, Haoli Bai, Lifeng Shang, Lingpeng Kong, Ngai Wong
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) benefit from test-time scaling, but existing methods face significant challenges, including severe synchronization overhead, memory bottlenecks, and latency, especially during speculative decoding with long reasoning chains. We introduce A1 (Asynchronous Test-Time Scaling), a statistically guaranteed adaptive inference framework that addresses these challenges. A1 refines arithmetic intensity to identify synchronization as the dominant bottleneck, proposes an online calibration strategy to enable asynchronous inference, and designs a three-stage rejection sampling pipeline that supports both sequential and parallel scaling. Through experiments on the MATH, AMC23, AIME24, and AIME25 datasets, across various draft-target model families, we demonstrate that A1 achieves a remarkable 56.7x speedup in test-time scaling and a 4.14x improvement in throughput, all while maintaining accurate rejection-rate control, reducing latency and memory overhead, and no accuracy loss compared to using target model scaling alone. These results position A1 as an efficient and principled solution for scalable LLM inference. We have released the code at https://github.com/menik1126/asynchronous-test-time-scaling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces A1 (Asynchronous Test-Time Scaling), a new framework for scaling large language models (LLMs) at test time.  A1 addresses the challenges of synchronization overhead, memory bottlenecks, and latency encountered in existing test-time scaling methods, particularly during speculative decoding with long reasoning chains. A1 employs several key techniques: (1) asynchronous arithmetic intensity, a new metric that focuses on synchronization bottleneck, (2) online calibration based on conformal prediction to ensure both efficient rejection and retain high quality reasoning chains, and (3) a three-stage rejection sampling pipeline supporting both sequential and parallel scaling.  Experiments on mathematical and reasoning datasets (MATH, AMC23, AIME24, AIME25) demonstrate that A1 achieves significant speedup (56.7x) and throughput improvement (4.14x) compared to target model scaling alone, without sacrificing accuracy and effectively controling rejection rate. The code is released.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates good novelty by addressing a pressing need for efficient and scalable LLM inference. While speculative decoding and test-time scaling are not entirely new concepts, A1's combination of techniques, especially the asynchronous approach guided by conformal prediction, appears to be a novel solution to a significant problem.  The introduction of the "asynchronous arithmetic intensity" metric to characterize bottlenecks is a worthwhile contribution. The online calibration technique is also novel, addressing the limitation of traditional conformal prediction in test-time settings where held-out calibration data is unavailable. The emphasis on a statistically guaranteed adaptive inference framework based on conformal prediction distinguishes the work from prior efforts in speculative decoding or parallel sampling, which often lack such guarantees and can suffer from issues such as uncalibrated confidence.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to substantially improve the practicality of test-time scaling for LLMs, particularly for complex reasoning tasks. The reported speedup and throughput improvements are substantial and could make advanced reasoning capabilities more accessible in real-world applications. The framework's ability to reduce latency and memory overhead also addresses important limitations of current scaling methods. The statistical guarantee provided by conformal prediction approach is very useful in the context of high stakes reasoning tasks.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of A1 across multiple datasets and model families. The comparisons against baseline methods (especially those focusing on speculative decoding) highlight the advantages of the A1 approach. The use of multiple reasoning benchmarks strengthens the argument that A1 is particularly well-suited for tasks demanding intricate reasoning steps.
    * **Principled Approach:** The statistical backing through conformal prediction provides a solid theoretical foundation for the framework. It ensures that scaling decisions are made in a principled way, and reject rate can be accurately controlled during online operation.
    * **Code Release:** The availability of the code enhances reproducibility and allows others to build upon the work.
    * **Well-Defined Metric:** The asynchronous arithmetic intensity is a meaningful measure for understanding performance bottlenecks in this specific scenario.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** While the paper is well-written, the overall framework is quite complex, involving multiple interacting components.  This might make it more challenging to implement and optimize in practice. The description can be made more easily accessible.
    * **Limited Generalizability Evidence:**  The evaluation focuses primarily on a specific class of reasoning tasks (mathematical problem solving). While these tasks are important, it would be beneficial to demonstrate the effectiveness of A1 on a wider range of LLM applications, such as natural language understanding or code generation. The paper makes the implicit claim that the asynchronous sampling is the core advancement, while also pointing out that it works best when the target is a reasoning model. The paper needs to spend more time justifying what properties of "reasoning" are leading to the improvements, so that the work can be generalized to settings beyond mathematical reasoning.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper doesn't thoroughly explore the sensitivity of A1's performance to various hyperparameters (e.g., the miscoverage rate, token budget). A more detailed analysis would be valuable for practitioners who want to apply A1 in different settings.
    * **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:** While the paper reports strong average performance, it doesn't delve deeply into specific failure cases where A1 might struggle. Discussing such limitations would provide a more balanced and informative assessment of the framework's strengths and weaknesses.

* **Potential Influence:** A1 has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM inference. Its efficient and principled approach to test-time scaling could become a standard technique, particularly for applications where high accuracy and low latency are critical. The techniques introduced in A1, such as the asynchronous arithmetic intensity metric and the online calibration method, could also inspire further research in efficient LLM inference.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a significant and novel contribution to the field of efficient LLM inference. The A1 framework addresses a critical challenge of test-time scaling (managing synchronization overhead).  The introduction of techniques such as asynchronous arithmetic intensity and online calibration based on conformal prediction is novel and appears effective. The experimental results are convincing, showcasing substantial performance improvements compared to existing methods. The paper is also well-structured and clearly written, enhancing its accessibility. While the framework is complex, and the evaluation is somewhat limited in scope, the potential impact of A1 on the field warrants a score of 8. The paper introduces truly novel ideas that address a practical challenge, which will undoubtedly push the entire field forward.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Asymptotic Study of In-context Learning with Random Transformers through Equivalent Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15152v1)
- **Authors**: Samet Demir, Zafer Dogan
- **Abstract**: We study the in-context learning (ICL) capabilities of pretrained Transformers in the setting of nonlinear regression. Specifically, we focus on a random Transformer with a nonlinear MLP head where the first layer is randomly initialized and fixed while the second layer is trained. Furthermore, we consider an asymptotic regime where the context length, input dimension, hidden dimension, number of training tasks, and number of training samples jointly grow. In this setting, we show that the random Transformer behaves equivalent to a finite-degree Hermite polynomial model in terms of ICL error. This equivalence is validated through simulations across varying activation functions, context lengths, hidden layer widths (revealing a double-descent phenomenon), and regularization settings. Our results offer theoretical and empirical insights into when and how MLP layers enhance ICL, and how nonlinearity and over-parameterization influence model performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper analyzes the in-context learning (ICL) capabilities of a random Transformer model with a nonlinear MLP head for nonlinear regression tasks. The key idea is to show that in a high-dimensional asymptotic regime, this Transformer is equivalent to a finite-degree Hermite polynomial model. This equivalence is validated through simulations across different activation functions, context lengths, hidden layer widths, and regularization settings. The paper identifies conditions under which nonlinear MLPs improve ICL performance and shows that the ICL error of such Transformers can exhibit a double-descent phenomenon.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in bridging the gap between the theoretical understanding of ICL in Transformers and the asymptotic theory of MLPs. Specifically, it analytically characterizes the impact of a nonlinear MLP head in a *standard* Transformer architecture (i.e., MLPs *after* attention) for ICL on regression tasks, which distinguishes it from prior works that focus on classification, ReLU-only MLPs, or non-standard architectures. Establishing the equivalence to a Hermite polynomial model provides a tractable way to analyze the otherwise complex Transformer. This allows for analyzing the conditions where the MLP truly improves ICL performance.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance comes from providing theoretical insights into the role of nonlinearity and over-parameterization in Transformer-based ICL. It offers concrete guidance for designing Transformer architectures with MLP layers to enhance ICL performance in regression tasks. The double-descent phenomenon is an important observation with implications for model selection and regularization. The equivalence allows us to use known tools from Random Feature Models, which adds value to this kind of analysis.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of asymptotic analysis allows for a rigorous mathematical treatment of the problem.
    *   The derivation of the equivalent Hermite polynomial model is a crucial step in making the analysis tractable.
    *   The simulations provide empirical validation of the theoretical results, demonstrating the equivalence between the Transformer and the polynomial model.
    *   The paper identifies practical conditions for when MLPs enhance ICL (specific activations, context length, etc.).
    *   The demonstration of the double-descent phenomenon provides insights into the importance of model complexity and regularization.
    *   The paper acknowledges prior work and precisely defines the gap it aims to address.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The analysis is restricted to a specific architecture (random Transformer with linear attention and a single MLP head). While this simplification is necessary for tractability, it limits the generalizability of the results to more complex Transformer architectures.
    *   The asymptotic regime may not perfectly reflect the behavior of Transformers in practical, non-asymptotic settings.
    *   While the theoretical derivations are solid, the paper may benefit from more detailed discussion and comparisons regarding the assumptions and limitations of their findings compared to similar analyses of Random Feature Models.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of Transformer architectures for ICL, especially in regression tasks. The insights into the role of nonlinearity, context length, and regularization can guide practitioners in choosing appropriate architectures and hyperparameters. The mathematical tools developed in the paper could also be applied to analyze other aspects of Transformer behavior.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the balance of novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign the paper a score of 7.5. This reflects that the paper makes a substantial contribution to the theoretical understanding of Transformer-based ICL, providing analytical results and empirical validation. The derivation of the equivalent Hermite polynomial model is a key strength that allows for tractable analysis. It's not a higher score as the model and assumptions are quite specific, therefore a strong argument can be made about generalizability. While solid, the reliance on asymptotic results also limits the practical implications somewhat. There is a good amount of effort and the impact on a specialized part of the field should be tangible.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: stat.ML
- **Score**: 7/10

### AnoF-Diff: One-Step Diffusion-Based Anomaly Detection for Forceful Tool Use
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15153v1)
- **Authors**: Yating Lin, Zixuan Huang, Fan Yang, Dmitry Berenson
- **Abstract**: Multivariate time-series anomaly detection, which is critical for identifying unexpected events, has been explored in the field of machine learning for several decades. However, directly applying these methods to data from forceful tool use tasks is challenging because streaming sensor data in the real world tends to be inherently noisy, exhibits non-stationary behavior, and varies across different tasks and tools. To address these challenges, we propose a method, AnoF-Diff, based on the diffusion model to extract force-torque features from time-series data and use force-torque features to detect anomalies. We compare our method with other state-of-the-art methods in terms of F1-score and Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (AUROC) on four forceful tool-use tasks, demonstrating that our method has better performance and is more robust to a noisy dataset. We also propose the method of parallel anomaly score evaluation based on one-step diffusion and demonstrate how our method can be used for online anomaly detection in several forceful tool use experiments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "AnoF-Diff: One-Step Diffusion-Based Anomaly Detection for Forceful Tool Use":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AnoF-Diff, a novel anomaly detection method tailored for forceful tool use tasks in robotics. The method leverages diffusion models to denoise state-dependent features, specifically force-torque data, extracted from multivariate time series.  It employs a conditional masking strategy within the diffusion process, preserving robot state and task conditions while focusing the denoising on force-torque components. A parallel anomaly score evaluation method, based on one-step diffusion denoising, is proposed for efficient real-time performance.  The method is evaluated on real-world forceful tool-use tasks (placement, prying, socket mating, and nut tightening) and compared against various baseline methods, demonstrating improved performance and robustness, especially in noisy environments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects:

    *   The application of diffusion models to anomaly detection in robotic tool use tasks, particularly focusing on force-torque data, is relatively novel. While diffusion models have been used for anomaly detection in time series data before, tailoring them to robotic manipulation and incorporating state-dependent conditioning is a distinct contribution.
    *   The conditional masking strategy within the diffusion model, which selectively denoises force-torque components while preserving robot state information, is a valuable innovation. This allows the model to learn the conditional probability distribution of force-torque given the robot's state, leading to more informed anomaly detection.
    *   The parallel anomaly score evaluation method, based on one-step diffusion denoising, offers a significant improvement in inference speed, enabling real-time anomaly monitoring. This is crucial for practical robotic applications.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The ability to detect anomalies in real-time during robotic tool use tasks has significant implications for safety, reliability, and task completion. Early detection of anomalies can prevent damage to the robot, the environment, or the objects being manipulated. It can also trigger corrective actions to ensure successful task completion.
    *   The paper's focus on force-torque data is particularly relevant for forceful tool-use tasks, where subtle deviations from expected force profiles can indicate problems.
    *   The demonstrated performance improvements over existing baseline methods, especially in noisy environments, highlight the practical value of the proposed method. The experiments provide sufficient evidence to support the claims of the paper.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, including comparisons against multiple baseline methods and real-world robot experiments.
    *   The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of the different components of the proposed method.
    *   The introduction of the parallel anomaly score evaluation method shows an understanding of practical implementation challenges and addresses the need for real-time performance.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The use of a fixed-length sliding window limits the ability to handle variable-length time series signals and potentially loses long-term dependency information. Addressing this limitation would further enhance the practical applicability of the method.
    *   The training data is gathered from relatively simple task-specific controllers. Testing the method with more complex control policies would provide a more rigorous evaluation of its robustness.

*   **Impact:**
    The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
        1.  Providing a new approach for anomaly detection in robotic tool use tasks that leverages the power of diffusion models.
        2.  Introducing a conditional masking strategy that allows for the integration of state-dependent information into the anomaly detection process.
        3.  Addressing the need for real-time performance through the proposed parallel anomaly score evaluation method.
        4.  Demonstrating the practical value of the method through real-world robot experiments.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated anomaly detection method that is tailored to robotic tool use tasks. The conditional masking strategy, and the parallel anomaly score evaluation method represent significant contributions. The results demonstrate solid performance improvements over existing baselines in real-world experiments, and the speed improvements from parallelization are significant. However, the limitations with sliding windows and simple controllers keep it from being a top-tier publication. Therefore, I assign a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Self-Improving Embodied Foundation Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15155v1)
- **Authors**: Seyed Kamyar Seyed Ghasemipour, Ayzaan Wahid, Jonathan Tompson, Pannag Sanketi, Igor Mordatch
- **Abstract**: Foundation models trained on web-scale data have revolutionized robotics, but their application to low-level control remains largely limited to behavioral cloning. Drawing inspiration from the success of the reinforcement learning stage in fine-tuning large language models, we propose a two-stage post-training approach for robotics. The first stage, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), fine-tunes pretrained foundation models using both: a) behavioral cloning, and b) steps-to-go prediction objectives. In the second stage, Self-Improvement, steps-to-go prediction enables the extraction of a well-shaped reward function and a robust success detector, enabling a fleet of robots to autonomously practice downstream tasks with minimal human supervision. Through extensive experiments on real-world and simulated robot embodiments, our novel post-training recipe unveils significant results on Embodied Foundation Models. First, we demonstrate that the combination of SFT and Self-Improvement is significantly more sample-efficient than scaling imitation data collection for supervised learning, and that it leads to policies with significantly higher success rates. Further ablations highlight that the combination of web-scale pretraining and Self-Improvement is the key to this sample-efficiency. Next, we demonstrate that our proposed combination uniquely unlocks a capability that current methods cannot achieve: autonomously practicing and acquiring novel skills that generalize far beyond the behaviors observed in the imitation learning datasets used during training. These findings highlight the transformative potential of combining pretrained foundation models with online Self-Improvement to enable autonomous skill acquisition in robotics. Our project website can be found at https://self-improving-efms.github.io .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a two-stage post-training approach for Embodied Foundation Models (EFMs) to improve their low-level control capabilities in robotics.  The first stage, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), refines pre-trained foundation models using both behavioral cloning and a novel "steps-to-go" prediction objective. The second stage, Self-Improvement, leverages the steps-to-go prediction to extract a reward function and success detector, allowing a fleet of robots to autonomously practice downstream tasks with minimal human supervision. Experiments on real-world and simulated robot embodiments demonstrate that this combination is more sample-efficient and leads to higher success rates than scaling imitation data alone. Furthermore, it unlocks the ability for robots to autonomously practice and acquire new skills beyond the behaviors seen in the imitation dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in its integration of web-scale pretraining with a two-stage post-training process inspired by Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically applying RL fine-tuning (Self-Improvement) to embodied robotics. The steps-to-go prediction objective and its use as a data-driven reward signal for online RL is also novel. While individual components (behavioral cloning, RL fine-tuning, foundation models for robotics) have been explored before, their specific combination and the reported performance gains, especially in terms of sample efficiency and out-of-distribution generalization, constitute a significant contribution. The concept of leveraging steps-to-go prediction to autonomously acquire new skills is a strong point.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to address the limitations of purely behavioral cloning-based approaches for EFMs.  By incorporating online RL using self-predicted rewards, the authors demonstrate a pathway towards more autonomous skill acquisition in robotics, reducing the reliance on large, curated imitation datasets and extensive manual reward engineering. The demonstrated sample efficiency is a particularly important factor, as data collection in robotics is often a bottleneck. If the results hold up to more scrutiny and wider adoption, this approach could accelerate the development of more robust and generalizable robot policies. The experiments, while conducted on specific robot platforms and tasks, provide compelling evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The integration of SFT and Self-Improvement is well-motivated by the success of similar methods in LLMs.
    *   The steps-to-go prediction and self-predicted reward function are clever and effective.
    *   The experiments demonstrate significant improvements in sample efficiency and success rates.
    *   The experiments on domain transfer (Sim2Real) and learning novel skills (BananaTable) are particularly compelling.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on a large, pre-trained foundation model (PaLI) might limit the accessibility of the approach for some researchers. However, the use of a popular and relatively accessible model mitigates this.
    *   The paper acknowledges potential degradation of success rates if the Self-Improvement process is pushed beyond its optimal point and suggests the need for better stopping criteria and regularization. This represents an area where further research is warranted.
    *   The experiments, while extensive, are still limited to a few specific robot platforms and tasks. More diverse and complex environments would strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
    * It's important to note that the paper uses simulation and RL for post training tasks and it's not completely clear what is the role of pre-training. Does it add that much or it's just helpful to have a good initialization.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the direction of research on EFMs by highlighting the benefits of incorporating online RL fine-tuning. The steps-to-go prediction and self-rewarding approach could become a standard technique in the field.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:** I would argue for an 7.5 score. The technical contribution is solid, the experimental results are compelling, and the potential impact on the field is significant, but the reliance on large foundation models, the limited diversity of robot platforms used, and the preliminary nature of the out-of-distribution generalization results prevent a higher score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mind the Gap: Data Rewriting for Stable Off-Policy Supervised Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15157v1)
- **Authors**: Shiwan Zhao, Xuyang Zhao, Jiaming Zhou, Aobo Kong, Qicheng Li, Yong Qin
- **Abstract**: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) of large language models can be viewed as an off-policy learning problem, where expert demonstrations come from a fixed behavior policy while training aims to optimize a target policy. Importance sampling is the standard tool for correcting this distribution mismatch, but large policy gaps lead to high variance and training instability. Existing approaches mitigate this issue using KL penalties or clipping, which passively constrain updates rather than actively reducing the gap. We propose a simple yet effective data rewriting framework that proactively shrinks the policy gap by keeping correct solutions as on-policy data and rewriting incorrect ones with guided re-solving, falling back to expert demonstrations only when needed. This aligns the training distribution with the target policy before optimization, reducing importance sampling variance and stabilizing off-policy fine-tuning. Experiments on five mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent and significant gains over both vanilla SFT and the state-of-the-art Dynamic Fine-Tuning (DFT) approach. The data and code will be released at https://github.com/NKU-HLT/Off-Policy-SFT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Mind the Gap: Data Rewriting for Stable Off-Policy Supervised Fine-Tuning":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the instability of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) for large language models (LLMs), framing it as an off-policy learning problem. It argues that the discrepancy between the distribution of expert demonstrations (the behavior policy) and the model's evolving policy (the target policy) leads to instability during training due to high variance in importance sampling. The authors propose a data rewriting framework to proactively reduce this "policy gap." Their method samples responses from the model, keeps correct answers, and rewrites incorrect answers by prompting the model to re-solve the problem using a "digest-and-retell" strategy with the ground-truth solution as a reference. If this fails, they fall back to the original expert demonstration. This rewritten dataset, intended to be more aligned with the target policy, is then used for SFT with importance sampling. Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements over vanilla SFT and Dynamic Fine-Tuning (DFT).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of proactively rewriting the dataset to reduce the policy gap in SFT is relatively novel. While data curation and transformation have been explored in SFT, framing the process explicitly within an off-policy learning paradigm and then designing a specific data rewriting strategy to *reduce* the policy gap before training is a key contribution. The digest-and-retell strategy, borrowing from educational principles, also adds a level of sophistication to the rewriting process.

*   **Significance:** Stabilizing SFT training is significant, especially given SFT's wide use in LLM development. The demonstrated improvements on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, particularly over the DFT baseline, suggest that the approach is effective in practice. By reducing the policy gap, the method offers a potentially more robust and efficient way to fine-tune LLMs, which could have practical implications for model development.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** Framing SFT as an off-policy problem is well-articulated.
    *   **Effective Data Rewriting:** The Digest-and-Retell approach seems practically effective as shown by the results.
    *   **Solid Empirical Evaluation:** Performance on multiple benchmarks strengthens the findings. The comparison to DFT, a state-of-the-art method, is particularly relevant.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study justifies the combination of Self-alignment and Guided-alignment.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is primarily restricted to mathematical reasoning tasks. It's unclear if the rewriting strategy would generalize equally well to other types of tasks or datasets (e.g., creative writing, dialogue generation).
    *   **Data Rewriting Cost:** The process of generating multiple responses and re-solving problems can be computationally expensive, potentially limiting the scalability of the approach, especially for large datasets.
    *   **Simplified Importance Sampling:** The importance sampling uses a simplified approximation by setting the denominator to 1. While common, this approximation may limit the effectiveness of importance sampling.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence research in a few ways:

    *   **Data-Centric Approaches:** It reinforces the importance of data curation and transformation for SFT.
    *   **Off-Policy Learning in SFT:** It may encourage researchers to view SFT through the lens of off-policy learning, leading to new methods for stabilization and optimization.
    *   **Digest-and-Retell Inspiration:** It could inspire new data rewriting techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**. The paper makes a novel and significant contribution by addressing the instability of SFT through a proactive data rewriting strategy framed within an off-policy learning context. The empirical results are compelling, and the method shows promise. However, the limited scope of the evaluation (primarily mathematical reasoning) and the potential computational costs associated with data rewriting limit the paper's broader impact. Further research is needed to assess the generalizability and scalability of the approach.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### AIP: Subverting Retrieval-Augmented Generation via Adversarial Instructional Prompt
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15159v1)
- **Authors**: Saket S. Chaturvedi, Gaurav Bagwe, Lan Zhang, Xiaoyong Yuan
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances large language models (LLMs) by retrieving relevant documents from external sources to improve factual accuracy and verifiability. However, this reliance introduces new attack surfaces within the retrieval pipeline, beyond the LLM itself. While prior RAG attacks have exposed such vulnerabilities, they largely rely on manipulating user queries, which is often infeasible in practice due to fixed or protected user inputs. This narrow focus overlooks a more realistic and stealthy vector: instructional prompts, which are widely reused, publicly shared, and rarely audited. Their implicit trust makes them a compelling target for adversaries to manipulate RAG behavior covertly. We introduce a novel attack for Adversarial Instructional Prompt (AIP) that exploits adversarial instructional prompts to manipulate RAG outputs by subtly altering retrieval behavior. By shifting the attack surface to the instructional prompts, AIP reveals how trusted yet seemingly benign interface components can be weaponized to degrade system integrity. The attack is crafted to achieve three goals: (1) naturalness, to evade user detection; (2) utility, to encourage use of prompts; and (3) robustness, to remain effective across diverse query variations. We propose a diverse query generation strategy that simulates realistic linguistic variation in user queries, enabling the discovery of prompts that generalize across paraphrases and rephrasings. Building on this, a genetic algorithm-based joint optimization is developed to evolve adversarial prompts by balancing attack success, clean-task utility, and stealthiness. Experimental results show that AIP achieves up to 95.23% ASR while preserving benign functionality. These findings uncover a critical and previously overlooked vulnerability in RAG systems, emphasizing the need to reassess the shared instructional prompts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel attack, "AIP" (Adversarial Instructional Prompt), against Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems.  Unlike prior attacks that focus on manipulating user queries or directly poisoning the knowledge base, AIP exploits adversarial instructional prompts, which are commonly used, shared, and often trusted components of RAG interfaces.  These prompts, designed to guide the RAG system's behavior, are subtly crafted to steer the system towards adversarial outputs when a user query contains a target concept, while maintaining utility and naturalness for benign queries. The attack framework involves three stages: (1) prompt and document initialization using LLMs to identify semantic triggers, (2) diverse query generation to ensure robustness across different query variations, and (3) adversarial joint optimization using a genetic algorithm to refine the prompt and associated documents.  Experimental results show that AIP can achieve high attack success rates while preserving clean-task functionality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in shifting the attack surface from user queries (as in previous work) to instructional prompts.  This is a significant departure, as instructional prompts are often widely shared, reused, and implicitly trusted, making them a compelling and stealthy attack vector. The approach of jointly optimizing prompts and documents to trigger biased retrieval behaviour using a genetic algorithm is also reasonably novel in the context of RAG attacks.

*   **Significance:** The findings have considerable practical significance. The paper highlights a previously overlooked vulnerability in RAG systems that could be exploited in real-world applications to promote misinformation, bias, or malicious content.  The attack does not require access to model internals or the ability to modify user queries, making it highly practical and applicable in black-box settings. The high attack success rates achieved in the experiments underscore the severity of the threat. The study raises serious concerns about the security of prompt-driven systems and emphasizes the need for prompt-level auditing and retrieval-aware defenses.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem and motivates the need for a new attack vector.
    *   The AIP framework is well-defined and the three stages are logically connected.
    *   The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating the effectiveness of the attack and its superiority over existing baselines.
    *   The analysis of the failure cases and the discussion of potential defense strategies adds to the paper's overall value.
    *   The method is practical and doesn't require unrealistic assumptions about attacker access.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper shows that AIP is effective, it does not propose any concrete defenses.
    *   The reliance on LLM-based judgments for evaluating naturalness is a potential limitation. Human evaluations, though more time-consuming, would provide a more robust assessment of the stealthiness of the adversarial prompts.
    *   The evaluation is limited to three knowledge bases. More extensive testing across diverse datasets and real-world applications would strengthen the findings.
    *   The work assumes the attacker has the ability to inject malicious documents into the knowledge base. In some real-world scenarios, this may not be feasible.
    *   The paper does not explore in detail the impact of prompt engineering and how it impacts ASR, leaving a key factor relatively under-explored.

*   **Impact:** This work is likely to have a significant impact on the RAG security community. It identifies a crucial vulnerability that could inspire further research into prompt-based attacks and defenses. It could also influence the design of more secure and robust RAG systems. It calls for a fundamental shift in how instructional prompts are perceived and managed, encouraging more rigorous auditing and validation procedures.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The paper makes a substantial contribution to the field by identifying and exploiting a previously unaddressed vulnerability in RAG systems. While there are some weaknesses in evaluation such as the reliance of LLMs for evaluating naturalness, the attack itself is well-designed, practical, and effective. The approach opens up new avenues for research in RAG security and has the potential to influence the development of more robust and secure RAG systems. This paper provides a critical assessment and highlights vulnerabilities of RAG systems and does so with strong empirical evidence.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### An Evaluation-Centric Paradigm for Scientific Visualization Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15160v1)
- **Authors**: Kuangshi Ai, Haichao Miao, Zhimin Li, Chaoli Wang, Shusen Liu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) have enabled increasingly sophisticated autonomous visualization agents capable of translating user intentions into data visualizations. However, measuring progress and comparing different agents remains challenging, particularly in scientific visualization (SciVis), due to the absence of comprehensive, large-scale benchmarks for evaluating real-world capabilities. This position paper examines the various types of evaluation required for SciVis agents, outlines the associated challenges, provides a simple proof-of-concept evaluation example, and discusses how evaluation benchmarks can facilitate agent self-improvement. We advocate for a broader collaboration to develop a SciVis agentic evaluation benchmark that would not only assess existing capabilities but also drive innovation and stimulate future development in the field.
- **Summary**: The paper "An Evaluation-Centric Paradigm for Scientific Visualization Agents" argues for a shift in the development of scientific visualization (SciVis) agents, emphasizing that evaluation should drive design, rather than being a post-hoc validation. It highlights the current lack of comprehensive, large-scale benchmarks for evaluating real-world SciVis agent capabilities, which hinders progress and reliable deployment. The paper proposes a taxonomy for evaluating SciVis agents, dividing it into outcome-based and process-based evaluations, and discusses various dimensions such as task complexity, tool use, and agent proficiency. It also addresses the challenges of accurate and effective evaluation, particularly concerning the use of LLMs as judges, coverage of SciVis tasks, and cost-effectiveness. The paper presents an illustrative case study using the Bonsai dataset and ParaView to demonstrate its evaluation framework. Finally, it advocates for evaluation-driven agent design and emphasizes the importance of collaboration in building community benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its focused application of evaluation-centric principles to the specific domain of SciVis agents. While the general idea of evaluation-driven development is not new, its structured adaptation and detailed consideration for the unique challenges of SciVis are valuable. The taxonomy proposed, dividing evaluations into outcome-based and process-based with specific dimensions, offers a more systematic approach than previous ad-hoc evaluation methods. However, the individual evaluation techniques discussed are not entirely novel, mostly drawing from existing methods for LLM or AI agent evaluation.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it addresses a critical bottleneck in the development and adoption of SciVis agents. By highlighting the lack of robust benchmarks, it correctly identifies a major obstacle to measuring progress and comparing different agents effectively. The paper's detailed taxonomy and discussion of evaluation challenges provide a valuable roadmap for future research and development. It is also significant because scientific visualization is a growing and important area that will benefit greatly from more robust automated agents, with applications to science and industry. The call for community collaboration to build benchmarks addresses the scope of the work required to make the vision possible.

*   **Strengths:** The paper's strengths include:

    *   Clear articulation of the problem: The need for better evaluation frameworks in SciVis agent development is convincingly argued.
    *   Well-defined taxonomy: The outcome-based and process-based evaluation framework provides a structured approach.
    *   Practical focus: The Bonsai dataset example illustrates how the proposed framework can be applied.
    *   Emphasis on automation and reproducibility: Critical for scientific applications, and explicitly addressed.
    *   Strong Call for Community Collaboration: Recognizing the scale of the endeavor, the paper explicitly calls for collaborative effort.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited empirical validation: While the Bonsai example is helpful, the paper lacks a comprehensive empirical study demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed framework across a wider range of SciVis tasks and agents.
    *   Reliance on LLM judges: Although acknowledged, the paper's reliance on LLM judges raises concerns about accuracy and reliability, particularly in nuanced visual tasks. Mitigation using hard coded verification alleviates this to some extent, but there remains residual concerns.
    *   Lack of detail on Benchmark Implementation: The paper provides a theoretical framework but is lacking on practical aspects of implementing an evaluation benchmark, like a detailed evaluation of the feasibility of applying each technique in practice.
    *   Focus on Fully Autonomous Agents: By excluding human-AI collaboration scenarios, the paper neglects an important aspect of real-world SciVis applications, where users often interact with agents to refine visualizations. This limitation is clearly acknowledged, though.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

    *   Inspiring the development of comprehensive SciVis agent benchmarks.
    *   Guiding the design of more effective and reliable SciVis agents.
    *   Promoting collaboration among researchers, domain scientists, and tool developers.

**Overall Justification:**

The paper addresses an important issue in a growing field, proposes a reasonable framework for improvement, and articulates challenges and mitigation strategies. However, the lack of extensive empirical validation and reliance on potentially unreliable LLM judges detracts from the overall impact. Taking those points into consideration, the paper can be rated as a good effort.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Watermarking and Anomaly Detection in Machine Learning Models for LORA RF Fingerprinting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15170v1)
- **Authors**: Aarushi Mahajan, Wayne Burleson
- **Abstract**: Radio frequency fingerprint identification (RFFI) distinguishes wireless devices by the small variations in their analog circuits, avoiding heavy cryptographic authentication. While deep learning on spectrograms improves accuracy, models remain vulnerable to copying, tampering, and evasion. We present a stronger RFFI system combining watermarking for ownership proof and anomaly detection for spotting suspicious inputs. Using a ResNet-34 on log-Mel spectrograms, we embed three watermarks: a simple trigger, an adversarially trained trigger robust to noise and filtering, and a hidden gradient/weight signature. A convolutional Variational Autoencoders (VAE) with Kullback-Leibler (KL) warm-up and free-bits flags off-distribution queries. On the LoRa dataset, our system achieves 94.6% accuracy, 98% watermark success, and 0.94 AUROC, offering verifiable, tamper-resistant authentication.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a more robust Radio Frequency Fingerprint Identification (RFFI) system. It combines deep learning on spectrograms with watermarking for ownership proof and anomaly detection to identify suspicious inputs. The system uses a ResNet-34 model on log-Mel spectrograms, embedding three watermarks: a trigger, an adversarially trained trigger, and a hidden gradient/weight signature. A convolutional VAE is included as a guard, flagging out-of-distribution queries using Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence warm-up and free-bits regularization. The results demonstrate high accuracy, watermark success, and anomaly detection AUROC on the LoRa dataset, suggesting verifiable, tamper-resistant authentication.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:* The paper's novelty lies in the *combination* of existing techniques (RFFI via deep learning, watermarking, and anomaly detection using autoencoders) into a more secure and reliable RFFI system.  While each individual component is not necessarily groundbreaking, the integrated approach is a significant contribution. Prior work addressed RFFI accuracy and adversarial attacks separately; this paper attempts to combine defenses. The idea of using a VAE as a "guard" against adversarial examples is a notable contribution within the context of RFFI, although VAEs have been used for anomaly detection in other domains. The use of three different types of watermarks with varying strengths (black-box trigger, adversarial robustness, pruning resilience) adds a valuable layer of defense-in-depth.

*Significance:*  The paper addresses a crucial gap in the RFFI field: the lack of comprehensive security measures against model theft, tampering, and adversarial input. RFFI's reliance on subtle device characteristics makes it inherently vulnerable to these attacks. The proposed system attempts to make RFFI practically deployable by adding defenses that were mostly lacking in prior research.

*Strengths:*
    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** The combination of watermarking and anomaly detection is a strength, providing multiple layers of security.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper tackles a real-world problem in RFFI security, enhancing its usability.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The results on the LoRa dataset show promising performance for classification accuracy, watermark verification, and anomaly detection.
    *   **Defense-in-Depth:** Addresses different attack vectors (model theft, tampering, input evasion) with different protection mechanisms.
    *   **Thorough evaluation:** Evaluation considers attacks through pruning, quantization and adversarial trigger inputs.

*Weaknesses:*
    *   **Incremental Improvement:**  The individual components are not particularly innovative. While the integration is valuable, the improvement might be considered incremental rather than revolutionary.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** The LoRa dataset is common in RFFI research, but the effectiveness on other wireless technologies and datasets isn't explored. Further investigation into different datasets could broaden the work’s applicability.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:** The choice of parameters and thresholds within the autoencoder for anomaly detection requires careful tuning. The authors acknowledge this (FPR ≈ 5% on clean data) indicating a need for more robust methods for setting these parameters that would work across a diverse dataset.
    *   **Computational Overhead:** The introduction of VAE and watermarks add computational complexity.

*Potential Influence:* The paper's approach could influence future research in RFFI security. The integration of anomaly detection and watermarking can inspire more comprehensive solutions to protect RFFI systems. The use of adversarial training to improve the robustness of watermarks is a valuable insight that could be adopted in other contexts.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper makes a valuable contribution to RFFI security by integrating existing techniques into a more comprehensive and robust system. The experimental results are promising, and the paper addresses a real-world problem with practical relevance. While the novelty of individual components is limited, the *combination* and demonstration of the system's effectiveness justifies the score. The identified weaknesses, such as the incremental improvement and dataset dependence, are factors that prevent a higher rating.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### SMARTER: A Data-efficient Framework to Improve Toxicity Detection with Explanation via Self-augmenting Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15174v1)
- **Authors**: Huy Nghiem, Advik Sachdeva, Hal Daumé III
- **Abstract**: WARNING: This paper contains examples of offensive materials. Toxic content has become pervasive on social media platforms. We introduce SMARTER, a data-efficient two-stage framework for explainable content moderation using Large Language Models (LLMs). In Stage 1, we leverage LLMs' own outputs to generate synthetic explanations for both correct and incorrect labels, enabling alignment via preference optimization with minimal human supervision. In Stage 2, we refine explanation quality through cross-model training, allowing weaker models to align stylistically and semantically with stronger ones. Experiments on three benchmark tasks -- HateXplain, Latent Hate, and Implicit Hate -- demonstrate that SMARTER enables LLMs to achieve up to a 13.5% macro-F1 improvement over standard few-shot baselines while using only a fraction of the full training data. Our framework offers a scalable strategy for low-resource settings by harnessing LLMs' self-improving capabilities for both classification and explanation.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SMARTER, a data-efficient two-stage framework to improve toxicity detection using Large Language Models (LLMs). Stage 1 focuses on self-augmentation, where an LLM generates synthetic explanations for both correct and incorrect labels, enabling alignment via preference optimization. Stage 2 refines explanation quality by cross-model training, allowing weaker models to align stylistically and semantically with stronger ones.  Experiments on three benchmark datasets (HateXplain, Latent Hate, Implicit Hate) demonstrate improved macro-F1 scores compared to few-shot baselines with limited training data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Data Efficiency:**  A key strength is the focus on data efficiency, which is highly relevant for low-resource scenarios. The self-augmentation technique reduces the reliance on large, expensive, and time-consuming human-annotated datasets.
    *   **Explainability:** SMARTER addresses the critical need for explainable AI in content moderation.  By generating explanations, the framework enhances transparency and trustworthiness, which are vital in sensitive domains like toxicity detection.
    *   **Two-Stage Approach:** The separation into self-augmentation and cross-model refinement is a well-structured approach. It allows independent optimization and leverages the synergy between different LLMs.
    *   **Rigorous Experiments:** The evaluation is conducted on three benchmark datasets, providing a good baseline for comparison. The comparison between different alignment techniques (DPO vs. KTO) and sub-sampling strategies is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Human Evaluation:** Human evaluation of explanations is only conducted on one dataset (HateXplain) due to resource constraints.  This limits the generalizability of the findings related to explanation quality. The human evaluation relies on a small number of crowdworkers, which could introduce bias.
    *   **Model Selection:** While the choice of Llama and T5 are reasonable, the experiments could have benefitted from exploring a wider range of LLMs or architectures. The impact of architecture on the explainability performance differences observed is not fully explored.
    *   **English-Centric:** The study focuses exclusively on English, which is a limitation given the global nature of social media and the need for multilingual content moderation.
    *   **Generalization concerns:** The experiments are conducted in-domain.  The paper would be strengthened by showing how well the refined models generalize out-of-domain. The risk of models overfitting to the benchmark datasets exists.
    *   **Reliance on Initial Seed Explanations:** The framework relies on initial seed explanations, which could introduce bias or limit the diversity of the generated explanations.
    *   **Lack of Ablation on Stage 2:** A thorough ablation of the benefits of Stage 2 would better show the effect of the cross-model refinement.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The combination of self-augmentation with preference optimization for toxicity detection is a novel contribution.
    *   The cross-model refinement approach offers a unique way to improve explanation quality by leveraging the strengths of different models.
    *   The data efficiency focus makes it highly relevant for practitioners in low-resource settings.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The framework could inspire further research on data-efficient and explainable AI for content moderation.
    *   The self-augmentation technique could be adapted to other NLP tasks and domains.
    *   The cross-model refinement approach could be explored as a general technique for improving the quality of LLM-generated content.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper addresses a significant problem and proposes a novel framework, the limitations related to human evaluation, model selection, and lack of generalization limit its overall impact. The empirical evaluation is well-executed but the lack of out-of-domain experiments is a significant drawback. More detailed ablation experiments would better show the effectiveness of all aspects of the pipeline, and further investigation into different types of LLMs and architectures would be useful. The focus on data efficiency and explainability is a positive contribution, but the paper could be significantly strengthened by addressing these concerns.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Unleashing the Potential of Multimodal LLMs for Zero-Shot Spatio-Temporal Video Grounding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15178v1)
- **Authors**: Zaiquan Yang, Yuhao Liu, Gerhard Hancke, Rynson W. H. Lau
- **Abstract**: Spatio-temporal video grounding (STVG) aims at localizing the spatio-temporal tube of a video, as specified by the input text query. In this paper, we utilize multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to explore a zero-shot solution in STVG. We reveal two key insights about MLLMs: (1) MLLMs tend to dynamically assign special tokens, referred to as \textit{grounding tokens}, for grounding the text query; and (2) MLLMs often suffer from suboptimal grounding due to the inability to fully integrate the cues in the text query (\textit{e.g.}, attributes, actions) for inference. Based on these insights, we propose a MLLM-based zero-shot framework for STVG, which includes novel decomposed spatio-temporal highlighting (DSTH) and temporal-augmented assembling (TAS) strategies to unleash the reasoning ability of MLLMs. The DSTH strategy first decouples the original query into attribute and action sub-queries for inquiring the existence of the target both spatially and temporally. It then uses a novel logit-guided re-attention (LRA) module to learn latent variables as spatial and temporal prompts, by regularizing token predictions for each sub-query. These prompts highlight attribute and action cues, respectively, directing the model's attention to reliable spatial and temporal related visual regions. In addition, as the spatial grounding by the attribute sub-query should be temporally consistent, we introduce the TAS strategy to assemble the predictions using the original video frames and the temporal-augmented frames as inputs to help improve temporal consistency. We evaluate our method on various MLLMs, and show that it outperforms SOTA methods on three common STVG benchmarks. The code will be available at https://github.com/zaiquanyang/LLaVA_Next_STVG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Unleashing the Potential of Multimodal LLMs for Zero-Shot Spatio-Temporal Video Grounding":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of spatio-temporal video grounding (STVG) in a zero-shot setting using multimodal large language models (MLLMs). The authors identify that MLLMs, while capable of grounding, often struggle with complex STVG due to the failure to fully integrate cues like attributes and actions within a query. They propose a framework that includes:

1.  **Decomposed Spatio-Temporal Highlighting (DSTH):** Decomposes the query into attribute and action sub-queries to highlight different aspects of the target. A Logit-guided Re-Attention (LRA) module learns latent variables as prompts to emphasize spatial and temporal cues, directing model attention to related visual regions.
2.  **Temporal-Augmented Assembling (TAS):** Uses temporally perturbed frames to improve the consistency of spatial grounding derived from the attribute sub-query.

The method is evaluated across several MLLMs and demonstrates superior performance compared to existing methods on common STVG benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the application of MLLMs to STVG in a truly zero-shot manner, without fine-tuning. The *DSTH* and *TAS* strategies are significant contributions. Decomposing the query and using LRA to learn visual prompts offers a fresh perspective on how to guide MLLMs for localization tasks. The analysis of special tokens, or "grounding tokens" is also a interesting contribution.
*   **Significance:** STVG is a crucial task with applications in areas like video surveillance and autonomous driving. A zero-shot solution is extremely valuable, as it eliminates the need for expensive, task-specific training data. By demonstrating that MLLMs can be effectively leveraged for STVG without fine-tuning, the paper paves the way for more generalizable and adaptable video understanding systems.  The approach directly addresses a critical limitation of current approaches by improving the integration of different types of information present in the query, rather than treating it as a monolithic piece of data.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition and Motivation:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of MLLMs in the context of STVG and provides compelling motivation for the proposed solution.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The DSTH and TAS strategies are well-explained and intuitively sound.  The LRA module is a clever way to learn visual prompts without explicit supervision.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The method is tested on multiple benchmarks and with various MLLMs, demonstrating the robustness of the approach. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The results show significant improvements over state-of-the-art methods, particularly in the zero-shot setting.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Existing Models:** The framework builds on top of existing MLLMs and pre-trained object detectors/trackers. While this allows for easy integration, the performance is inherently limited by the capabilities of these underlying models.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While not explicitly stated, using LRA to fine-tune prompts at test time introduces a computational overhead, which might limit the scalability of the approach, especially for real-time applications.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Long Videos:** The paper acknowledges the potential struggle with processing long videos and suggests that further research to address the high computational load is needed.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach for zero-shot STVG using MLLMs. The insights into grounding tokens, the DSTH strategy with LRA, and TAS all contribute to the significant improvements observed in the experiments. Although the method depends on existing components and has scalability limitations, its impact on the field is considerable.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of STVG by demonstrating how to effectively leverage MLLMs in a zero-shot setting. The proposed DSTH and TAS strategies are novel, well-motivated, and lead to substantial performance gains. While there are weaknesses related to the reliance on pre-trained models and computational cost, the overall impact and potential for future research are high. Thus, a score of 8 reflects the paper's strong contribution and the need for further work to address the identified limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Conditional Prior-based Non-stationary Channel Estimation Using Accelerated Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15182v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Ahmed Mohsin, Ahsan Bilal, Muhammad Umer, Asad Aali, Muhammad Ali Jamshed, Dean F. Hougen, John M. Cioffi
- **Abstract**: Wireless channels in motion-rich urban microcell (UMi) settings are non-stationary; mobility and scatterer dynamics shift the distribution over time, degrading classical and deep estimators. This work proposes conditional prior diffusion for channel estimation, which learns a history-conditioned score to denoise noisy channel snapshots. A temporal encoder with cross-time attention compresses a short observation window into a context vector, which captures the channel's instantaneous coherence and steers the denoiser via feature-wise modulation. In inference, an SNR-matched initialization selects the diffusion step whose marginal aligns with the measured input SNR, and the process follows a shortened, geometrically spaced schedule, preserving the signal-to-noise trajectory with far fewer iterations. Temporal self-conditioning with the previous channel estimate and a training-only smoothness penalty further stabilizes evolution without biasing the test-time estimator. Evaluations on a 3GPP benchmark show lower NMSE across all SNRs than LMMSE, GMM, LSTM, and LDAMP baselines, demonstrating stable performance and strong high SNR fidelity.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a conditional prior-based diffusion model for channel estimation in non-stationary wireless environments, specifically urban microcells (UMi). The core idea is to learn a history-conditioned prior from a causal window of channel observations using a temporal encoder based on ConvLSTM with cross-time attention. This prior is then used to guide the reverse diffusion process, aligning denoising updates with the channel's coherence length. The method also incorporates SNR-matched initialization and geometrically spaced sampling to accelerate inference. Temporal self-conditioning and a training-only smoothness penalty are employed to stabilize the channel estimate evolution. Experimental results on a 3GPP benchmark demonstrate improved NMSE compared to several baselines, particularly at high SNR.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of techniques to address the non-stationary channel estimation problem. While diffusion models and recurrent neural networks have been used in wireless communication before, the integration of a ConvLSTM-based temporal encoder with cross-time attention, SNR-matched initialization for accelerated inference, and temporal self-conditioning within a diffusion framework appears to be a unique contribution. The FiLM conditioning is a standard approach, but its use in this specific setting contributes to the effectiveness of the overall system. The geometrical scheduling is an interesting feature that would speed up the algorithm in practice.

*   **Significance:** Channel estimation is a fundamental problem in wireless communications, and addressing non-stationarity is crucial for improving performance in mobile environments. By demonstrating improved NMSE over existing methods, particularly at high SNR, the paper makes a potentially significant contribution to the field. The accelerated inference procedure makes the method more practical for real-time applications. The UMi benchmark is also a valid setting for the proposed method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem of non-stationary channel estimation and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   The proposed method is well-motivated and technically sound. The integration of several techniques is well explained.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach compared to several competitive baselines.
    *   The paper offers an efficient inference technique via geometrical schedules that has the potential to be used in real-world systems.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance increase is relatively small in absolute terms (-17.3 dB against a -15 dB baseline), albeit statistically significant. It remains to be seen how it would translate into end-to-end performance improvement of the communication system.
    *   The paper provides limited details about the implementation of the baselines, which could affect the fairness of the comparison. For instance, it is not clear whether the baselines have been optimized for the same channel conditions and hardware resources.
    *   While the inference is improved thanks to geometrical schedules, the overall computational cost of diffusion models could still be a limiting factor for real-time applications, specifically in high-dimensional channels.
    *   There is limited discussion on model sensitivity to different hyperparameter choices, such as window size (Tw), geometric spacing ratio (r), and smoothness penalty factor (lambda).

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by demonstrating the potential of conditional diffusion models for non-stationary channel estimation. The specific techniques used, such as the temporal encoder and SNR-matched initialization, could inspire new approaches to channel estimation and other signal processing problems in wireless communications.

*   **Overall Impression:** This paper makes a solid contribution to the field of wireless communications by addressing a relevant problem with a well-designed and implemented method. The experimental results are promising, and the paper is well-written and easy to follow. While the improvement over existing methods is not dramatically high and some additional experimental details would strengthen the paper further, the paper shows the potential for diffusion models in channel estimation and the accelerated inference makes it a relevant candidate for real-time applications.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel combination of techniques within a well-established framework (diffusion models) to tackle a crucial challenge (non-stationary channel estimation) in wireless communications. The demonstrated improvement and efficiency of the accelerated inference procedure merit a relatively high score. The weaknesses regarding the magnitude of improvements, baseline implementation details, and hyperparameter sensitivity prevent it from receiving a higher score. However, the paper offers a valuable contribution to the field and is likely to inspire further research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Understand Before You Generate: Self-Guided Training for Autoregressive Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15185v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaoyu Yue, Zidong Wang, Yuqing Wang, Wenlong Zhang, Xihui Liu, Wanli Ouyang, Lei Bai, Luping Zhou
- **Abstract**: Recent studies have demonstrated the importance of high-quality visual representations in image generation and have highlighted the limitations of generative models in image understanding. As a generative paradigm originally designed for natural language, autoregressive models face similar challenges. In this work, we present the first systematic investigation into the mechanisms of applying the next-token prediction paradigm to the visual domain. We identify three key properties that hinder the learning of high-level visual semantics: local and conditional dependence, inter-step semantic inconsistency, and spatial invariance deficiency. We show that these issues can be effectively addressed by introducing self-supervised objectives during training, leading to a novel training framework, Self-guided Training for AutoRegressive models (ST-AR). Without relying on pre-trained representation models, ST-AR significantly enhances the image understanding ability of autoregressive models and leads to improved generation quality. Specifically, ST-AR brings approximately 42% FID improvement for LlamaGen-L and 49% FID improvement for LlamaGen-XL, while maintaining the same sampling strategy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Understand Before You Generate: Self-Guided Training for Autoregressive Image Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses limitations in autoregressive models for image generation, specifically focusing on their image understanding capabilities.  The authors identify three key properties that hinder performance: local and conditional dependence, inter-step semantic inconsistency, and spatial invariance deficiency. To combat these, they propose a novel training framework called Self-guided Training for AutoRegressive models (ST-AR). ST-AR integrates self-supervised learning techniques, including masked image modeling (MIM) and contrastive learning, into the next-token prediction paradigm. MIM helps expand the receptive field, while contrastive learning encourages feature consistency across time steps and views.  The paper demonstrates significant improvements in both image understanding (measured by linear probing accuracy) and generation quality (measured by FID score) without relying on pre-trained representation models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of incorporating self-supervised objectives into the training of autoregressive image generation models is somewhat novel. Prior work has explored enhancing generative models with pre-trained representations or semantic regularization, but ST-AR presents a direct integration of SSL techniques *within* the AR training loop. The specific choice and implementation of MIM and contrastive losses tailored for the AR setting further contribute to the novelty. The identified properties are not completely new, however their systematization and targeted approach to address them are novel.

*   **Significance:**  The significance lies in improving the performance of autoregressive models, which are seen as a potential unified framework for various data modalities. The paper shows a substantial boost in performance on ImageNet, suggesting that ST-AR can make AR models more competitive with other generative approaches like GANs and diffusion models, especially when computational resources are limited. The potential of AR models to act as a unified framework provides importance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges faced by autoregressive models in image generation, providing a well-defined motivation for the proposed solution.
    *   **Thorough Analysis:** The analysis of attention maps and linear probing results provides valuable insights into the behavior of AR models.
    *   **Effective Solution:** The ST-AR framework demonstrates significant performance improvements across different model sizes.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The ablation studies provide a good understanding of the contribution of each component of ST-AR.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Evaluation:** The paper primarily focuses on ImageNet. Evaluating on other datasets (e.g., those with more complex scenes or objects) would strengthen the claims of improved image understanding and generalization.
    *   **Incremental Improvement over iBOT:** iBOT already combined MLM with contrastive learning. This paper is an adaptation to the autoregressive regime, rather than a fundamentally new combination.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper claims improved performance without relying on pre-trained representation models, it's important to acknowledge that adding MIM and contrastive learning increases the training cost compared to standard AR training.
